The surface category and tropical curves
Jan Steinebrunner

Abstract
We compute the classifying space of the surface category Cob 2 \mathrm{Cob}_{2} whose objects are closed 1 1 -manifolds and whose morphisms are diffeomorphism classes of surface bordisms, and show that it is rationally equivalent to a circle. It is hence much smaller than the classifying space of the topologically enriched surface category 𝒞 2 \mathcal{C}_{2} studied by Galatius-Madsen-Tillmann-Weiss. However, we also show that for the wide subcategory Cob 2 χ ≤ 0 ⊂ Cob 2 \mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2} that contains all morphisms without disks or spheres, the classifying space B ​ Cob 2 χ ≤ 0 B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}} is surprisingly large. Its rational homotopy groups contain the homology of all moduli spaces of tropical curves Δ g \Delta_{g} as a summand. The technical key result shows that a version of positive boundary surgery applies to a large class of discrete symmetric monoidal categories, which we call labelled cospan categories . We also use this to show that the ( 2 , 1 ) (2,1) -category of cospans of finite sets has a contractible classifying space.



1
Introduction

The main object of study of this paper is the surface category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
.
Objects in
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
are closed oriented
1
-manifolds and
morphisms are compact oriented surface cobordisms, up to boundary-preserving diffeomorphisms.
This category is best known as the
source of
2
-dimensional topological field theories.

Motivated by Segal’s work on conformal field theories
[
Seg04
]
,
Madsen and Tillmann
[
MT01
]
define a topological category
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
where the space of morphisms
M\to N
is the moduli space of surfaces with boundary
M^{-}\amalg N
.
This category has proven to be a useful tool for studying the moduli spaces
B\mathrm{Diff}(\Sigma_{g})
in the large genus limit
g\to\infty
.
In their seminal paper
[
GMTW09
]
Galatius, Madsen, Tillmann, and Weiss
compute the classifying space of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
as
B(\mathcal{C}_{2})\simeq\Omega^{\infty-1}\mathit{MTSO}_{2}
,
and deduce an alternative proof of the Mumford conjecture, which was originally proven in
[
MW07
]
.

In this paper we compute the classifying space
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})
and some closely related spaces.
We will refer to
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
as the
truncated
surface category in order to distinguish
it from the topological surface category
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
Indeed, the functor
\mathcal{C}_{2}\to\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
that sends a surface cobordism to its diffeomorphism class
identifies
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
as the homotopy-category
h\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
However, this functor is very far from being an equivalence:
it collapses each of the moduli spaces
B\mathrm{Diff}(\Sigma_{g,k})
to a point.
The category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

does not “know” about diffeomorphisms,
but only about the combinatorics of how surfaces can be glued.

The above might suggest that the classifying space of the truncated surface category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

is much simpler than its topological analogue
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
Indeed, our Theorem
B
implies that
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})
is rationally equivalent to a circle.
However, we will see that this is only the case because the disk morphism
D^{2}:\emptyset\to S^{1}

yields a trivial nullhomotopy of a certain obstruction category
\mathcal{F}_{g}
described below.
We hence restrict our attention to the wide subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

where cobordisms are not allowed to have connected components that are disks or spheres.
For this subcategory we show:

Theorem A
.

The rational homotopy groups of
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
are:

\pi_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\cong\mathbb{Q}\langle\alpha\rangle\oplus\mathbb{Q}\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{g\geq 2}H_{*}(\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{g};\mathbb{Q})

where
|\alpha|=1
,
|\rho_{i}|=4i+2
, and
\Delta_{g}
is the moduli space of tropical curves of genus
g
and volume
1
.

The classifying space of the surface category

Since
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
is a symmetric monoidal category its classifying space
B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

admits the structure of an infinite loop space.
It will be useful to keep track of this structure and all our computations respect it.
The only previously known computation regarding
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})
is a theorem
of Tillmann
[
Til96
]
, which shows
that there is an equivalence of infinite loop spaces
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})\simeq S^{1}\times X
,
where
X
is some simply connected infinite loop space.
It was conjectured
[
JT13
, Conjecture 5.3]
that
X
is contractible,
more generally that the classifying spaces
of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
and several related categories should be
1
-types.
One of our main theorems states that this is
almost
true:

Theorem B
.

There is an equivalence of infinite loop spaces
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2})\simeq S^{1}
.

Here
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}
is a
(2,1)
-category that we will think of as a refinement
of the truncated cobordism category in which closed components are counted “properly”.
In general it is defined as follows:

Definition 1.1
.

For all
d\geq 0
the
2
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{d}
has
as objects closed oriented
(d-1)
-manifolds and
morphisms
W:M\to N
are compact oriented cobordisms from
M
to
N
.
A
2
-morphism
\alpha:W\Rightarrow W^{\prime}
is a bijection
\alpha:\pi_{0}(W)\cong\pi_{0}(W^{\prime})

such that there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi:W\cong W^{\prime}

that is the identity on the boundary and satisfies
\pi_{0}(\varphi)=\alpha
.

It is important to note that the diffeomorphism
\varphi
is not part of
the data of the
2
-morphism
\alpha:W\Rightarrow W^{\prime}
.
There are always at most finitely many
2
-morphisms
W\Rightarrow W^{\prime}

and if
W
has no closed components, then there is at most one.
From this it follows that the quotient map

B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{d})\to B(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
is a rational equivalence.
For
d=2
we construct an equivalence of infinite loop spaces:

B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})\simeq B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2})\times\tau_{\geq 3}Q\left(\bigvee\nolimits_{g\geq 0}S^{2}\right).

Here
\tau_{\geq 3}Q(\bigvee_{g\geq 0}S^{2})
denotes the
2
-connected cover of the free infinite loop space on
\bigvee_{g\geq 0}S^{2}
.
As a consequence of Theorem
B
this is exactly
the mysterious infinite loop space
X
in Tillmann’s theorem.
It is rationally trivial and can be thought of as an “error-term” coming from
the fact that in
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
we are not counting the closed components “properly”.

The surface category without disks and the moduli space of tropical curves

The above result seems to suggest that
– as long as one looks at the slightly refined
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}
–
the classifying space of the surface category is not very interesting.
However, in the proof of Theorem
B
we will see
that this is only the case because several interesting subspaces of
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2})

happen to admit a null-homotopy.
This uses the fact that for any surface cobordism
W:M\to N

and point
p\in W
one may decompose
W=W^{\prime}\cup_{S^{1}}D^{2}
where
W^{\prime}

is obtained from
W
by removing a ball around
p
.
If one restricts to the following subcategory,
this is no longer possible and the homotopy type becomes more interesting.

Definition 1.2
.

The subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
is defined to contain all objects,
but only those morphisms
W:M\to N
where
no connected component of
W
is a disk or a sphere.

Equivalently, one can require that every component
V\subset W

has non-positive Euler characteristic, i.e.
\chi(V)\leq 0
.
Using this reformulation one checks that

\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
is closed under both composition and disjoint union.
1
1

1

One could also consider another, even larger subcategory

\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\rm no\ disks}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
where spheres are allowed.
This simply splits as a product
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\rm no\ disks}\cong\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\times\mathbb{N}
,
where the second factor counts the number of spheres.

By passing to this subcategory we uncover the rich homotopical structure
that was lurking behind the seemingly simple statement of Theorem
B
:

Theorem C
.

There is an equivalence of infinite loop spaces:

B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\simeq S^{1}\times Q(\Sigma^{2}BO_{2})\times Q\left(\bigvee\nolimits_{g\geq 2}\Sigma^{2}BJ_{g}\right).

The spaces
BJ_{g}
are the classifying spaces of certain finite categories
of stable graphs, which have appeared in the study of tropical curves.
In fact, we construct a rational homology isomorphism
BJ_{g}\to\Delta_{g}
,
where
\Delta_{g}
is the moduli space of tropical curves of genus
g
and volume
1
.
The rational homology of
\Delta_{g}
was described by
[
CGP21
]

in terms of Kontsevich’s commutative graph complex.
Following
[
Wil15
]
we let
\mathsf{fGC}_{0,\rm conn}
denote the graph complex
of connected graphs without tadpoles, where the degree of a graph
is given by its number of edges.
Combining these results we obtain:

Corollary D
.

The rational homotopy groups of
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
, or equivalently of
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
, are:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Q}\langle\alpha\rangle\oplus H_{*-1}(\mathsf{fGC}_{0,\rm conn}).

Here
\alpha
is a generator of
\pi_{1}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
The identification of the rational homotopy of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}

with the homology of the graph complex leads to exponential lower bounds on its dimension:
as observed in
[
CGP21
]
it follows from Willwacher’s computation

H^{0}(\mathsf{GC}_{2})=\mathfrak{grt}_{1}
in
[
Wil15
]
that

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H_{2g}(\mathsf{fGC}_{0,\rm conn})\geq\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H_{0}(\mathsf{GC}_{2}^{g\rm-loop})>1.3^{g}

for
g\gg 0
.
To better understand the connection between
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
and the
\Delta_{g}
,
we will construct an explicit comparison map,
illustrated in figure
1
.
Let
\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(X)
denote the infinite symmetric power on a based space
X
;
this is the free topological commutative monoid on
X
.
By
[
DT58
]
we have
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(X))\cong\tilde{H}_{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Theorem E
.

There is an explicit continuous map of partially defined commutative topological monoids:

\mu:B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}\left(\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{2}\vee\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{3}\vee\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{4}\vee\dots\right)

such that up to rational homotopy equivalence
it corresponds to the projection to the third factor in Theorem
C
.
In particular, the map on homotopy groups

\pi_{*}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\to\bigoplus_{g\geq 2}\tilde{H}_{*-2}(\Delta_{g})

rationally agrees with the map from Theorem
A
.

Figure 1:
An example of how the map
\mu
(defined in
6.9
)
can be evaluated on a
4
-simplex in
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
.
The
4
-simplex is parametrised by
(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})\in[0,1]^{5}
with
\sum t_{i}=1
.
The double-suspension
\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{g}
is given by triples
[(G,w,d),a,b]

where
a,b\in[0,1]
with
a+b\leq 1

and
(G,w,d)
a stable metric graph of genus
g
and volume
1-a-b
.
This is identified with the base-point if
a=0
or
b=0
.
To evaluate
\mu
we sum over closed components
V
of the diagram,
discarding components with boundary.
To each
V
we assign a stable metric graph
with an edge of length
t_{i}/k_{i}
for every time
V
intersects the
i
th vertical line,
where
V
intersects the
i
th line
k_{i}
times.
To be precise, valence
2
genus
0
vertices should be deleted and the length
of their adjacent edges added.
The coordinates
(a,b)
in the suspension
\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{g}
are given
by the sum of the
t_{i}
“before” and “after”
V
, respectively.

Labelled cospan categories

The techniques used to prove the main theorems stated so far apply
in much greater generality than just to
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
and its subcategories.
In fact, the main property of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
that we are using is
that we can talk about connected components of objects and morphisms.
One way of formalising this is to say that
\pi_{0}
defines
a symmetric monoidal functor:

\pi_{0}:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}\to\mathrm{Csp},\quad M\mapsto\pi_{0}(M),\quad([W]:M\to N)\mapsto[\pi_{0}(M)\to\pi_{0}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N)]

from
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
to the category
\mathrm{Csp}
of cospans in finite sets.
This category
\mathrm{Csp}
has as objects finite sets
A
and
as morphisms
A\to B
it has isomorphism classes of cospans
[A\to X\leftarrow B]
.
Two cospans are isomorphic if there is a bijection
\sigma:X\cong X^{\prime}

compatible with the maps from
A
and
B
.

Since connected surfaces are uniquely determined by their genus
and number of boundary components,
giving a morphism
M\to N
in
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
amounts to the same data
as giving a cospan
[\pi_{0}(M)\to X\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N)]

and a labeling
g:X\to\mathbb{N}
that encodes the genus of each component.
It will be a very useful perspective to think of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

as a category of cospans labelled by natural numbers
as indicated in figure
2
.

Figure 2:
A morphism in
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
can be thought of as a cospan of finite sets labelled in
\mathbb{N}
.

Formalising this leads to the definition of labelled cospan categories.
The reader is referred to section
2
,
which serves as an introduction to this paper from the perspective of labelled cospan categories.

Definition 1.3
.

A
labelled cospan category
is a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}

together with a symmetric monoidal functor
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp}

satisfying four axioms, which ensure that every object
and morphism in
\mathcal{C}
uniquely decomposes as the disjoint union
of its connected components. (See definition
2.4
for details.)

In the study of topologically enriched cobordism categories
a key theorem is that the inclusion of the positive boundary
subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{d}^{\partial_{+}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{d}
induces an equivalence on classifying spaces.
(See
[
GMTW09
, Theorem 6.1]
and
[
GRW14
, Theorem 3.1]
.)
Here the positive boundary subcategory contains all objects
and all those cobordisms
W:M\to N
where each component
V\subset W

intersects
N
non-trivially, i.e. has positive boundary.
We can define a positive boundary subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\subset\mathcal{C}

for any labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
by allowing those
morphisms
f:x\to y
where the right-hand arrow in the cospan

[\pi(x)\to\pi(f)\leftarrow\pi(y)]
is surjective.

For truncated cobordism categories the positive boundary category
is often much easier to compute.
For example we have
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{1}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq QS^{0}

by
[
Rap14
, Corollary 4.3.2]
,

B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq S^{1}
by
[
Til96
]
,
and
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\chi\leq 0,{\partial_{+}}})\simeq S^{1}
and
B(\mathrm{Csp}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq*

by proposition
4.27
.
However, we cannot generally expect the inclusion
of the positive boundary category to be an equivalence.
After all
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{1})
is more complicated than
QS^{0}
by
[
Ste21
]

and
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
is more complicated than
S^{1}
by Theorem
C
.

The key theorem about the classifying spaces of labelled cospan categories
will describe the failure of
B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\to B(\mathcal{C})
to be an equivalence.
For this theorem to be stated in its most natural form we
replace
\mathcal{C}
by a
(2,1)
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
,
in analogy with how we replaced
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
by
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}
.
This recovers the previous construction as
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})

and just like before it does not change the classifying space
up to rational equivalence:
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}B(\mathcal{C})
.

Theorem F

(Decomposition and Surgery Theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
be a labelled cospan category
that admits surgery (definition
2.42
)
and assume that
B(\mathcal{C})
is group-complete.
Then there is a fiber sequence of infinite loop spaces:

B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\longrightarrow Q\left(\bigvee\nolimits_{g}S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathcal{C}))\right).

Here the wedge runs over all connected morphisms
g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
,

\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathcal{C})
denotes the category of non-trivial factorisations

(g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to x\to 1_{\mathcal{C}})
from definition
2.34
,
and
S^{2}
denotes the unreduced double-suspension.

The condition of admitting surgery is satisfied by all truncated cobordism categories
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}

as well as by
\mathrm{Csp}
and various labelled cospan categories
that one can construct by labelling cospans in an abelian monoid.
The proof of Theorem
F
takes up the majority of this paper
(section
3
and
4
).
Once Theorem
F
is established
most other computations amount to determining the homotopy types
of the factorisation categories
\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathcal{C})
.
In the case of
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Csp}
we show that
B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
is contractible.
This concludes the computation started in
[
Stb19
]
:

Theorem G
.

The classifying space of the
(2,1)
-category of cospans
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}

is contractible and the classifying space of its homotopy category
is
B(\mathrm{Csp})\simeq\tau_{\geq 3}Q(S^{2})
.

We further show that
B\mathcal{F}_{[W]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
is contractible for any closed
d
-manifold
W
,

d\geq 2
.
This makes use of the disk morphisms
D^{d}:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}
,
which were not available in
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
.
This implies:

Theorem H
.

For any dimension
d\geq 2

the classifying space of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{d}
is equivalent to the classifying space
of its positive boundary subcategory:

B(\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{d})
.

The classifying spaces of factorisation categories are not always trivial, though.

[
Ste21
, Section 8]
implies that
\mathcal{F}_{S^{1}}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1})

is equivalent to Connes’ cyclic category
\Lambda
.
Even though
\mathrm{Cob}_{1}
does not admit surgery in the sense
of definition
2.42
,
the conclusion of Theorem
F

still seems to apply and the map
B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1})\to Q(\Sigma^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{S^{1}}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1})))

corresponds to the continuation of the reduction fiber sequence
that we constructed in
[
Ste21
, Section 7]
.

The subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
is another example where
the arguments for the contractibility of
\mathcal{F}_{g}
fail
as we cannot use the disk morphism
D^{2}:\emptyset\to S^{1}
.
In section
6
we prove Theorem
C

by showing that there is a zig-zag of functors inducing an equivalence
of classifying spaces
B(\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}))\simeq B(J_{g})
for all
g\geq 2
.

Outline

This paper is split into two parts.
In sections
2
,
3
, and
4

we study the general theory of labelled cospan categories,
and in sections
5
and
6
we focus on the case of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}

and its relation to
\Delta_{g}
.

We begin in section
2
by defining labelled cospan categories,
giving several general constructions, and stating the decomposition theorem
and the surgery theorem (which together form Theorem
F
).
Section
3
proves the decomposition theorem.
First we establish a general fiber sequence criterion
and a base-change theorem that will be useful throughout,
these are then applied to various spaces of cuts similar to
[
Ste21
, Section 7]
.
In the end of the section we also show that several factorisation categories
are contractible.
Section
4
proves the surgery theorem by applying ideas
of
[
Gal11
]
and
[
GRW14
]
to labelled cospan categories.
We also compute the positive boundary category of any weighted cospan category
by generalising
[
Til96
]
.
At this point Theorems
B
,
G
,
and
H
follow.

Section
5
relates the factorisation category
\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})

to the category of graphs
J_{g}
by constructing a category of filtered graphs

\int_{J}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
that admits functors to both.
We then show that both functors induce equivalences by showing that
the space of filtrations
B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
on any given graph
(X,w)
is contractible.
Together with the surgery and decomposition theorem this implies Theorem
C
.
Section
6
recalls the moduli space
\Delta_{g}
and
constructs a rational homology equivalence
BJ_{g}\to\Delta_{g}
.
Moreover, we prove Theorem
E
by constructing the rational splitting map

\mu:B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\to\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(\Sigma^{2}\Delta_{2}\vee\dots)
.

2
Labelled cospan categories

In this section we introduce the framework of labelled cospan categories,
give several basic constructions that will be needed later,
and state the two key theorems about classifying spaces of labelled cospan categories:
the decomposition theorem
3.1

and the surgery theorem
4.1
.

2.1
Labelled cospans

2.1.1
Definition

Definition 2.1
.

The cospan category
\mathrm{Csp}
has as objects finite sets
A

and as morphisms
A\to B
isomorphism classes
[A\to X\leftarrow B]

of cospans of finite sets. Here two cospans
f:A\to X\leftarrow B:g

and
f^{\prime}:A\to X^{\prime}\leftarrow B:g^{\prime}
are called isomorphic if there
is a bijection
\varphi:X\cong X^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}=\varphi\circ f
and
g^{\prime}=\varphi\circ g^{\prime}
.
The composite of two morphisms
[A\to X\leftarrow B]

and
[B\to Y\leftarrow C]
is defined by taking the pushout

[A\to X\cup_{B}Y\leftarrow C]
.
This is a symmetric monoidal category under disjoint union.

Example 2.2
.

The key to the following definitions is that any cobordism
W:M\to N

yields a cospan of finite sets
\pi_{0}(M)\to\pi_{0}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N)

in such a way that composition of cobordisms corresponds to compositon
of cospans. In other words, taking connected components defines
a symmetric monoidal functor
\pi_{0}:\mathrm{Cob}_{d}\to\mathrm{Csp}
.

Definition 2.3
.

Consider a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,1_{\mathcal{C}})

together with a symmetric monoidal functor
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp}
.
We say that an object
M\in\mathcal{C}
is
connected
with respect to
\pi

if
\pi(M)
is a set with one element.
Similarly, we say that a morphism
W:M\to N\in\mathcal{C}
is
connected

with respect to
\pi
if the set
\pi(W)
in the cospan

[\pi(M)\to\pi(W)\leftarrow\pi(N)]
has a single element.
We say that
W
is
reduced
if the cospan
[\pi(M)\to\pi(W)\leftarrow\pi(N)]

has the property that
\pi(M)\amalg\pi(N)\to\pi(W)
is surjective.
Let
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(M,N)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)

and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M,N)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)

denote the subset of connected and reduced morphisms, respectively.

The following definition encodes the idea that in
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
any object and cobordism
canonically decomposes into its set of connected components.

Definition 2.4
.

A
labelled cospan category
is a symmetric monoidal category

(\mathcal{C},\otimes,1_{\mathcal{C}})
together with a symmetric monoidal functor

\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp}
satisfying the following axioms:

(i)

For any object
M\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\pi(M)
has
n
elements
we can find connected objects
M_{1},\dots,M_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
such that

M\cong M_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes M_{n}
. If
n=0
, we require
M\cong 1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

(ii)

The abelian monoid
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})

is freely generated by the subset of
connected morphisms.

(iii)

For any two objects
M,N\in\mathcal{C}
the following map is a bijection

\otimes:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M,N)\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N).

(iv)

For any four objects
M_{1},M_{2},N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathcal{C}

the following diagram is a pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M_{1},N_{1})\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M_{2},N_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M_{1}\otimes M_{2},N_{1}\otimes N_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\pi(M_{1}),\pi(N_{1}))\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\pi(M_{2}),\pi(N_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\pi(M_{1})\amalg\pi(M_{2}),\pi(N_{1})\amalg\pi(N_{2}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg}

A functor of labelled cospan categories

F:(\pi_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})\to(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Csp})

is a symmetric monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
together with a
symmetric monoidal natural isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{\mathcal{D}}\circ F\cong\pi_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark 2.5
.

We will show in lemma
2.28
that any labelled cospan category

(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
is equivalent to another category
h\mathrm{Csp}(\mathcal{C})

where morphisms are indeed cospans of finite sets labelled
by connected morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. This justifies the name,
but the list of axioms might still seem a little arbitrary.
In definition
2.30
we provide a much simpler
definition in the world of
\infty
-categories, which conjecturally generalises
the above and only imposes a single axiom.
For now we shall motivate the definition by giving examples.

Example 2.6
.

For any dimension
d
and tangential structure
\theta
the truncated

\theta
-structured
d
-dimensional cobordism category
\mathrm{Cob}_{d,\theta}=h(\mathcal{C}_{d,\theta})

is a labelled cospan category when equipped with the symmetric monodial
functor
\pi_{0}:\mathrm{Cob}_{d,\theta}\to\mathrm{Csp}
that sends a closed
(d-1)
-manifold
M

to the finite set
\pi_{0}(M)
and a
d
-dimensional cobordism
W:M\to N

to the cospan
[\pi_{0}(M)\to\pi_{0}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N)]
.

Example 2.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
be some labelled cospan category and let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{C}

be a symmetric monodial subcategory such that:

(a)

If
M\otimes N\in\mathcal{D}
for some
M,N\in\mathcal{C}
, then
M,N\in\mathcal{D}
.

(b)

If
f\otimes g\in\mathcal{D}
for some
(f:M\to N),(g:M^{\prime}\to N^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
,
then
f,g\in\mathcal{D}
.

Then
(\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Csp})
is a labelled cospan category.
To see this we check the four properties:

(i)

This follows because we had such a decomposition in
\mathcal{C}
and
the connected objects appearing in the decomposition of an object of
\mathcal{D}

also need to lie in
\mathcal{D}
by (a).

(ii)

This follows because if a submonoid
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})

of a free monoid
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
satisfies the cancellation
property (b), then it is necessarily freely generated on a subset
of the generators of the original monoid.

(iii)

The map is injective because it is the restriction
of an injection and it is surjective by (b).

(iv)

The map from the top-left corner to the pullback is
injective because it is the restriction of an injection
and it is surjective by (b).

Combining this with the previous example we see that
(\pi_{0}:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\to\mathrm{Csp})

is a labelled cospan category.

2.1.2
Decomposing labelled cospans

The following lemma explains how we can think of any morphism
W:M\to N

as the cospan
\pi(M)\to\pi(W)\leftarrow\pi(N)
together with a labelling
of the components of
\pi(W)
by connected morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Consider two objects
M,N\in\mathcal{C}
and decompose them into connected objects

M=M_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes M_{m}
and
N=N_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes N_{n}
.
The the set of morphisms
M\to N
can be described as:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\times\coprod_{[X]:\pi(M)\to\pi(N)}\ \prod_{x\in X}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in f^{-1}(x)}M_{i},\bigotimes_{j\in g^{-1}(x)}N_{j}\right)

where the coproduct runs over all isomorphism classes of reduced cospans

f:\pi(M)\to X\leftarrow\pi(N):g
.
This decomposition is natural with respect to functors of labelled cospan categories.

Proof.

We have the decompositon

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M,N)

by condition (iii). The set of reduced morphisms maps to the set of
reduced cospans, so we get a disjoint decomposition

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M,N)\cong\coprod_{[X]:\pi(M)\to\pi(N)}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{[X]}(M,N)

where
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{[X]}(M,N)=\{W:M\to N\;|\;\pi(W)=[X]\}
is the set
of morphisms with underlying cospan
X
. To understand this set of morphisms
we decompose
M
and
N
according to the cospan
X
:

M\cong\bigotimes_{x\in X}M_{x},\quad M_{x}:=\bigotimes_{i\in f^{-1}(x)}M_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad N\cong\bigotimes_{x\in X}N_{x},\quad N_{x}:=\bigotimes_{j\in g^{-1}(x)}N_{j}

Here we identified
\pi(M)
with
\{1,\dots,m\}
according to the
decomposition
M=M_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes M_{m}
and similarly for
N
.
We can now apply condition (iv) multiple times and glue the pullback squares
to obtain the following pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{x\in X}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M_{x},N_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(\otimes_{x\in X}M_{x},\otimes_{x\in X}N_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{x\in X}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\pi(M_{x}),\pi(N_{x}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\amalg_{x\in X}\pi(M_{x}),\amalg_{x\in X}\pi(N_{x})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg}

By definition of
M_{x}
and
N_{x}
the cospan
[\pi(M)\to X\leftarrow\pi(N)]

corresponds to the
element of the product
\prod_{x\in X}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(\pi(M_{x}),\pi(N_{x}))

that is given by the unique connected cospan

[\pi(M_{x})\to\{x\}\leftarrow\pi(N_{x})]

in each entry.
We can now compare the fibers of the vertical maps at the points corresponding to
[X]
.
Since the diagram is a pullback square the fibers are isomorphic:

\prod_{x\in X}\{W\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M_{x},N_{x})\;|\;\ \pi(W)=\{x\}\}\cong\{W\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(\otimes_{x\in X}M_{x},\otimes_{x\in X}N_{x})\;|\;\pi(W)=[X]\}.

The left-hand side is the product over
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(M_{x},N_{x})

and the right-hand side is
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{[X]}(M,N)
.
Together with the first part this concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

A functor
F:(\pi_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})\to(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Csp})

of labelled cospan categories is an equivalence of categories
if and only if it satisfies the following two conditions:

•

For any connected object
M_{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathcal{D}
there is a connected object

M_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
F(M_{\mathcal{C}})\cong M_{\mathcal{D}}
.

•

For any two objects
M,N\in\mathcal{C}
the functor
F
induces a bijection

F:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(M,N)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm con}(F(M),F(N)).

Proof.

The first condition implies that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is essentially surjective
because by condition (i) every object in
\mathcal{D}
is the product of connected objects.
The second condition applied to
M=1_{\mathcal{C}}=N
implies, together with condition (ii),
that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(1_{\mathcal{D}},1_{\mathcal{D}})
is a bijection.
Then we can use the decomposition from lemma
2.8

to see that being a bijection on connected morphisms and endomorphisms of
1_{\mathcal{C}}

implies that
F
is a bijection on all homs.
∎

For any labelled cospan category we can define a reduced category in which
all closed components are deleted.

Definition 2.10
.

The reduced category
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}

and the morphisms are equivalence classes of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}

under the action of the abelian monoid
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
.
This indeed defines a category as for any
W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}

the action
(W\amalg\underline{\ \ }):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)
.
commutes with pre- and post-composition with any morphism.

Note that it follows from the axioms for labelled cospan categories
that every morphism
[W]:M\to N
in
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
has a unique
representative
W\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M\to N)
that is reduced.
In other words, the composite

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(M,N)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}}(M,N)

is a bijection.
We could therefore equivalently define the category
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}

to have the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and to have as morphisms the reduced morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
Composition would then be defined by
W\circ_{\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}}V=U

where
U
is the unique reduced morphism such that there is
Q:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}

with
W\circ_{\mathcal{C}}V=U\amalg Q
.

Example 2.11
.

There is a canonical bijection between
the set of equivalence relations on
A\amalg B
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Csp}}^{\rm red}(A,B)

defined by sending an equivalence relation
R\subset(A\amalg B)^{2}

to the cospan
[A\to(A\amalg B)/\!R\leftarrow B]
.
This can be used to give an alternative description of
\mathrm{Csp}^{\rm red}

where morphisms
A\to B
are equivalence relations on
A\amalg B
.
In this category morphisms
R\subset(A\amalg B)^{2}
and
S\subset(B\amalg C)^{2}

are composed by restricting the equivalence relation on
A\amalg B\amalg C

that is generated by
R
and
S
to the subset
A\amalg C
.

2.1.3
Weighted cospans

Another class of examples can be constructed by labeling cospans with elements
of an abelian monoid.
This includes
\mathrm{Csp}
,
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
, and will provide a useful point of view
in section
5
.
In principle it should be possible to label cospans with operations
of any modular operad, or even a coloured properad
(see conjecture
2.31
).
For the purpose of this thesis it will suffice to consider the case
where the operations in the modular operad are elements or an abelian monoid
A

and pairing two inputs is given by addition with a fixed element
\alpha
.
We also allow to specify a subset
A_{1}\subset A
, which can be thought
of as a stability condition.

Definition 2.12
.

A
weighting monoid
is a triple
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
where
A
is an abelian monoid, which we will denote additively,

A_{1}\subset A
is a subset satisfying
A_{1}+A\subset A_{1}
,
and
\alpha\in A_{1}
is some element.

The most important weighting monoid is
(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},1)
,
and we will usually think about this case.

Definition 2.13
.

For a monoid
(A,+)
with a preferred element
\alpha\in A

we define the
(A,\alpha)
-weighted cospan category
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)
as follows.
Objects are finite sets.
Morphisms are equivalence classes of labelled cospans

[i:M\to X\leftarrow N:j,l:X\to A]
.
Two morphisms are equivalent if there is a bijection
\varphi:X\cong X^{\prime}

satisfying
l^{\prime}\circ\varphi=l
,
\varphi\circ i=i^{\prime}
, and
\varphi\circ j=j^{\prime}
.
The composition of two morphisms
(i:M\to X\leftarrow N:j,l)
and

(i^{\prime}:N\to Y\leftarrow L:j^{\prime},l^{\prime})
is defined by composing the cospans as usual
and equipping the pushout with the labeling:

(l*l^{\prime})([u]\in X\amalg_{N}Y)=\sum_{x\in X,x\in[u]}l(x)+\sum_{y\in Y,y\in[u]}l^{\prime}(y)+\alpha^{b_{1}([u];X,Y)}.

Here
b_{1}([u];X,Y)\in\mathbb{N}
is what one could call the first Betti number
of the equivalence class
[u]\subset X\amalg Y
.
Concretely, we define it as:

b_{1}([u];X,Y):=|(N\to X\amalg_{N}Y)^{-1}([u])|-|(X\amalg Y\to X\amalg_{N}Y)^{-1}([u])|+1.

Lemma 2.14
.

Definition
2.13
yields a well-defined category
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)

and disjoint union defines a symmetric monoidal structure on this category.
Moreover, the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)\to\mathrm{Csp}
is symmetric monoidal
and makes
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)
into a labelled cospan category.

Proof.

To see that
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)
we need to check that composition is associative.
Since we already know that the composition of cospans is associative
up to canonical isomorphism, all that is left check is that this canonical
isomorphism respects the labeling.
Consider three composable morphisms
(i:M\to X\leftarrow N:j,l)
,

(i^{\prime}:N\to Y\leftarrow L:j^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, and
(i^{\prime\prime}:L\to Z\leftarrow O:j^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})
.
To see that
(l*l^{\prime})*l^{\prime\prime}\equiv l*(l^{\prime}*l^{\prime\prime})
it suffices to check the following
identity on Betti numbers for every point
[u]\in X\amalg_{N}Y\amalg_{L}Z
:

\displaystyle b_{1}([u];X\amalg_{N}Y,Z)+\sum_{[v]\in X\amalg_{N}Y,[v]\subset[u]}b_{1}([v];X,Y)

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}b_{1}([u];X,Y\amalg_{L}Z)+\sum_{[w]\in Y\amalg_{L}Z,[w]\subset[u]}b_{1}([w];Y,Z)

(The point is represented by an equivalence class
[u]\subset X\amalg Y\amalg Z
.)
Indeed one checks that both sides of the equation are equal to

|(N\amalg L\to(X\amalg_{N}Y\amalg_{L}Z))^{-1}([u])|-|(X\amalg Y\amalg Z\to(X\amalg_{N}Y\amalg_{L}Z))^{-1}([u])|+1.

Hence the composition in
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)
is indeed associative.

The composition of
(A,\alpha)
-labelled cospans is defined component-wise
and hence disjoint union
\amalg:\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)\times\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)\to\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)

is a functor. This defines a symmetric monoidal structure with the same
structure isomorphisms as for
\mathrm{Csp}
(now labelled by
0
).
By construction the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)\to\mathrm{Csp}
is symmetric monoidal.
Moreover, one checks that this functor satisfies the conditions of
definition
2.4
and hence defines
a labelled cospan category.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

For
(A,\alpha)
as above and a subset
A_{1}\subset A
satisfying
\alpha\in A_{1}

and
A_{1}+A\subset A_{1}
we define

\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)\subset\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)

as the subcategory that contains all objects and those morphisms

(i:M\to X\leftarrow N:j,l)
where for each
x\in X

with
|i^{-1}(x)\amalg j^{-1}(x)|\leq 1
we require
l(x)\in A_{1}
.

One checks that
\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)\subset\mathrm{Csp}(A,\alpha)
is indeed
closed under composition by using
\alpha\in A_{1}
and
A_{1}+A\subset A_{1}
.
It is also closed under disjoint union and further satisfies the condition
of example
2.7
. Hence
\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
is a labelled cospan category.

Lemma 2.16
.

There are equivalences of labelled cospan categories:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Csp}(0,0)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Csp},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Cob}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}.

Proof.

The first equivalence is clear since a cospan labelled in the trivial monoid
0

is the same data as just a monoid, so the forgetful map
\mathrm{Csp}(0,0)\to\mathrm{Csp}

is an isomorphism of categories.

For the second equivalence we define a functor
F:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}\to\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},1)

as the lift of the functor
\pi_{0}:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}\to\mathrm{Csp}
by labelling the
cospan
[\pi_{0}(M)\to\pi_{0}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N)]
obtained from a morphism
W:M\to N

with the function
g:\pi_{0}(W)\to\mathbb{N}
that sends a component to its genus.
We need to check that
F
is in fact functorial. This means that we
have to verify for any two cobordisms
W:M\to N
and
V:N\to L

that the genus of some component
U\subset W\cup_{N}V
is given by:

\sum_{U_{0}\in\pi_{0}(U\cap W)}g(U_{0})+\sum_{U_{1}\in\pi_{0}(U\cap V)}g(U_{1})+\big(|\pi_{0}(U\cap N)|+1-|\pi_{0}(U\cap W)\amalg\pi_{0}(U\cap V)|\big).

Without loss of generality we may assume that
W\cup_{N}V
is connected
and hence that
U=W\cup_{N}V
. The Euler characteristic is additive
under the composition of surface-bordisms and so we can compute:

\displaystyle\chi(W\cup_{N}V)

\displaystyle=\chi(W)+\chi(V)=\sum_{U_{0}\subset W}(2-2g(U_{0})-|\pi_{0}(\partial U_{0})|)+\sum_{U_{1}\subset V}(2-2g(U_{1})-|\pi_{0}(\partial U_{1})|)

\displaystyle=-2\left(\sum_{U_{0}\subset W}g(U_{0})+\sum_{U_{1}\subset V}g(U_{1})\right)+2|\pi_{0}(W)\amalg\pi_{0}(V)|-2|\pi_{0}(N)|-|\pi_{0}(M)|-|\pi_{0}(L)|

Combining this with
\chi(W\cup_{N}V)=2-2g(W\cup_{N}V)-|\pi_{0}(M)|-|\pi_{0}(L)|

yields the desired formula.

The functor
F
is by construction symmetric monoidal and compatible
with the projection to
\mathrm{Csp}
.
We may hence use corollary
2.9

to check that it is an equivalence of labelled cospan categories.
Indeed,
F
is surjective on connected objects (it hits the one-point set)
and
F
is fully faithful on connected morphisms since a connected surface
with fixed boundary is uniquely determined by its genus.

The third equivalence is obtained by restricting the previous equivalence.
The subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
corresponds exactly to
\mathrm{Csp}^{\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}(\mathbb{N},1)

since every connected cobordism
W:M\to N
satisfies:

\chi(W)\leq 0\Leftrightarrow(|\pi_{0}(M)\amalg\pi_{0}(N)|\leq 1\Rightarrow g(W)\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}).

∎

Example 2.17
.

For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}
one can define a category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{g<\gamma}

as the quotient of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
by the equivalence relation
\sim_{\gamma}

on morphisms that is generated as follows:
whenever
W:M\to N
is a cobordism and
V\subset W
is a component of
genus at least
g
, then we set
W\sim_{\gamma}W\#(S^{1}\times S^{1})

where the connected sum is taken at
V
.
Under this equivalence relation two morphisms
W,W^{\prime}:M\to N

are identified if they become diffeomorphic after arbitarily increasing
the genus of every component that has genus at least
g
.

This category is equivalent to the weighted cospan category

\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N}/\gamma,1)
where
\mathbb{N}/\gamma
is the abelian monoid
defined as
\mathbb{N}/(\mathbb{N}+\gamma)
.
For
\gamma=0
we have that
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{g<0}\simeq\mathrm{Csp}
.

One can also define a subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\chi\leq 0,g<\gamma}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{g<\gamma}
.
(This will be the entire category if
\gamma=0
.)
This category is equivalent to
\mathrm{Csp}^{\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}/\gamma}(\mathbb{N}/\gamma,1)
.

Example 2.18
.

For any
d\geq 2
consider the full subcategory
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{S^{d-1}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{d}

of the truncated
d
-dimensional oriented cobordism category on all the objects
of the form
\coprod_{i=1}^{k}S^{d-1}
.
2
2

2

Equivalently we could consider the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}

on all objects that are diffeomorphic to a disjoint union of spheres.
However, without choosing identifications with
S^{d-1}

it is more difficult to define the functor
F
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{d}
be the monoid of diffeomorphism classes of connected closed oriented

d
-manifolds with addition given by connected sum.
For
d=2
we have
\mathcal{M}_{2}\cong\mathbb{N}
and for
d=3
Milnor
[
Mil62
]
showed
that the monoid
\mathcal{M}_{3}
is freely generated by the set of diffeomorphism classes
of prime
3
-manifolds.
We define a functor

F:\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{S^{d-1}}\to\mathrm{Csp}(\mathcal{M}_{d},[S^{1}\times S^{d-1}])

equipping the cospan
(\pi_{0}(M)\to\pi_{0}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{0}(N))

with the labeling
l:\pi_{0}(W)\to\mathcal{M}_{d}
that sends
a connected component
(W^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\to N^{\prime})\subset(W:M\to N)

to the closed manifold

\widehat{W^{\prime}}:=(\amalg_{i=1}^{k}D^{d})\cup_{\amalg S^{d-1}}W^{\prime}\cup_{\amalg S^{d-1}}(\amalg_{j=1}^{l}D^{d}).

Here we use standard identifications

M^{\prime}\cong\amalg_{i=1}^{k}S^{d-1}
and
N^{\prime}\cong\amalg_{j=1}^{l}S^{d-1}
,
which are canonical up to permutation of components.

We need to show that
F
is functorial.
Let
W:M\to N
and
V:N\to L
two morphisms in
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{S^{d-1}}

such that
W\cup_{N}V
is connected. Then we need to check:

\widehat{W\cup_{N}V}\cong(\#_{i}\widehat{W_{i}})\,\#\,(\#_{j}\widehat{V_{j}})\,\#\,(S^{1}\times S^{d-1})^{\#b_{1}}

where
W_{i}\subset W
,
V_{i}\subset V
denote the connected components and

b_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
is defined as
|\pi_{0}(N)|-|\pi_{0}(W\amalg V)|+1
.
Indeed,
\widehat{W\cup_{N}V}
can be obtained from

\coprod_{i}\widehat{W_{i}}\amalg\coprod_{j}\widehat{V_{j}}

by attaching a
1
-handle for every component of
N
.
Let us attach these
1
-handles one after another.
Whenever the
1
-handle is attached at two disks that are not yet path-connected,
attaching this handle is equivalent to taking a connected sum at these disks.
This will happen
|\pi_{0}(W\amalg V)|-1
times.
For each of the remaining
1
-handles we attach the both endpoints
to the same component. This is equivalent to taking the connected
sum with
(S^{1}\times S^{d-1})
and will happen

|\pi_{0}(N)|-(|\pi_{0}(W\amalg V)|-1)=b_{1}
times.
Hence the above formula holds and
F
is indeed a functor.
It follows from the construction that
F
is symmetric monoidal
and defines a functor of labelled cospan categories.

We would like to apply corollary
2.9

to show that
F
is an equivalence of labelled cospan categories.
Since
F
is certainly essentially surjective we only need to check
that for all
k,l\geq 0
the map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cob}_{d}}^{\rm con}(\amalg_{i=1}^{k}S^{d-1},\amalg_{i=1}^{l}S^{d-1})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{d},\qquad[W]\mapsto[\widehat{W}]

is a bijection.
To construct an inverse map choose for every connected closed oriented

d
-manifold
V
an orientation preserving embedding

\iota:(\amalg_{i=1}^{k}D^{d})\amalg(\amalg_{j=1}^{l}D^{d})\hookrightarrow V
.
Then
V\cong\widehat{W_{\iota}}
for
W_{\iota}=V\setminus\mathrm{Im}(\iota)^{\circ}
.
For any other choice of orientation preserving embedding
\iota^{\prime}
there is a diffeomorphism

\psi:V\cong V
with
\iota^{\prime}\cong\psi\circ\iota
(by
[
Hir94
, Chapter 8, Theorem 3.2]
).
This restricts to a diffeomorphism
\psi_{|W_{\iota}}:W_{\iota}\cong W_{\iota^{\prime}}

that is compatible with the boundary identifications.
So the construction
[V]\mapsto[W_{\iota}]
is independent of
\iota

and hence a well-defined map

\mathcal{M}_{d}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cob}_{d}}^{\rm con}(\amalg_{i=1}^{k}S^{d-1},\amalg_{i=1}^{l}S^{d-1})

that is inverse to
[W]\mapsto[\widehat{W}]
.

Note that the above example fails in the non-oriented case.
The cylinder with flipped boundary identification defines
a non-identity morphism
C:S^{1}\to S^{1}
in the unoriented surface category

\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\rm unor}
such that
\widehat{C}=D^{2}\cup_{S^{1}}C\cup_{S^{1}}D^{2}\cong S^{2}
.
Therefore the functor
F:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\rm unor}\to\mathrm{Csp}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\rm unor},[S^{1}\times S^{1}])

is not faithful.

2.2
The enhanced cospan category

Given a labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
we now want to build
a refinement
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
that keeps track of permutations of closed components.
We begin by defining the groupoid-enriched category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
:
the
2
-category of finite sets, cospans of finite sets, and isomorphisms of cospans.
To ensure that everything is a set let
\Omega
denote some infinite “background set”.

This subsection might be skipped at a first read. By theorem
3.4

the canonical functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
induces a rational equivalence
on classifying spaces.

2.2.1
The unlabelled case

Definition 2.19
.

A
presented set
is a triple
(l,X,R)
where
l\in\{0,1,\dots\}
,

X\subset\{1,\dots,l\}
, and
R\subset X\times X
,
such that
R
is an equivalence relation on
X
.
We let
\underline{X}:=X/R
denote the set of equivalence classes of
X
under
R
,
and we will often write
\underline{X}
when we mean the entire datum
(l,X,R)
.

Two presented sets
(l,X,R)
and
(k,Y,S)
are called
disjoint

if
l=k
and
X\cap Y=\emptyset
. In this case we write
(l,X,R)\bot(k,Y,S)

and we define their disjoint union as
(l,X,R)\amalg(k,Y,S):=(l,X\cup Y,R\cup S)
.

For any two presented sets
(l,X,R)
and
(k,Y,S)
and maps
i:A\to X/R

and
j:A\to Y/S
from some set
A
we define the
glueing
as

(l,X,R)\cup_{A}(k,Y,S):=(l+k,X\cup(Y+l),R\cup_{A}(S+l))

where
Y+l:=\{y+l\;|\;y\in Y\}
and
R\cup_{A}(S+l)
is
the equivalence relation generated by
R
,
(S+l)
, and
i(a)\sim j(a)

for all
a\in A
.

Note that the disjoint union of disjoint presented sets is strictly associative
and commutative whenever it is defined.
Moreover, the gluing operation

(\underline{X}\leftarrow A\to\underline{Y})\to\underline{X}\cup_{A}\underline{Y}

is strictly associative, which allows us to make the following definition:

Definition 2.20
.

The
(2,1)
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
is defined as follows.
Objects are finite subsets
A\subset\Omega
.
Non-identity morphisms
A\to B
are cospans
(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g_{X})

where
\underline{X}
is a presented set.
We also have identity morphisms, which we denote as
(A=A=A)
.
The composition of morphisms is defined as:

(f_{Y}:B\to\underline{Y}\leftarrow C:g_{Y})\circ(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g_{X}):=(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}\leftarrow C:g_{Y}).

2
-morphisms
\varphi:(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g_{X})\Rightarrow(f_{X}^{\prime}\to\underline{X}^{\prime}\leftarrow B:g_{X}^{\prime})

are bijections
\varphi:\underline{X}\cong\underline{Y}
such that

\varphi\circ f_{X}=f_{X}^{\prime}
and
\varphi\circ g_{X}=g_{X}^{\prime}
.
(Note that
\varphi
is not an isomorphism of presented sets,
but just a bijection between the resulting quotient sets.)
These are composed in the obvious way.

Remark 2.21
.

The addition of identity morphisms in the above definition is a little ad-hoc.
Let us briefly explain how they interact with the other morphisms.
The horizontal composition is defined such that they act as identities:

(A=A=A)\cup_{A}(A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B):=(A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B)
,
and similarly when composing on the other side.
The
2
-morphisms are defined by treating the middle
A
as if it were
a presented set
\underline{X}
:
a
2
-morphism
\varphi:(A=A=A)\Rightarrow(f:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow A:g)

is a bijection
\varphi:A\to\underline{X}
such that
\varphi\circ\mathrm{id}_{A}=f_{X}

and
\varphi\circ\mathrm{id}_{A}=g_{X}
.
This means that such a
2
-morphism exists if and only if
f=g

is a bijection and the
2
-morphisms is unique if it exists.

To describe the operation of disjoint union on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
we will use
the notion of partial commutative monoidal structures that we introduce in
definition
3.5
and
3.11
.
See section
3.1
for an introduction to how we deal
with symmetric monoidal structures.

Definition 2.22
.

The partial commutative monoidal structure on the
(2,1)
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}

is defined by the relation
\bot
and the operation
\amalg
.
On objects we set
A\bot B
whenever
A\cap B=\emptyset

and on morphisms we set
(\underline{X},i,i^{\prime})\bot(\underline{Y},j,j^{\prime})
whenever

\underline{X}\bot\underline{Y}
in the sense of
2.19
.
The operation
\amalg
is given by taking disjoint unions.

Lemma 2.23
.

The canonical functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}\to\mathrm{Csp}
that sends a cospan

(A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B)
to its isomorphism class
induces an equivalence of categories
h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})\simeq\mathrm{Csp}
.

Proof.

The
1
-category
h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})
is defined by identifying all
1
-morphisms
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
that are connected by a
2
-morphism.
The functor
h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})\to\mathrm{Csp}
is hence faithful by definition:
on both sides isomorphic cospans are identified.
It is also full because every cospan
A\to Z\leftarrow B
is isomorphic
to a cospan
A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B
seeing as any finite set
Z

is in bijection with some presented set
\underline{X}
.
The functor is also essentially surjective as any finite set
A

can be embedded
\iota:A\hookrightarrow\Omega
and the cospan

(A\cong\iota(A)=\iota(A))
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Csp}
.
∎

2.2.2
The general case

We now define a version of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
where the finite sets and cospans are
labelled by connected objects and morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
In lemma
2.27
we check that this is indeed well-defined
and that the
2
-functors described below are indeed functorial.

Definition 2.24
.

For any labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
we define the

enhanced cospan category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}

to be the following
(2,1)
-category.

•

An objects is a tuple
(A,c_{A})
where
A\subset\Omega
is a finite set
and
c_{A}:A\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
is a labelling by connected objects of
\mathcal{C}
.

•

A non-identity
1
-morphism
(A,c_{A})\to(B,c_{B})
is a tuple
of a non-identity morphisms
(f:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g)
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}

and a labelling
o:\underline{X}\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})

such that for each
x\in\underline{X}
the label
o(x)
is a connected morphism

\bigotimes_{a\in f^{-1}(x)}c_{A}(a)\to\bigotimes_{b\in g^{-1}(x)}c_{B}(b).

We also allow for identity morphisms
\mathrm{id}_{(A,c_{A})}
, which we think of as
a cospan
(A=A=A)
where the labelling
o:A\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
is
given by
o(a):=\mathrm{id}_{c_{A}(a)}
.

•

A
2
-morphism

(f:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g,o)\Rightarrow(f^{\prime}:A\to\underline{Y}\leftarrow B:g^{\prime},o^{\prime})

is a bijection
\varphi:\underline{X}\cong\underline{Y}
such that

f^{\prime}=\varphi\circ f
,
g^{\prime}=\varphi\circ g
, and
o^{\prime}\circ\varphi=o
.

The composition of two morphisms

(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g_{X},o_{X})
and
(f_{Y}:B\to\underline{Y}\leftarrow C:g_{Y},o_{Y})

is defined as the glued cospan

(f_{Z}:A\to\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}\leftarrow C:g_{Z},o_{X}*o_{Y})

where the induced labeling
o_{X}*o_{Y}
is defined as follows.
For each
z\in\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}
we let

\underline{X}_{z}\subset\underline{X}
and
\underline{Y}_{z}\subset\underline{Y}
denote
the preimages of
z
under
\underline{X}\amalg\underline{Y}\to\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{a\in f_{Z}^{-1}(z)}c_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{c\in g_{Z}^{-1}(z)}c_{C}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{x\in X_{z}}\bigotimes\limits_{a\in f_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{x\in X_{z}}\bigotimes\limits_{b\in g_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{B}(b)\cong\bigotimes\limits_{y\in Y_{z}}\bigotimes\limits_{b\in f_{Y}^{-1}(y)}c_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{y\in Y_{z}}\bigotimes\limits_{c\in g_{Y}^{-1}(y)}c_{C}(c).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(o_{X}*o_{Y})(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{x}o_{X}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{y}o_{Y}(y)}

Definition 2.25
.

There is a functor
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
defined by sending

(A,c_{A})
to
\bigotimes_{a\in A}c_{A}(a)
and
sending morphisms
(f_{X}:A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B:g_{X},o)
to

\bigotimes_{a\in A}c_{A}(a)\cong\bigotimes_{x\in\underline{X}}\bigotimes_{a\in f_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{A}(a)\xrightarrow{\otimes_{[x]}o([x])}\bigotimes_{x\in\underline{X}}\bigotimes_{b\in g_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{B}(a)\cong\bigotimes_{b\in B}c_{B}(b).

Definition 2.26
.

We define a partial commutative monodial structure (see definition
3.5
)
on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
by saying that two objects or morphisms are disjoint
iff they are disjoint as objects or morphisms in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
after forgetting
labelings. The union of disjoint objects or morphisms is defined by
taking the union in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
and equipping it with the evident labeling.

Lemma 2.27
.

Horizontal composition of
1
-morphisms in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
is associative
and hence
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
is a well-defined
2
-category.
Moreover, the following three constructions are
2
-functors:

\mathrm{forget}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp},\quad\amalg:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{2\bot}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}),\quad\text{and}\quad\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}.

Proof.

We will show that associativity holds when composing three
composable labelled cospans:

(A\to\underline{X}\leftarrow B,o_{X})
,

(B\to\underline{Y}\leftarrow C,o_{Y})
,

(C\to\underline{Z}\leftarrow D,o_{Z})
.
The functoriality of “
\mathrm{forget}
”,
\amalg
, and
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}
will follow from the proof.
The underlying cospan is
\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}\cup_{C}\underline{Z}

and it does not matter how we parenthesise this expression
as composition in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
is strictly associative.
We need to check that the two induced labellings on this composed cospan agree.

Before we show this, let us consider the case of a single composition

(\underline{X},o_{X})\cup_{B}(\underline{Y},o_{Y})
.
For
p\in\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}
we let
\underline{X}_{p}\subset\underline{X}
,

\underline{Y}_{p}\subset\underline{Y}
, and
B_{p}\subset B
denote the subsets
that are sent to
p
under the quotient map.
The label
(o_{X}*o_{Y})(p)
of
p
in the composite cospan is exactly the morphism

\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p}\cup_{B_{p}}\underline{Y}_{p},o_{X}*o_{Y})
in
\mathcal{C}
.
(If
(\underline{W},o_{W})
is a labelled cospan where
\underline{W}=\{p\}

is a single point, then
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{W},o_{W})=o_{W}(p)
.)
By inspecting the definition it follows that this composite label is
exactly
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Y}_{p},o_{Y})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p},o_{X})

as illustrated in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{a\in A}c_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{c\in C}c_{C}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{x\in X_{p}}\bigotimes\limits_{a\in f_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{A}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{b\in B_{p}}c_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{y\in Y_{p}}\bigotimes\limits_{c\in g_{Y}^{-1}(y)}c_{C}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{x\in X_{p}}\bigotimes\limits_{b\in g_{X}^{-1}(x)}c_{B}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes\limits_{y\in Y_{p}}\bigotimes\limits_{b\in f_{Y}^{-1}(y)}c_{B}(b)}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(o_{X}*o_{Y})(p)={\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p}\cup_{B_{p}}\underline{Y}_{p},o_{X}*o_{Y})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p},o_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{x}o_{X}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Y}_{p},o_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{y}o_{Y}(y)}

This already implies that
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

will be a
2
-functor once we show that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
is a
2
-category.

Returning to the case of a triple composition we can now see
that a point
q\in\underline{X}\cup_{B}\underline{Y}\cup_{C}\underline{Z}
is labelled by:

\displaystyle((o_{X}*o_{Y})*o_{Z})(q)

\displaystyle=\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{q}\cup_{B_{q}}\underline{Y}_{q},o_{X}*o_{Y})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Z}_{q},o_{Z})

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{p\in\underline{X}_{q}\cup_{B_{q}}\underline{Y}_{q}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p}\cup_{B_{p}}\underline{Y}_{p},o_{X}*o_{Y})\right)\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Z}_{q},o_{Z})

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{p\in\underline{X}_{q}\cup_{B_{q}}\underline{Y}_{q}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{p},o_{X})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Y}_{p},o_{Y})\right)\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Z}_{q},o_{Z})

\displaystyle=(\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{X}_{q},o_{X})\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Y}_{q},o_{Y}))\circ\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\underline{Z}_{q},o_{Z})

Since composition in
\mathcal{C}
is associative it follows that this
agrees with
(o_{X}*(o_{Y}*o_{Z}))(q)
and hence horizontal composition in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})

is indeed associative.
∎

Lemma 2.28
.

The enhanced cospan category fits into a diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Csp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Moreover, for any two objects
(A,c_{A})
and
(B,c_{B})
there is
a canonical equivalence of groupoids

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}(\emptyset,\emptyset)\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(\otimes(A,c_{A}),\otimes(B,c_{B})).

and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
is the free symmetric monoidal
groupoid on the set
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
.

Proof.

The existence of the commutative diagram is clear by construction.
We need to check that
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence
of categories. Note that
\pi_{0}:h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to h(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})\simeq\mathrm{Csp}

is a labelled cospan category by construction,
so we can use corollary
2.9
to check
that
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivalence.
Indeed, it is clear that
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}
is surjective on connected objects,
and one also easily sees that
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}
is fully faithful on
connected morphisms.

For the second claim we begin by defining a functor:

(c,r):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}(\emptyset,\emptyset)\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}^{\rm red}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B})).

Here
c(\underline{X},o)=(\underline{Y},o_{|\underline{Y}})
where
\underline{Y}\subset\underline{X}

is the subset containing those equivalence classes that are not
in the image of
f\amalg g:A\amalg B\to\underline{X}
.
Similarly we let
r(\underline{X},o)=(\underline{Z},o_{|\underline{Z}})

where
\underline{Z}\subset\underline{X}
is the image of
f\amalg g
.
One now checks that
(c,r)
is indeed a functor and that it is an isomorphism
between
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))
and the subspace of the product
on those
((\underline{Y},o),(\underline{Z},o^{\prime}))
such that
\underline{Y}
and
\underline{Z}
are disjoint.
Hence
(c,r)
is fully faithful, and it is essentially surjective because
any tuple is isomorphic to one that satisfies the disjointness.
It remains to check that the functor
\otimes
induces an equivalence of groupoids:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}^{\rm red}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(\otimes(A,c_{A}),\otimes(B,c_{B})).

By lemma
2.8
this functor induces a bijection
between the isomorphism classes of objects on the left
and the elements on the right.
Moreover, any object in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}^{\rm red}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))

only has the identity automorphism as
\varphi:\underline{X}\to\underline{X}

is uniquely determined by the requirement that it has to be compatible
with the surjection
A\amalg B\to\underline{X}
.
The right-hand side has only identity morphisms by definition
and hence the two groupoids are equivalent.

Note also that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
is equivalent
to the groupoid of finite sets labelled in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
,
which is indeed the free symmetric monoidal groupoid on this set.
∎

2.2.3
Higher labelled cospan categories

While the list of conditions used to characterise labelled cospan categories
in definition
2.4
is rather long
and might seem a bit arbitrary, the enhanced version
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
satisfies
a much simpler condition:

Lemma 2.29
.

For any labelled cospan category
(\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})

the following diagram is a pullback square of
2
-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{2\bot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}^{2\bot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{0})^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg}

Proof.

On objects we have that

\displaystyle\{A,B\subset\Omega,

\displaystyle\ l:A,B\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})\;|\;A\cap B=\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\{A,B\subset\Omega\;|\;A\cap B=\emptyset\}\times_{\{C\subset\Omega\}}\{C\subset\Omega,l:C\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})\}

and a similar statement is true for
1
-morphisms and
2
-morphisms.
∎

This motivates the following definition, which we will give in the language of

\infty
-categories.

Definition 2.30
.

A labelled cospan
\infty
-category is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category

\mathcal{C}
together with a symmetric monoidal functor
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}
.
This functor is subject to the condition that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\amalg}

is a pullback square of
\infty
-categories.

We will not explore this definition in much more detail - it mainly serves
as an aesthetically more pleasing analogue of definition
2.4
.
However, let us briefly note that one can show that for any
labelled cospan
\infty
-category

(\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})
its homotopy category is a labelled cospan category

(h\mathcal{C}\to h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}\simeq\mathrm{Csp})
in the sense of definition
2.4
.
Together with lemma
2.29

this almost proves that labelled cospan categories form a full reflective subcategory
of the
\infty
-category of labelled
\infty
-categories of cospans.

Further, we suggest the following conjecture that relates the notions of labelled
cospan category and labelled
\infty
-category of cospans to previously
established notions.

Conjecture 2.31
.

There is a biequivalence between the
2
-category of labelled cospan categories
and the
2
-category of coloured properads as described in

[
HRY15
, Chapter 3]
.

Let
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\otimes}
be the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
and let
\mathcal{P}\subset(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\otimes})_{/\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}}
be the full subcategory
of the comma category on those
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp})
that satisfy the condition
of definition
2.30
.
Then
\mathcal{P}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-properads
described in
[
HRY15
, Chapter 7]
.

2.3
The decomposition theorem for cospan categories

For the purpose of this section we fix a cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})

and we assume that
B\mathcal{C}
is group-like.
In lemma
2.40
we explain this assumption is often satisfied,
and we note that in this case
B\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to an infinite loop space.
Moreover, we let
G:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
denote the set
of connected endomorphisms of the unit in
\mathcal{C}
.
We prove the following comparison result for
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
,
which is a generalisation of
[
Stb19
, Theorem B]
.

Proposition 2.32

(See
3.4
)
.

There are homotopy fiber sequences of infinite loop spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\left(\bigvee_{G}S^{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\left(\bigoplus_{G}\mathbb{N}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}).}

In particular, the map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to B(\mathcal{C})
is a rational equivalence.

The next theorem will decompose the category
\mathcal{C}
into parts
with no closed components and factorisations of closed components.
We first make the necessary definitions:

Definition 2.33
.

The simplicial set
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
is the simplicial subset
of the nerve
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}
containing only those
n
-simplices
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}

where
W(0\to n)
is a reduced morphism.

Definition 2.34
.

For any connected endomorphism of the unit
s:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}

we define the factorisation category
\mathcal{F}_{s}(\mathcal{C})
, or
\mathcal{F}_{s}
for short, as follows.
An object is a triple
(M,W,V)
where
M\not\cong 1_{\mathcal{C}}
is a non-trivial object
and
W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M
and
V:M\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
are morphisms such that
W\cup_{M}V=s
.
A morphism
(M,W,V)\to(M^{\prime},W^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is a morphism
X:M\to M^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}

such that
W\cup_{M}X=W^{\prime}
and
V=X\cup_{M^{\prime}}V^{\prime}
.

Theorem 2.35

(Decomposition Theorem, see
3.1
)
.

For any labelled cospan category
\mathcal{C}
such that
B\mathcal{C}
is group-like,
there is a homotopy fiber sequence of infinite loop spaces:

|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\longrightarrow Q\left(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})\right).

Here the wedge runs over the set of connected morphisms
g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark 2.36
.

Note that as long as each
\mathcal{F}_{g}
is non-empty the unreduced suspension

S(B\mathcal{F}_{g})
is equivalent to the reduced suspension
\Sigma(B\mathcal{F}_{g})

for any choice of base-point.

Remark 2.37
.

If
B\mathcal{C}
is not group-like, then the above is still a homotopy cofiber sequence
of
E_{\infty}
-algebras. However, this case will not be as useful for computations.
One of the particular advantages of the group-like case is that any
homotopy cofiber sequence of infinite loop spaces is also a homotopy fiber sequence,
and moreover induces a homotopy fiber sequence of underlying spaces.
In the general case we still have a homotopy fiber sequence after passing to group-completions.

Example 2.38
.

In section
3.9
we will show that for

\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Csp}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
with
d\geq 2
all the factorisation categories

\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathcal{C})
have a contractible classifying space.
In these cases it follows that
|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|\simeq B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Since
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
is not the nerve of a category it is not a priori clear
that its homotopy type is invariant under equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 2.39
.

Let
(\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
and
(\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Csp})
be two labelled cospan categories
and let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be an equivalence of symmetric monoidal categories
together with a symmetric monoidal natural isomorphism
F\circ\pi\cong\pi^{\prime}
.
Then
F
induces an equivalence:

N_{\bullet}F:N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{D}.

Proof.

Pick a homotopy-inverse functor
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
and natural isomorphisms

\alpha:F\circ G\cong\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\beta:G\circ F\cong\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Then we have maps
BF:B\mathcal{C}\longleftrightarrow B\mathcal{D}:BG

and
\alpha
and
\beta
yield homotopies that witness that
BF
and
BG
are homotopy inverse.
This homotopy equivalence restricts to a homotopy equivalence

|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{D}|
.
To see this, note that if
a:[n]\to\mathcal{C}
represents an
n
-simplex
a\in N_{n}\mathcal{C}

that lies in
N_{n}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
and
a^{\prime}:[n]\to\mathcal{C}
is naturally isomorphic to
a
,
then
a^{\prime}
lies in the no-closed components part as well:
a^{\prime}\in N_{n}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
.
∎

We briefly discuss some sufficient conditions for
B\mathcal{C}
to be group-like.

Lemma 2.40
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
be a labelled cospan category satisfying that
for all connected
M\in\mathcal{C}
there is some object
N\in\mathcal{C}
for which
there exists a morphism
W:M\otimes N\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
or a morphism

V:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M\otimes N
.
Then
\pi_{0}B\mathcal{C}
is group-like and hence
B\mathcal{C}
is equivalent
to an infinite loop space.

Proof.

The connected components of the classifying space
\pi_{0}B(\mathcal{C})

can be computed as the commutative monoid of isomorphism classes
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
modulo the relation
A\sim B
whenever
there is a morphism from
A
to
B
.
By axiom (i) of definition
2.4
every object of
\mathcal{C}

can be written as a
\otimes
-product of connected objects.
So the connected objects generate
\pi_{0}B(\mathcal{C})
.
The conditions of this lemma imply that for each connected object
M

there is some object
N
with
[M]\otimes[N]=[1_{\mathcal{C}}]
in
\pi_{0}B(\mathcal{C})
.
This implies that all the connected objects have inverses and
as these generate the monoid we can conclude that
\pi_{0}B(\mathcal{C})

is indeed a group under
\otimes
.
It now follows from the methods of
[
Seg74
]
,
which we recall in section
3.5
,
that
B\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to an infinite loop space.
∎

2.4
The surgery theorem for cospan categories

In this section we try to understand the realisation of the simplicial set

N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
, which is one of the two main components in the decomposition theorem.
We will show that under favorable circumstances it is equivalent
to the classifying space of the positive boundary subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\subset\mathcal{C}
,
which will often be computable.

Definition 2.41
.

For a labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
we define the

positive boundary category

\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}
as the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}

that contains all objects, but only those morphisms
W:M\to N

for which
\pi(N)\to\pi(W)
is surjective.

Definition 2.42
.

We say that a labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})

admits surgery

if one can pick the following data:

•

A connected object
O\in\mathcal{C}
and a connected morphism
T:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to O
.

•

For any connected object
A\in\mathcal{C}
a connected morphism
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A
.

subject to the condition that for any two connected objects
A,B\in\mathcal{C}

and any two arbitrary objects
M,N\in\mathcal{C}

the following diagrams commute for all connected morphisms

U:A\otimes M\to B\otimes N
,

V:A\otimes B\otimes M\to N
, and

W:M\to A\otimes B\otimes N
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes B\otimes N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A\otimes B\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A\otimes B\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{O,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes M})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes P_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes V}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B\otimes N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B\otimes N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A\otimes B\otimes N.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{A,O}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes N})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes P_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B\otimes N}}

See figure
3
for an illustration of these three equations.

Remark 2.43
.

Note that the morphism
T
is not required to satisfy any equation.
(In fact, we do not even need it to be connected.)
Nevertheless, it is crucial that there exists a morphism from
1_{\mathcal{C}}
to
O

as we use it to introduce new connected components during the surgery.
This would for example be impossible in the negative boundary category

\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{-}}=(\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{+}})^{\rm op}
.
This category cannot satisfy the conclusion of the surgery theorem as

\pi_{0}B((\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{-}})^{\partial_{+}})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{+}})|=\pi_{0}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{+}})\cong\Omega_{d-1}^{\rm or}

is not injective because in
(\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{\partial_{-}})^{\partial_{+}}

there are no morphisms to or from the empty set.

Figure 3:
The three conditions that the morphism
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A
has to satisfy
for all connected objects
B
, arbitrary objects
M
,
N
, and connected morphisms

U
,
V
,
W
.

Theorem 2.44

(Surgery Theorem, see
4.1
)
.

If the labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
admits surgery,
then the inclusion of simplicial sets
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\subset N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}

induces an equivalence:

B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|.

Combining this with the decomposition theorem we have the following immediate consequence:

Corollary 2.45

(Theorem
F
)
.

If the labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
admits surgery
and
B(\mathcal{C})
is group-like, then there is a homotopy fiber sequence
of infinite loop spaces.

B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\longrightarrow Q\left(\bigvee\nolimits_{g\in G}S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})\right).

The condition of admitting surgery might seem unreasonably strong
if one thinks about a general labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
.
We are essentially requiring that
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A

commutes with all other operations.
However, it turns out to satisfied in the cases we are interested in.

Example 2.46
.

We will now show that many geometric examples admit surgery.
Let, for example,
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
be the
d
-dimensional cobordism category
for
d\geq 2
. Then we can let
O:=S^{d-1}
