be the sphere and

T:=D^{d}:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}
the disk.
For the morphism
P_{M}:M\to S^{d-1}\amalg M
we can choose the
connected sum of the cobordisms
D^{d}:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}

and
M\times[0,1]:M\to M
.
Since both cobordisms are oriented and connected it does not matter at which
point we take the connected sum.
We need to make sure that this makes the relevant diagrams commute.
For example, for all connected morphisms
W:A\amalg M\to B\amalg N

one checks that both ways of going around the diagram
can be described as the connected sum of
W
with the cobordism

D^{d-1}:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}
. Since
W
is connected any
two such connected sums are diffeomorphic.

We can also replace the morphism
T:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}
by
any other connected manifold with boundary
S^{d-1}
.
In particular for
d=2
we can choose a genus
g\geq 1
surface
with one boundary component, which implies that
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}

also admits surgery.

Example 2.47
.

As similar argument as above also applies to the weighted cospan categories

\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
from definition
2.13
.
One can choose
O:=*
to be the singleton,
T:\emptyset\to*

the cospan
(\emptyset\to*=*)
weighted by any element of
A_{1}
(for example
\alpha
),
and
P:*\to*\amalg*
the cospan
(*\to*\leftarrow*\amalg*)

weighted by the unit
0\in A
.
This makes all the relevant diagrams commute as the weighting monoid
A

is commutative.

By lemma
2.14
, example
2.17
, and example
2.18

this means that all of the following labelled cospan categories admit surgery:

\mathrm{Csp},\quad\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}},\quad\mathrm{Cob}_{d}^{S^{d-1}},\quad\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{g<\gamma},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\chi\leq 0,g<\gamma}.

Example 2.48
.

If a labelled cospan category
\mathcal{C}
admits surgery,
has a group-complete classifying space
B\mathcal{C}
,
and satisfies that
\mathcal{F}_{g}(\mathcal{C})
is contractible for all
g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
,
then the inclusion of the positive boundary subcategory induces an equivalence:

B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})).

We will show that this is the case for
\mathrm{Csp}
and
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
with
d\geq 2

in section
3.9
. This implies Theorem
H

and together with the computations
B\mathrm{Csp}^{\partial_{+}}\simeq*

and
B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}\simeq S^{1}
made in proposition
4.27

it implies Theorem
G
and Theorem
B
.

3
The decomposition theorem

In this section we decompose the classifying space of a labelled cospan category

(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
into various smaller parts that are usually easier to compute.
We will also show that the two variants
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}

are closely related to
\mathcal{C}
.
Concretely we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1

(Decomposition theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
be a labelled cospan category such that
B\mathcal{C}

is group-like and let
G=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})
be the set of
connected endomorphisms of the unit.
Then there is a homotopy fiber-sequence of infinite loop spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}(\mathcal{C})|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\left(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})\right)}

Remark 3.2
.

Recall that we in
2.33

defined
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}
as the simplicial subset
on those
n
-simplices
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}
such that
W(0\to n)
has no closed components.

The notation
S^{2}X
denotes the unreduced double-suspension of an unpointed space.
(See
3.40
.)
If we assume that each
\mathcal{F}_{g}
is non-empty, then we may choose any
basepoint to form the reduced suspension and the result will
be equivalent to the unreduced suspension:
S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})\simeq\Sigma^{2}B\mathcal{F}_{g}
.
However, it can happen that a connected endomorphism
g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}

does not admit a factorisation. (For example if one adds spheres to
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
.)
In this case
B\mathcal{F}_{g}=\emptyset
and by convention
S(\emptyset)=S^{0}
,
so
S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})=S^{1}
.

Remark 3.3
.

One might think of the term
Q(\bigvee_{g\in G}\Sigma S(B\mathcal{F}_{g}))

as an “error-term” that obstructs
B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
from being equivalent
to the subspace with no closed components.
In the analogy where
\mathcal{C}
is a topologically enriched cobordism category
this term always vanishes: it is a theorem by
[
GRW10
, Definition 4.3 and lemma 4.7]

that in these cases
B\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the subspace with no closed components.
Note however, that this is only an analogy as our setting of labelled cospan categories
does not actually accommodate for topologically enriched cobordism categories,
and moreover the definition of
D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
is different in the topological case.

We also prove the following comparison result for
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
:

Proposition 3.4
.

There are homotopy fiber sequences of infinite loop spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\left(\bigvee_{G}S^{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\left(\bigoplus_{G}\mathbb{N}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}).}

In particular the map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to B(\mathcal{C})
is a rational equivalence.

3.1
Partial commutative monoidal categories

Throughout this paper we will work with infinite loop spaces that arise as
the classifying space of a symmetric monoidal categories.
The symmetric monodial categories that we consider will often be like
embedded cobordism categories in the sense that we can make sense of a notion
of disjointness
M\cap N=\emptyset
for objects
M,N
and that
for disjoint objects taking their union
(M,N)\mapsto M\cup N

is a strictly associative and commutative operation.
To extract a symmetric monoidal structure from this we need to fix a
functorial replacement
(M,N)\mapsto(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
such that
M^{\prime}\cap N^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
This choice is always somewhat arbitrary, which would make it difficult
to construct the simplicial objects in symmetric monoidal categories that we need later.
Instead of making a choice of a replacement we will work with the notion
of a partial commutative monoidal category, which formalises the idea
of having a notion of disjointness and disjoint union.
These partial commutative monoidal categories directly yield
\Gamma
-categories
(and hence special
\Gamma
-spaces) in the sense of Segal,
as we shall see below.

Definition 3.5
.

A
partial commutative monoidal
structure
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a choice of a relation
\bot
on the set of objects of
\mathcal{C}
,
an object
\emptyset\in\mathcal{C}
, and a functor
\amalg:\mathcal{C}^{2\bot}\to\mathcal{C}

where
\mathcal{C}^{2\bot}\subset\mathcal{C}^{2}
is the full subcategory on those
(x,y)
where
x\bot y
.
This data is subject to the following axioms:

(i)

For all
x\in\mathcal{C}
we have
\emptyset\bot x
and the functor

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
defined by
x\mapsto\emptyset\amalg x
is the identity.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{C}^{3\bot}\subset\mathcal{C}^{3}
denote the full subcategory on triples
(x,y,z)

satisfying
x\bot y
,
x\bot z
, and
y\bot z
.
Then for each such triple we have
x\bot(y\amalg z)
and
(x\amalg y)\bot z
, and
the two functors
\mathcal{C}^{3\bot}\to\mathcal{C}
defined by
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x\amalg y)\amalg z

and
(x,y,z)\mapsto x\amalg(y\amalg z)
are equal.

(iii)

The relation
\bot
is symmetric and the two functors
\mathcal{C}^{2\bot}\to\mathcal{C}

defined by
(x,y)\mapsto x\amalg y
and
(x,y)\mapsto y\amalg x
are equal.

(iv)

For any
n
-tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{C}^{n}
one can find
(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{n}

such that
x_{i}\cong x_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
and
x_{i}^{\prime}\bot x_{j}^{\prime}
for all
i\neq j
.

Given two such partial commutative monoidal categories
- or PCM categories for short -

(\mathcal{C},\bot_{\mathcal{C}},\emptyset_{\mathcal{C}},\amalg_{\mathcal{C}})
and

(\mathcal{D},\bot_{\mathcal{D}},\emptyset_{\mathcal{D}},\amalg_{\mathcal{D}})
,
we say that a strictly symmetric monoidal functor between them is a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

satisfying the properties:

F(\emptyset_{\mathcal{C}})=\emptyset_{\mathcal{D}}
,
x\bot y\Rightarrow F(x)\bot F(y)
,
and
\amalg_{\mathcal{D}}\circ(F,F)=F\circ\amalg_{\mathcal{C}}
as functors
\mathcal{C}^{2\bot}\to\mathcal{D}
.

Remark 3.6
.

One can think of a given PCM category as a category internal to the category
of partial commutative monoids. Conversely, a category
\mathcal{C}
internal
to partial commutative monoids represents a PCM category if it satisfies
the following conditions:

•

For any two morphisms
f:x\to y
and
g:x^{\prime}\to y^{\prime}
we have that

f\bot g
if and only if
x\bot x^{\prime}
and
y\bot y^{\prime}
.

•

For any
n
-tuples of objects
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

one can find
(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{n}

such that
x_{i}\cong x_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
and
x_{i}^{\prime}\bot x_{j}^{\prime}
for all
i\neq j
.

Remark 3.7
.

The key advantage of this definition is that it is a property for
a functor
F
to be commutatively monoidal. This will make
it much easier to construct simplicial objects in
\mathit{PCMC}

than it would be to construct them in the category of symmetric monoidal
categories.

To obtain infinite loop spaces from PCM categories we will use Segal’s
\Gamma
-spaces,
which we now recall.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\Gamma^{\rm op}
denote the full subcategory of the category of finite pointed
sets
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
on the objects
\langle n\rangle=\{*,1,\dots,n\}
.
Concretely, the morphisms
\langle n\rangle\to\langle m\rangle
in
\Gamma^{\rm op}

are maps
f:\{*,1,\dots,n\}\to\{*,1,\dots,m\}
satisfying
f(*)=*
.
We let
\rho_{n}^{i}:\langle n\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
denote the unique morphism with

(\rho_{n}^{i})^{-1}(1)=\{i\}
.

A
\Gamma
-space is a functor
X:\Gamma^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}
and we denote
the value on
\langle n\rangle
by
X^{\langle n\rangle}
. We say that
X

is a
special

\Gamma
-space if for each
n
the following map
is a weak equivalence:

(\rho_{n}^{1},\dots,\rho_{n}^{n}):X^{\langle n\rangle}\to X^{\langle 1\rangle}\times\dots\times X^{\langle 1\rangle}.

Our main source of special
\Gamma
-spaces will be of the form
B\mathcal{C}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}

for
\mathcal{C}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}
is a special
\Gamma
-category.

Definition 3.9
.

A
\Gamma
-category is a functor
\mathcal{C}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}:\Gamma^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Cat}
.
We say that
\mathcal{C}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}
is a
special
if for each
n
the map

(\rho_{n}^{1},\dots,\rho_{n}^{n}):\mathcal{C}^{\langle n\rangle}\to\mathcal{C}^{\langle 1\rangle}\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}^{\langle 1\rangle}

is an equivalence of categories.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any partial commutative monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\bot,\emptyset,\amalg)

we can define a special
\Gamma
-category be letting
\mathcal{C}^{\langle n\rangle}:=\mathcal{C}^{n\bot}

be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{n}
on those tuples
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
satisfying

x_{i}\bot x_{j}
for all
i\neq j
.

Proof.

It follows from axioms (i-iii) that for any finite family of objects

(x_{i}\in\mathcal{C})_{i\in I}

that are disjoint in the sense that
i\neq j\Rightarrow x_{i}\bot x_{j}

we can define:

\coprod_{i\in I}x_{i}:=(\dots((x_{i_{1}}\amalg x_{i_{2}})\amalg x_{i_{3}})\dots\amalg x_{i_{n}})

and that this is independent of the enumeration
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
one chooses of
I
.
Here we set
\coprod_{i\in\emptyset}x_{i}:=\emptyset_{\mathcal{C}}
.
We may therefore define for each
\lambda:\langle n\rangle\to\langle m\rangle
a functor

\lambda_{*}:\mathcal{C}^{n\bot}\to\mathcal{C}^{m\bot},\quad(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}),\text{ where }y_{j}=\coprod_{i\in\lambda^{-1}(j)}x_{i}.

This makes
\langle n\rangle\mapsto\mathcal{C}^{n\bot}
into a
\Gamma
-category.
It remains to check that this is special, i.e. that the inclusion

\mathcal{C}^{n\bot}\to(\mathcal{C}^{1\bot})^{n}=\mathcal{C}^{n}
is an equivalence of categories.
This inclusion is fully faithful by definition and it is essentially surjective
by axiom (iv).
∎

We also briefly describe how to generalise this to the setting of
2
-categories.

Definition 3.11
.

For a
2
-category
\mathcal{C}
let
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
be the
1
-category
where objects are
1
-morphisms
f:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}

and morphisms
f\to g
are
2
-morphisms
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
.
In particular there are no morphisms between
f
and
g

unless the have the same source and the same target.

A partial commutative monoidal structure on a
2
-category
\mathcal{C}

is a partial commutative monoid structure
(\mathrm{id}_{\emptyset},\bot,\amalg)

on
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
– except that instead of condition (iv) we require:

(iv’)

For any
n
-tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{C}^{n}

one can find an
n
-tuple
(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{n}

such that
x_{i}
is equivalent to
x_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i

and
\mathrm{id}_{x_{i}^{\prime}}\bot\mathrm{id}_{x_{j}^{\prime}}
for all
i\neq j
.

(iv”)

For any
n
-tuple
(f_{1}:x_{1}\to y_{1},\dots,f_{n}:x_{n}\to y_{n})\in\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})^{n}

satisfying
x_{i}\bot x_{j}
and
y_{i}\bot y_{j}
for all
i\neq j

one can find
an
n
-tuple
(f_{1}^{\prime}:x_{1}\to y_{1},\dots,f_{n}^{\prime}:x_{n}\to y_{n})\in\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})^{n}

such that
f_{i}\cong f_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
and
f_{i}\bot f_{j}
for all
i\neq j
.

(v)

Composition defines a PCM functor

\circ:\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})\times_{\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})}\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
.

We will also write
x\bot y
for two objects
x,y\in\mathcal{C}

to mean
\mathrm{id}_{x}\bot\mathrm{id}_{y}
.

Corollary 3.12
.

For any PCM
2
-category
\mathcal{C}
the classifying space
B\mathcal{C}

naturally has the structure of a special
\Gamma
-space.

Proof.

We would like to mimic the proof of lemma
3.10
.
For all
n
let
\mathcal{C}^{\bot n}\subset\mathcal{C}^{n}
be the following sub
2
-category.
It contains an object
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
if
\mathrm{id}_{x_{i}}\bot\mathrm{id}_{x_{j}}

holds for all
i\neq j
, and it contains a
1
-morphism
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})

between two such objects if
f_{i}\bot f_{j}
holds for all
i\neq j
.
It contains all
2
-morphism between any
1
-morphism it contains.
By condition (v) this is indeed a sub
2
-category.
In the same way as in lemma
3.10
these
2
-categories
assemble into a
\Gamma
-object in
2
-categories, and
therefore
\langle n\rangle\mapsto B\mathcal{C}^{\bot n}
defines a
\Gamma
-space.

We need to show that the Segal map
B\mathcal{C}^{\bot n}\to B\mathcal{C}^{n}
is a weak equivalence.
The functor
\mathcal{C}^{\bot n}\to\mathcal{C}^{n}
is
surjective up to equivalence condition (iv’) and
by condition (iv”) it is an equivalence on
\operatorname{Hom}
-categories.
Now recall that the classifying space of a
2
-category
is defined by first taking the nerve of
\operatorname{Hom}
-categories to obtain
a simplicially enriched category and then taking the usual classifying space.
By the above, the simplicially enriched functor obtained from
the
2
-functor
\mathcal{C}^{\bot n}\to\mathcal{C}^{n}
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence,
and hence it induces an equivalence on classifying spaces.
∎

3.2
A general fiber sequence

We now introduce the abstract technique that we will use to show that certain
sequences of simplicial symmetric monoidal groupoids yield fiber sequences
of infinite loop spaces.

Theorem 3.13
.

Consider three simplicial special
\Gamma
-spaces

A_{\bullet},B_{\bullet},C_{\bullet}:\Delta^{\rm op}\times\Gamma^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}

with two simplicial
\Gamma
-maps:

A_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\ f\ }B_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\ g\ }C_{\bullet}

and a homotopy of simplicial
\Gamma
-maps
\alpha:g\circ f\simeq 1_{C}

to the unit in
C_{\bullet}
.
Assume further that for every
n
there is a
\Gamma
-map

s_{n}:B_{n}\to A_{n}
such that the induced maps

(s_{n},g_{n}):B_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}\to A_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}\times C_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{n}\circ f_{n}:A_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}\to A_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}

are weak equivalences of spaces.
Then the following is a homotopy cofiber sequence of special
\Gamma
-spaces:

\|A_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\ f\ }\|B_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\ g\ }\|C_{\bullet}\|

If moreover each of these spaces is group-like, then this is a homotopy fiber sequence.

Proof.

We will base this proof on the framework of
[
GGN15
]
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the
\infty
-category of spaces, which receives a functor

\mathrm{Top}\to\mathcal{S}
. The category
\operatorname{Mon}_{E_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S})\subset\operatorname{Fun}(\Gamma^{\rm op},\mathcal{S})

is defined as the full subcategory on those functors that satisfy a Segal-condition.
The homotopy category of special
\Gamma
-spaces is pre-additive,
i.e. it has a
0
-object
*
and the canonical map
x\amalg y\to x\times y

from the coproduct to the product is an isomorphism for all
x
and
y
.
This allows us to apply lemma
3.14
and conclude from
the existence of
s_{n}
that for each
n
the sequence:

A_{n}\longrightarrow B_{n}\longrightarrow C_{n}

is a homotopy cofiber sequence of special
\Gamma
-spaces.
Since homotopy colimits commute, taking the homotopy colimit over
\Delta^{\rm op}

yields the following cofiber sequence in
\operatorname{Mon}_{E_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S})
:

\mathrm{hocolim}_{\Delta^{\rm op}}A_{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathrm{hocolim}_{\Delta^{\rm op}}B_{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathrm{hocolim}_{\Delta^{\rm op}}C_{\bullet}.

To compute this homotopy colimit note that the functor

\operatorname{Mon}_{E_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{S}
that sends a special
\Gamma
-space
A

to the underlying space
A^{\langle 1\rangle}
is conservative and commutes with sifted colimits.
Therefore

(\mathrm{hocolim}_{\Delta^{\rm op}}A_{\bullet})^{\langle 1\rangle}\simeq\mathrm{hocolim}_{\Delta^{\rm op}}A_{\bullet}^{\langle 1\rangle}

where the latter is computed as a homotopy colimit in
\mathcal{S}
.
Given a simplicial topological space
X_{\bullet}:\Delta^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}

the homotopy colimit of
X_{\bullet}:\Delta^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}\to\mathcal{S}
is modelled
by the fat geometric realisation
\|X_{\bullet}\|
.
Similarly, for a simplicial special
\Gamma
-space

A_{\bullet}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}:\Delta^{\rm op}\times\Gamma^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}

we can construct a
\Gamma
-space
\langle n\rangle\mapsto\|A_{\bullet}^{\langle n\rangle}\|

by taking the level-wise realisation.
Note that this
\Gamma
-space is still special since
\|\underline{\ \ }\|
commutes with products
up to weak equivalence (see
[
Seg74
, Proposition A.1(iii)]
or
[
ERW19
, Theorem 7.2]
).
It follows by our previous comments that
\|A_{\bullet}\|
is in fact
a model for the homotopy colimit in special
\Gamma
-spaces.
This shows that

\|A_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\ f\ }\|B_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\ g\ }\|C_{\bullet}\|

is a homotopy cofiber sequence in
\operatorname{Mon}_{E_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S})
.

Finally we would like to show that
\|A_{\bullet}\|\to\|B_{\bullet}\|\to\|C_{\bullet}\|

is homotopy fiber sequence in
\mathcal{S}
if we assume that each of the spaces is group-like.
Indeed, for any cofiber sequence
X\to Y\to Z
in
\operatorname{Mon}_{E_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S})
,
the group completion
X^{gp}\to Y^{gp}\to Z^{gp}
is a fiber sequence.
If we assume that each of
\|A_{\bullet}\|
,
\|B_{\bullet}\|
, and
\|C_{\bullet}\|
is already group-like,
then it follows that
\|A_{\bullet}\|\to\|B_{\bullet}\|\to\|C_{\bullet}\|
is a homotopy fiber sequence.
∎

The following lemma holds in any pre-additive
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
,
i.e. in every
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
such that the homotopy category
h\mathcal{C}

has a
0
-object and such that coproducts and products agree via the canonical map.
The same argument applies in any model category whose homotopy category is pre-additive.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
be two morphisms in a pre-additive

\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
such that
g\circ f
is null-homotopic.
Assume further that there is a map
s:B\to A
such that the maps

(s,g):B\to A\times C
and
s\circ f:A\to A
are equivalences.
Then any choice of a null-homotopy for
g\circ f
makes
A\to B\to C

into a cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Pick a morphism
r:C\to B
such that
s\circ r=0:C\to A
and
g\circ r=\mathrm{id}_{C}
.
This can be done by lifting
(0,\mathrm{id}_{C}):C\to A\times C

against the equivalence
(s,g):B\to A\times C
.
We claim that the maps
f
and
r
exhibit
B
as a coproduct of
A
and
C
.
So we need to check that
f\amalg r:A\amalg C\to B
is an equivalence.
Since
(s,g)
is an equivalence it will suffice to check that
(s,g)\circ(f\amalg r)

is an equivalence. Using that
h\mathcal{C}
is pre-additive we can represent this morphism
by a
2\times 2
matrix with entries
s\circ f
,
s\circ r
,
g\circ f
,
and
g\circ r
.
By construction
s\circ r
and
g\circ f
are
0
-maps and
s\circ f

and
g\circ r
are equivalences. Hence
(s,g)\circ(f\amalg r)
is the direct
sum of two equivalences and therefore itself an equivalence.

Consider the following diagram in the homotopy category
h\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

This diagram can be lifted to a diagram
\Delta^{2}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{C}
in the

\infty
-category by choosing any null-homotopy of
g\circ f
in the right square
and the trivial homotopy in the left square.
The left-hand square is a pushout square because we checked that
f
and
r

exhibit
B
as the coproduct of
A
and
C
.
The entire rectangle is a pushout square (independent of which homotopies we chose!)
because the top horizontal arrow
*\to*
and the bottom horizontal arrow

g\circ r:C\to C
both are equivalences.
It therefore follows from the pushout pasting lemma
[
Lur09
, Lemma 4.4.2.1.]

that the right-hand square
is a pushout square, which is exactly what we claimed.
∎

3.3
A base-change theorem for simplicial spaces

We prove a theorem that allows us to change the space of
0
-simplices
X_{0}

of a simplicial space
X_{\bullet}
without affecting the homotopy type of
\|X_{\bullet}\|
.
This is motivated by the basic idea in category theory that
one can change the number of objects of each isomorphism type without
changing the category, up to equivalence.
In this sense, the following definition can be thought of as a generalisation
of the notion of a fully faithful functor:

Definition 3.15
.

For any semi-simplicial object
X_{\bullet}
let let
\partial_{n}^{X}:X_{n}\to(X_{0})^{n+1}

denote the map that sends an
n
-simplex to its vertices.
We call a map of semi-simplicial spaces
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}

a
homotopy base-change
if the map

(\partial_{n}^{X},f_{n}):X_{n}\longrightarrow(X_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(Y_{0})^{n+1}}^{h}Y_{n}

is an equivalence for all
n\geq 1
.

The following theorem generalises
[
ERW19
, Theorem 5.2]

and
[
Ste21
, Lemma 2.30]
to arbitrary simplicial spaces
that are not necessarily the nerve of a topological category.
Note, however, that
[
ERW19
, Theorem 5.2]
has the advantage
of applying to weakly unital categories
\mathcal{C}
,
whereas we would need
\mathcal{C}
to have strict units in order
to apply the theorem to
N\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 3.16

(Base-change)
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a map of simplicial spaces
that is a homotopy base-change in the above sense
and satisfies that
\pi_{0}(f_{0}):\pi_{0}(X_{0})\to\pi_{0}(Y_{0})
is surjective.
Then
\|f\|:\|X_{\bullet}\|\to\|Y_{\bullet}\|
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

For any space
A
let
T(A)_{\bullet}
be the simplicial space defined by

T(A)_{n}:=A^{n+1}
with face and degeneracy operators given by forgetting and repeating.
A simplicial map
Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet}
amounts to the same data as a map
Z_{0}\to B
.
We can factor the map
X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
in the theorem as:

X_{\bullet}\longrightarrow T(X_{0})_{\bullet}\times_{T(Y_{0})_{\bullet}}^{h}Y_{\bullet}\longrightarrow Y_{\bullet}

The first map is a level-wise equivalence because
X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is a base-change,
and in particular it induces an equivalence on geometric realisations.
To prove the theorem we need to show that the second map also induces
and equivalence on geometric realisations.

For the purpose of this proof let us interpret “space” to mean
an object of the category of simplicial sets
\mathcal{S}:=\mathrm{sSet}_{Q}
,
which we equip with the Quillen model structure.
A simplicial space is hence a bisimplicial set and we equip

\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}:=\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\rm op},\mathrm{sSet}_{Q})

with the projective model structure.
This proof could also be translated into any other model,
or it could be given in the
\infty
-category of simplicial spaces.
3
3

3

Let us briefly comment on how to this proof would go in
\infty
-land.
Claim (i) is proven in the same way. Claim (ii) uses the
fact that the presheaf category (of
\Delta
) is generated by
representables under small colimits, see
[
Lur09
, Corollary 5.1.5.8]
.
Claim (iii) follows because in
\mathcal{S}
colimits are stable under pullback:

[
Lur09
, Lemma 6.1.3.14]
.

Since the statement of the theorem is invariant under level-wise
weak equivalences we may assume that
X_{0}\to Y_{0}
is a fibration,
and in this case we may replace the homotopy pullback by the strict pullback.

In fact, we will show:
for any surjective fibration of spaces (i.e. Kan-fibration of simplicial sets)

f:A\to B

and for any simplicial space
Z_{\bullet}
with map
p:Z_{0}\to B
the natural map

\alpha_{(Z_{\bullet},p)}:\|T(A)_{\bullet}\times_{T(B)_{\bullet}}Z_{\bullet}\|\longrightarrow\|Z_{\bullet}\|

is an equivalence.
This map defines a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}\to\mathcal{S}
.
We will prove the theorem by showing:

(i)

The map
\alpha_{(Z_{\bullet},p)}
is an equivalence if
Z_{\bullet}

is the discrete simplicial space
\Delta^{n}
for some
n
.

(ii)

The category
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}
is generated
under homotopy colimits and weak equivalences
by objects of the form
(\Delta^{n},p)
.

(iii)

The source and target functor of
\alpha
both commute with homotopy colimits.

Claim (i):
For
Z_{\bullet}=\Delta^{n}
the map
p:Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet}
picks out
(n+1)
points

p(0),\dots,p(n)\in B
.
Since
f:A\to B
is surjective we can find
a\in A
with
f(a)=p(0)
,
which we can use to define an extra degeneracy:

\displaystyle s_{-1}:A^{k+1}\times_{B^{k+1}}(\Delta^{n})_{k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow A^{k+2}\times_{B^{k+2}}(\Delta^{n})_{k+1}

\displaystyle((a_{0},\dots,a_{n}),(l_{0},\dots,l_{n}))

\displaystyle\longmapsto((a,a_{0},\dots,a_{n}),(0,l_{0},\dots,l_{n}))

Hence
\|T(A)_{\bullet}\times_{T(B)_{\bullet}}\Delta^{n}\|
is contractible and
since
\|\Delta^{n}\|
is also contractible it follows that
\alpha_{(\Delta^{n},p)}
is an equvialence.

Claim (ii):
Let
(p:Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet})\in\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}
be any object
in the over category.
The category
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}
is generated
by the representables
\Delta^{n}
under homotopy colimits.
Concretely,
[
Dug01
, Proposition 2.9]
shows that
we can find a diagram
F:I\to\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}

such that each
F(i)
is level-wise equivalent to some
\Delta^{n_{i}}
,
the colimit is
\colim_{i\in I}F(i)\cong Z_{\bullet}
,
and the map from the homotopy colimit

\hocolim_{i\in I}F(i)\to Z_{\bullet}
is a weak equivalence.

Using the map
p:Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet}
we can lift
F

to a diagram
F:I\to\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}
.
The homotopy colimit of this diagram is

\hocolim_{i\in I}(F(i)\to T(B)_{\bullet})\simeq(Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet})

since the forgetful functor
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}\to\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}

commutes with colimits and preserves weak equivalences.

Claim (iii):
The geometric realisation functor
\|\underline{\ \ }\|:\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}\to\mathcal{S}

commutes with homotopy colimits and so does the forgetful
functor
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}\to\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}
.
We need to show that the functor

\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}}_{/T(B)}\to\mathcal{S}^{\Delta^{\rm op}},\qquad(Z_{\bullet}\to T(B)_{\bullet})\mapsto(T(A)_{\bullet}\times_{T(B)_{\bullet}}Z_{\bullet})

commutes with homotopy colimits.
This functor preserves level-wise weak equivalences
(because we assumed that
A\to B
is a fibration)
so it will suffice to show that it commutes with strict colimits.
Strict colimits of bisimplicial sets (over
T(B)_{\bullet}
) can be computed point-wise,
so the claim follows from the fact that in the category of sets
the functor
(U\times_{V}\underline{\ \ }):\mathrm{Set}_{/V}\to\mathrm{Set}

commutes with colimits.
(This functor has a right-adjoint given by
W\mapsto\coprod_{v\in V}\mathrm{Map}(U\times_{V}\{v\},W)
.)
∎

Remark 3.17
.

Note it is crucial for theorem
3.16
and
corollary
3.20
that
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}

are simplicial spaces.
The analogous statements for semi-simplicial spaces are generally not true.
Consider for example the semi-simplicial space
Y_{\bullet}

with
Y_{0}=*
and
Y_{i}=\emptyset
for
i>0
.
The semi-simplicial map
Y_{\bullet}\amalg Y_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is a base-change
and it is surjective on
Y_{0}
, but on geometric realisations it is

*\amalg*\to*
.

To see how this is used in the proof note that in the above counter-example

Y_{\bullet}=\Delta_{s}^{0}
is the semisimplicial
0
-simplex,
i.e. the representable object on
[0]\in\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}}
.
This means that already the analogue of claim (i), where we show the theorem
for the case that
Z_{\bullet}
is representable, fails.
Of course one could still prove claim (i) for
\Delta^{n}

thought of as a semisimplicial space, but the
\Delta^{n}
do not generate

\mathcal{S}^{\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}}
under homotopy-colimits.

Remark 3.18
.

The theorem also holds with the fat geometric realisations
\|X_{\bullet}\|

replaced by the standard one
|X_{\bullet}|
, as long as both simplicial spaces

X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
have the property that the quotient maps
\|X_{\bullet}\|\to|X_{\bullet}|

and
\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to|Y_{\bullet}|
are weak equivalences.
This is the case if
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
are
good

[
Seg74
, Proposition A.1.(iv)]
,
which is the case if they are the level-wise realisation of a bi-simplicial set.
As we will basically only be encountering simplicial spaces
defined as the level-wise classifying space of a simplicial groupoid,
this will always be the case.

We now state a special case of the theorem where
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
are both
obtained as level-wise classifying spaces of simplicial groupoids.
To do so we quickly recall the following definition:

Definition 3.19
.

A functor
P:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{B}
is an
iso-fibration
if for any object

e\in\mathcal{E}
and isomorphism
f:P(e)\cong b
there is an isomorphism

g:e\to\overline{b}
in
\mathcal{E}
such that
P(\overline{b})=b
and
P(g)=f
.

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
F_{\bullet}:\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\bullet}
be a functor between
two simplicial groupoids such that

•

F_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\to\mathcal{Y}_{0}
is essentially surjective,

•

(\partial_{n}^{\mathcal{X}},F_{n}):\mathcal{X}_{n}\to(\mathcal{X}_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(\mathcal{Y}_{0})^{n+1}}\mathcal{Y}_{n}

is an equivalence of categories, and

•

For all
n
at least one of the functors

\partial_{n}^{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{Y}_{n}\to(\mathcal{Y}_{0})^{n+1}

and
F_{n}:\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{Y}_{n}
is an iso-fibration.

Then
|BF|:|B\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}|\to|B\mathcal{Y}_{\bullet}|
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The first condition implies the surjectivity of
\pi_{0}(BF_{0}):B\mathcal{X}_{0}\to B\mathcal{Y}_{0}
.
The second condition implies that
BF_{\bullet}
is a homotopy base-change,
if we can show that the map

B\left((\mathcal{X}_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(\mathcal{Y}_{0})^{n+1}}\mathcal{Y}_{n}\right)\longrightarrow(B\mathcal{X}_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(B\mathcal{Y}_{0})^{n+1}}^{h}B\mathcal{Y}_{n}

is an equivalence. By the third condition at least one of the functors
involved in the pullback is an iso-fibration.
Taking the pullback of classifying spaces along an iso-fibration of groupoids
always yields the homotopy pullback - this can be shown by elementary means
or by noting that iso-fibrations of groupoids are both Cartesian
and coCartesian, so that
[
Stm17
, Theorem 2.3]
may be applied.
∎

To illustrate the use of the base-change theorem we quickly prove that
the realisation of Rezk’s relative nerve is always equivalent to
the classifying space of the category.
This will be useful later. First recall:

Definition 3.21
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
its
relative nerve
4
4

4

This is what Rezk calls the classifying diagram of the category,
see
[
Rez01
, Section 3.5]
.

is the simplicial groupoid

N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
defined for each
n
as the groupoid of functors

[n]\to\mathcal{C}
with morphisms being natural isomorphisms.
This contains the usual nerve as a simplicial subgroupoid

N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}\subset N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
with only identity morphisms.

Lemma 3.22
.

The inclusion
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}\subset N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
always induces
an equivalence:

B\mathcal{C}\simeq\|N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}\|\simeq\|B(N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})\|.

Proof.

We need to verify the conditions of the base-change theorem for simplicial groupoids

3.20
. The functor
N_{0}\mathcal{C}\to N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

is a bijection on objects and in particular essentially surjective.
For any
n
the functor

N_{n}\mathcal{C}\to(N_{0}\mathcal{C})^{n+1}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})^{n+1}}N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

is an isomorphism of groupoids, as both sides can be identified with the
subcategory of
N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
containing only identity morphisms.
Finally, we claim that
\partial_{n}:N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}\to(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})^{n+1}

is an iso-fibration for all
n
. Indeed, given a functor
X:[n]\to\mathcal{C}

and isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}:X(i)\cong X^{\prime}(i)
there is a (unique) functor
X^{\prime}:[n]\to\mathcal{C}

extending the
X^{\prime}(i)
such that
\alpha:X\cong X^{\prime}
is a natural isomorphism.
(This functor
X^{\prime}
is given by
X^{\prime}(i\leq j)=\alpha_{j}\circ X(i\leq j)\circ\alpha_{i}^{-1}
.)
∎

3.4
The simplicial groupoid D(C)

Now that all the tools are in place we define the simplicial groupoid

D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
that serves as a more convenient replacement for
N\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
.
5
5

5

The name
D
is based on the topological poset
D_{\theta}^{\mathcal{C}}

from
[
GRW14
, Definition 2.13]
that is used as a convenient replacement
of the topological cobordism category
\mathcal{C}_{d}
in their work.

One can think of
D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
as Rezk’s
relative nerve
construction
applied to the
(2,1)
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
.
We will see that the realisation of
D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})

is equivalent to the classifying space of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
.
The advantage of
D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
is that for each
n
the groupoid
D_{n}(\mathcal{C})

inherits a PCM structure from
\mathcal{C}

(which would not be the case for
N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
)
and so we can think of
BD_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
as a simplicial
\Gamma
-space.

Definition 3.23
.

The simplicial groupoid
D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
is defined as follows.
An object of
D_{n}
consists of
n
composable morphisms in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
,
i.e. a datum
((A_{\bullet},c_{\bullet}),(\underline{X}_{\bullet},o_{\bullet}))
where

A_{0},\dots,A_{n}\subset\Omega
are finite sets with
labellings by connected objects
c_{i}:A_{i}\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
and
cospans
(f_{i}:A_{i-1}\to\underline{X}_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}:g_{i})
with labellings
by connected morphisms
o_{i}:\underline{X}_{i}\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
.
A morphism of
n
-simplices

((A_{\bullet},c_{\bullet}),(\underline{X}_{\bullet},o_{\bullet}))\to((A_{\bullet}^{\prime},c_{\bullet}^{\prime}),(\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},o_{\bullet}^{\prime}))

is a family of bijections
\alpha_{i}:A_{i}\cong A_{i}^{\prime}
and

\varphi_{i}:\underline{X}_{i}\cong\underline{X}_{i}^{\prime}
satisfying:

c_{i}\circ\alpha_{i}=c_{i}^{\prime},\quad o_{i}\circ\varphi_{i}=o_{i}^{\prime},\quad\varphi_{i}\circ f_{i}=f_{i}^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{i-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{i}\circ g_{i}=g_{i}^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{i}.

The face operator
d_{i}
is defined by composing the
i
th and
(i+1)
st morphism
according to the composition in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
.
The degeneracy operator
s_{i}
is defined by inserting identity morphisms.

Remark 3.24
.

In what follows we will often simply write
D_{\bullet}
for
D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
when
the labelled cospan category
(\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
is clear from the context.
In a similar vein we often denote objects of
D_{n}(\mathcal{C})
as tuples

(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})
where the labelings
c_{i}
and
o_{j}
are left implicit.
For
0\leq k<l\leq n
we also use the notation

\underline{X}_{k\to l}:=\underline{X}_{k+1}\cup_{A_{k}}\dots\cup_{A_{l-1}}\underline{X}_{l}

for the composite cospan and we write

o_{k\to l}:\underline{X}_{k\to l}\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})

for the labeling induced by composition.
Note that in this notation
\underline{X}_{k}=\underline{X}_{k-1\to k}
.

Remark 3.25
.

Note that there is an inclusion of simplicial groupoids:

N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow D_{\bullet}

which in level
n
identifies
N_{n}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
with the subcategory of
D_{n}

that contains all objects and all those morphisms
(\alpha_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})

where
\alpha_{i}=\mathrm{id}_{A_{i}}
holds for all
i
.

We can also define a variant with no closed components:

Definition 3.26
.

Let
D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\subset D_{\bullet}
be the simplicial subgroupoid defined
in each level
n
as the full subgroupoid
D_{n}^{\rm nc}\subset D_{n}

on those

(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})

such that the composite cospan

(A_{0}\to\underline{X}_{0\to n}\leftarrow A_{n})

is reduced.

Lemma 3.27
.

The inclusion of simplicial groupoids
N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})

induces equivalences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|BN_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}(\mathcal{C})|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|BN_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|BD_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

Both equivalences can be checked by applying the base-change theorem
for simplicial groupoids
3.20
.
We will verify the conditions for the bottom equivalence, the top map is similar.
Both are entirely analogous to lemma
3.22
.

To begin, note that
N_{n}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to D_{n}
is an inclusion of groupoids
that is a bijection on objects. So
N_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to D_{0}

is certainly essentially surjective.
Moreover, for all
n
both sides of

N_{n}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow(N_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))^{n+1}\times_{(D_{0})^{n+1}}D_{n}

can be thought of the subcategory of
D_{n}
that contains all objects
and all those morphisms
(\alpha_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
where
\alpha_{i}=\mathrm{id}_{A_{i}}
for all
i
.
So this functor is an isomorphism.
It remains to check that
\partial_{D}^{n}:D_{n}\to(D_{0})^{n+1}
is an isofibration.
This means that given some
n
-simplex
(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})
,
an
(n+1)
tuple
(B_{\bullet},c_{\bullet}^{\prime})
, and
(n+1)
bijections
\alpha_{i}:A_{i}\cong B_{i}

such that
c_{i}^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{i}=c_{i}
,
we need to find an
n
-simplex

(B_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},c_{\bullet}^{\prime},o_{\bullet}^{\prime})
that is isomorphic to the original
n
-simplex
via some isomorphism of the form
(\alpha_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
.
This can be achieved by simply setting
\underline{X}_{i}^{\prime}:=\underline{X}_{i}
and
then using the
\alpha_{i}
to define the structure maps of the cospans as

B_{i-1}\cong A_{i-1}\to\underline{X}_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}\cong B_{i}
.
The labeling
o_{\bullet}
stays the same.
∎

In the case of the no closed component space we can also compare
this to the much simpler simplicial set
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 3.28
.

The canonical projection
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}

is an equivalence on realisations.

Proof.

The functor
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
is essentially surjective, but
not necessarily surjective on the nose. Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}

be the full subcategory on those objects that are in the image,
i.e. those that are
\otimes
-products of connected objects.
The inclusion
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence
on realisations by lemma
2.39
.

We will show that
N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

satisfies the condition of corollary
3.20
.
By construction
N_{0}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to N_{0}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is surjective.
The boundary map
\partial_{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}:N_{n}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to(N_{0}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{n+1}

is an isofibration because it is a map between groupoids that
only have identity morphisms.
It remains to check that for all
n
the map

N_{n}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow(N_{0}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))^{n+1}\times_{N_{0}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

is an equivalence of groupoids.
This follows from the equivalence

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}}^{\rm red}((A,c_{A}),(B,c_{B}))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm red}(\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(A,c_{A}),\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(B,c_{B}))

shown in lemma
2.28
since every morphism
involved in
N_{n}^{\rm nc}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
is necessarily reduced.
∎

3.5
The groupoid of cuts

We now proceed to construct a simplicial groupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
that represents
the complement of
D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
in
D_{\bullet}
.
This is a generalisation of the ideas of our previous work
[
Ste21
, Part II]
,
with the key difference being that the morphisms involved in
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}

are allowed to have closed components whereas the space of cuts
in
[
Ste21
, Section 7]
only used reduced morphisms.

Definition 3.29
.

The simplicial groupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
is defined in each level
as the full subgroupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}\subset D_{n}
on those objects

(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})
where
A_{0}
and
A_{n}
are both empty.

There is a projection functor
R_{n}:D_{n}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
that discards
all elements of
A_{i}
and
\underline{X}_{i}
that correspond to an element of

\underline{X}_{0\to n}

that lies in the image of the map
A_{0}\amalg A_{n}\to\underline{X}_{0\to n}
.

We define the semi-simplicial structure on
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
by letting

\lambda:[n]\to[m]
act as

\lambda_{\mathrm{Cut}}^{*}:\mathrm{Cut}_{m}\subset D_{m}\xrightarrow{\ \lambda^{*}\ }D_{n}\xrightarrow{\ R_{n}\ }\mathrm{Cut}_{n}.

We equip each
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
with the PCM structure restricted from
D_{n}
.

Lemma 3.30
.

The above definition yields a well-defined simplicial PCM groupoid

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
such that the functors
R_{n}
induce a morphism of
simplicial PCM groupoids:
R_{\bullet}:D_{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

We need to check that the operations
\lambda_{\mathrm{Cut}}^{*}:\mathrm{Cut}_{m}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{n}

assemble into a simplicial object. Since we already know that

D_{\bullet}
is a simplicial object it will suffice to show that

R_{n}\circ\lambda^{*}\circ R_{m}=R_{n}\circ\lambda^{*}
, which will also
imply that
R_{\bullet}:D_{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
is a simplicial functor.

It will suffice to consider
\lambda=\delta^{i}:[n]\to[n+1]
, the unique
injective morphism that does not hit
i
.
(The degeneracy operators preserve the subgroupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}\subset D_{n}
.)
Then
\lambda^{*}=d_{i}
is the
i
-th face map, which composes the two adjacent morphisms
if
0<i<n
, forgets the first morphism if
i=0
and the last morphism
if
i=n
.
In the case
0<i<n
the total composite

\underline{X}_{0\to n}

stays the same before and after applying
d_{i}
.
Therefore deleting anything that corresponds to an element
of the image of

A_{0}\amalg A_{n}\to\underline{X}_{0\to n}

does the same whether we do it before or after applying
d_{i}
.
In formulas this means
\lambda^{*}\circ R_{n-1}=R_{n}\circ\lambda^{*}
.
Since
R_{n}
is idempotent when thought of as an endofunctor of
D_{n}
,
this in particular implies
R_{n-1}\circ\lambda^{*}\circ R_{n}=R_{n-1}\circ\lambda^{*}
,
which is what we claimed.

We now check the case
i=0
, the other remaining case
i=n
is similar.
When applying
R_{n-1}\circ d_{0}\circ R_{n}
to an
n
-simplex
we first delete anything in the image of

A_{0}\amalg A_{n}\to\underline{X}_{0\to n}
,
then we forget the first cospan, and then we delete anything in the image of

A_{1}\amalg A_{n}\to\underline{X}_{1\to n}
.
It follows from inspection that anything we delete in the first step
because it lies in the image of
A_{0}
will either be in the
first cospan or lie in the image of
A_{1}
later, so it will be deleted anyway.
This implies that the first
R_{n}
was redundant and so we have

R_{n-1}\circ d_{0}\circ R_{n}=R_{n-1}\circ d_{0}
.

Since
\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}
is generated by the
\delta^{i}

it follows that
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
is a well-defined simplicial object
in the category of groupoids and that
R_{\bullet}:D_{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}

is simplicial.
For the partial commutative monoidal structure
we simply note that
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}\subset D_{n}
is closed under disjoint
union and isomorphism, so the structure can be restricted.
Since we defined
R_{n}
in terms of components it commutes with
disjoint unions.
(As pointed out in remark
3.7
it is a property,
and not a structure,
for a functor between two partial commutative monoidal categories
to preserve the structure.)
It follows that
R_{\bullet}
is a morphism of simplicial PCM groupoids.
∎

We have constructed
\mathrm{Cut}
is such a way that every object
W\in D_{n}

canonically decomposes as a disjoint union
W=W^{\rm nc}\amalg W^{\mathrm{Cut}}

where
W^{\rm nc}\in D_{n}^{\rm nc}
and
W^{\mathrm{Cut}}\in\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
.
This means that we can apply the fiber sequence criterion
3.13

to show:

Lemma 3.31
.

Assume that
B\mathcal{C}
is group-like.
Then the inclusion
I:D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\subset D_{\bullet}
and the simplicial functor
R_{\bullet}

yield a fiber sequence of infinite loop spaces:

|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|\longrightarrow|BD_{\bullet}|\xrightarrow{\ R\ }|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|.

Proof.

To check the criteria of theorem
3.13
we need to
find for each
n
a PCM-functor
S_{n}:D_{n}\to D_{n}^{\rm nc}
such that the
functors
S_{n}\circ I:D_{n}^{\rm nc}\to D_{n}^{\rm nc}
and

(S_{n},R_{n}):D_{n}\to D_{n}^{\rm nc}\times\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
are equivalences of groupoids.

In analogy to the definition of
R_{n}
we let
S_{n}(W)
be defined
by deleting all components of
A_{i}
or
\underline{X_{i}}
that

do not
lie in the image of

A_{0}\amalg A_{n}\to\underline{X}_{0\to n}
.
Note that this functor does not satisfy the conditions we checked
in lemma
3.30
and so
S_{\bullet}
does not
define a semi-simplicial map
D_{\bullet}\to D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
.
This is not a problem since theorem
3.13

does not require the sections
S_{n}
to be related in any way.
The definition of
S_{n}
plays well with disjoint unions
and hence defines a PCM-functor.

The composition
S_{n}\circ I
is in fact the identity functor
\operatorname{Id}_{D_{n}^{\rm nc}}

by construction, so we only need to check that
(S_{n},R_{n})
is an equivalence.
We begin with essential surjectivity.
The image of
(S_{n},R_{n})
in
D_{n}^{\rm nc}\times\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
consists
precisely of those tuples
(\underline{X}_{\bullet},\underline{Y}_{\bullet})

such that
\underline{X}_{i}\bot\underline{Y}_{i}
for all
i
.
By standard arguments this disjointness may always be achieved,
up to isomorphism, and so
(S_{n},R_{n})
is essentially surjective.
We also need to check fully faithfulness.
Let
(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet}),(C_{\bullet},\underline{Z}_{\bullet})\in D_{n}
be any two objects.
(We suppress the data of the labelings
c_{A_{i}}
and
o_{\underline{X}_{i}}
.)
Then we need to show that isomorphism

(\alpha_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet}):(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})\cong(C_{\bullet},\underline{Z}_{\bullet})

is exactly given by a tuple of isomorphisms

S_{n}(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})\cong S_{n}(C_{\bullet},\underline{Z}_{\bullet})
and

R_{n}(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})\cong R_{n}(C_{\bullet},\underline{Z}_{\bullet})
.
But note that any such isomorphism induces compatible isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}\amalg A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}_{0\to n}={\underline{X}_{1}\cup_{A_{1}}\dots\cup_{A_{n-1}}\underline{X}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}\amalg C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Z}_{0\to n}={\underline{Z}_{1}\cup_{C_{1}}\dots\cup_{C_{n-1}}\underline{Z}_{n}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}\amalg\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

So the isomorphism
(\alpha_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
preserves the subsets
picked out by
S_{n}
and
R_{n}
, respectively, and it necessarily
restricts to an isomorphism on both of those subsets.
This shows that
(S_{n},R_{n})
is an equivalence of categories
and so we may apply the fiber sequence criterion
3.13
.

All that remains to complete the proof is to check that all three spaces are group-like.
The groupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{0}
is trivial be definition and hence
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|
is connected
and in particular group-like.
For the middle space we observe that
D_{0}\simeq N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

and
D_{1}\to N_{1}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
is essentially surjective.
Hence
\pi_{0}|BD_{\bullet}|\cong\pi_{0}B\mathcal{C}
, which we assumed to be group-like.
In the case of
D_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
it is still true that
D_{0}^{\rm nc}\simeq N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

and even though
D_{1}^{\rm nc}\to N_{1}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
is no longer essentially surjective,
it is still true that for any morphism
W:M\to N
in
\mathcal{C}
the
1
-cell

S(W)\in D_{1}^{\rm nc}
connects
M
and
N
. Hence
\pi_{0}|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|\cong\pi_{0}|B\mathcal{C}|

is group-like.
∎

3.6
Decomposing the space of cuts

So far we have seen that
|BD_{\bullet}|
fits into a fiber sequence
between
|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
and
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|
.
The purpose of this section is to show that
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|
is a free infinite loop space.
We will do so by first showing that each of the
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
is a free symmetric monoidal
groupoid on the connected cuts.

Definition 3.32
.

Let
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}\subset\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
denote the simplicial subgroupoid defined
in each level as the full subgroupoid on those

(A_{\bullet},W_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
where
\underline{X}_{0\to n}

is connected or empty.

We need to allow
\underline{X}_{0\to n}
to be empty in order for
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}

to be closed under those face maps that delete closed components.
We take
\emptyset\in\mathrm{Cut}_{0}^{\rm con}
as the base-point.

Lemma 3.33
.

The inclusion
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}\subset\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
induces an equivalence of
\Gamma
-spaces

Q(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|)\simeq|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|
.

We recall an explicit construction of the free
\Gamma
-category based on Segal’s work
[
Seg74
]
.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. Then the category
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{\langle n\rangle}
has as
objects triples
(T,\alpha,x)
where
T
is a finite set,

\alpha:T\to\{1,\dots,n\}
a map, and
x:T\to\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
an assignment
of objects that we denote by
t\mapsto x_{t}
.
Morphisms
(T,\alpha,x)\to(S,\beta,y)
are pairs of a bijection
\varphi:T\cong S

satisfying
\beta\circ\varphi=\alpha
together
with a collection of morphisms
f_{t}:x_{t}\to y_{\varphi(t)}
indexed by
t\in T
.
The composition of morphisms is given by

(\psi,g)\circ(\varphi,f)=(\psi\circ\varphi,h)
where
h_{t}=g_{\varphi(t)}\circ f_{t}
.
For a map
\lambda:\langle n\rangle\to\langle m\rangle
in
\Gamma^{\rm op}
we define a functor

\lambda_{*}:\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{\langle n\rangle}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{\langle m\rangle}
by sending
(T,\alpha,x)

to
(T^{\prime},\lambda\circ\alpha,x_{|T^{\prime}})
where
T^{\prime}=\{t\in T\;|\;\lambda(\alpha(t))\neq*\}
.

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}
be a simplicial category such that
\mathcal{A}_{0}=*
is the trivial
category and such that for all
n
the degeneracy operator
(s_{0})^{n}:\mathcal{A}_{0}\to\mathcal{A}_{n}

induces a disjoint decomposition
\mathcal{A}_{n}=(s_{0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{0})\amalg\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
.
Define a semi-simplicial
\Gamma
-category
\mathcal{D}
by setting

\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\langle m\rangle}
with the aforementioned
\Gamma
-structure
and face-maps defined by
d_{i}(T,\alpha,x):=(T^{\prime},\alpha_{|T^{\prime}},y)
where

T^{\prime}=\{t\in T\;|\;d_{i}x_{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\}
and

y_{t}:=d_{i}x_{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
.

Then the inclusion
B(\mathcal{A}_{n})\to B(\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle})
induces
an equivalence of
\Gamma
-spaces:

Q(|B\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}|)\simeq|B(\mathcal{D}_{\bullet})|.

Proof.

The proof of this is almost entirely formal.
We begin by noting that for any category
\mathcal{A}
the
\Gamma
-space

\langle n\rangle\mapsto B(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{\langle n\rangle})
is isomorphic to the
\Gamma
-space
that is denoted by
\Sigma_{X}
for
X=B\mathcal{A}
in
[
Seg74
, p. 299]
.
Let us denote by
(\underline{\ \ })^{\rm gp}
denote the construction
that sends a special
\Gamma
-space to its group completion,
which we assume to come with the data of an infinite loop space.
In particular, there is a canonical infinite loop space map
from the free infinite loop space
Q(Y^{\langle 1\rangle})=\colim\Omega^{N}\Sigma^{N}Y^{\langle 1\rangle}

to the group completion
Y^{\rm gp}
for any special
\Gamma
-space
Y^{\langle\bullet\rangle}
.

Segal shows that the spectrum associated to
\Sigma_{X}
is the suspension spectrum
of
X_{+}=X\amalg\{*\}
.
It follows that the inclusion
X\subset\Sigma_{X}^{\langle 1\rangle}
induces an equivalence:

Q(X_{+})\to Q(\Sigma_{X}^{\langle 1\rangle})\to(\Sigma_{X})^{\rm gp}

Letting
X:=B\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
we conclude that

Q((B\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime})_{+})\simeq(B\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}})^{\rm gp}=(B\mathcal{D}_{n})^{\rm gp}
for all
n
.
Note that we assumed that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is obtained by taking the disjoint union of

\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
with the trivial category
s_{0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
Hence
(B\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime})_{+}=B\mathcal{A}_{n}
and since all face maps preserve the basepoint

s_{0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{A}_{n}
the aforementioned equivalence is
in fact an equivalence of simplicial infinite loop spaces:

Q(B\mathcal{A}_{\bullet})\simeq(B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet})^{\rm gp}.

To proceed, recall that both
Q(\underline{\ \ })
and
(\underline{\ \ })^{\rm gp}
commute
with fat geometric realisations up to weak equvialence.
This follows from
[
May72
, Proposition 12.1 and Theorem 12.3]
,
see also
[
Bas+17
]
for a more detailed account for the case
of the group-completion functor.
So we have:

Q(|B\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}|)\simeq(|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|)^{\rm gp}.

The lemma now follows from the observation that

|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|\to(|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|)^{\rm gp}

is an equivalence:
we assumed that
\mathcal{A}_{0}=*
, which implies
\mathcal{D}_{0}=*
and so
|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|

is connected and in particular grouplike.
∎

Now that we have this general setting we can prove lemma
3.33

by comparing
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
to the free
\Gamma
-category on
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{\rm con}
.

Proof of lemma
3.33
.

The category of connected cuts decomposes as

\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{\rm con}=\{\emptyset\}\amalg\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{{\rm con},\neq\emptyset}

and the face operators all preserve
\emptyset
.
We can therefore construct a semi-simplicial
\Gamma
-category
\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}^{\langle\bullet\rangle}

as before by
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{{\rm con},\neq\emptyset}}^{\langle m\rangle}
.
To prove the lemma it will suffice to construct a zig-zag of semi-simplicial

\Gamma
-functors
\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
that induce equivalences

Q(|\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{lem:level-wise-free-sm-cat}}}{{\simeq}}|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|\simeq|B\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}|\simeq|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|.

For fixed
n
and
m
we let
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}\subset\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}

be the full subcategory on those
(T,\alpha,(A_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t})_{t\in T})

where the
\underline{X}_{i}^{t}\in\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{{\rm con},\neq\emptyset}

are pairwise disjoint as
t\in T
varies.
One checks that this is closed under face operators,
and also under functoriality in
\langle m\rangle\in\Gamma
.
As usual this hits all isomorphism classes and hence the inclusion

\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}
is a level-wise equivalence of simplicial
\Gamma
-categories.
In particular it realises to an equivalence
|B\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}|\simeq|B\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}|
.

The advantage of this subcategory is that the disjointness condition
allows us to define a functor
\amalg:\mathcal{E}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{\langle m\rangle}

by
(T,\alpha,(A_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t})_{t\in T})\mapsto((\coprod_{t\in T}A_{\bullet}^{t},\coprod_{t\in T}\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t}),\underline{X}_{0\to n}\to T\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle m\rangle)
.
This is well-defined because
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
is a partial commutative
monoidal category, and moreover it is natural in
n
and
\langle m\rangle
.
To complete the proof we need to check that

\amalg:\mathcal{E}_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}\to\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{\langle 1\rangle}
is an equivalence of categories.
To show essential surjectivity let
(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
be some object.
Set
T:=\underline{X}_{0\to n}
and let
B_{i}^{t}\subset A_{i}
and

\underline{Y}_{i}^{t}\subset\underline{X}_{i}
be the subsets that map to
t\in\underline{X}_{0\to n}
.
Then

\amalg(T,\alpha,(B_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{Y}_{\bullet}^{t})_{t\in T})=\coprod_{t\in\underline{X}_{0\to n}}(B_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{Y}_{\bullet}^{t})=(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet}),

so
\amalg
is surjective.
Next, we check fully faithfulness. For two objects

(T,\alpha,(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet}))
and
(S,\beta,(B_{\bullet},\underline{Y}_{\bullet}))

a morphism between them is a bijection
\varphi:T\cong S

together with isomorphisms

(\beta_{\bullet}^{t},\varphi_{\bullet}^{t}):(A_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t})\cong(B_{\bullet}^{\varphi(t)},\underline{Y}_{\bullet}^{\varphi(t)})

for all
t\in T
.
For any
t
the set
\underline{X}_{0\to n}^{t}
has a single element, so any isomorphism

(\beta_{\bullet},\psi_{\bullet}):\amalg_{t\in T}(A_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t})\cong\amalg_{s\in S}(B_{\bullet}^{s},\underline{Y}_{\bullet}^{s})

can uniquely be written as
(\varphi,(\gamma_{\bullet}^{t},\chi_{\bullet}^{t})_{t\in T})
where

\varphi:=\psi_{1\to n}:T=\underline{X}_{0\to n}\to\underline{Y}_{0\to n}=S

and
(\gamma_{\bullet}^{t},\chi_{\bullet}^{t})
is defined as the restriction

(\beta_{\bullet},\psi_{\bullet})_{|(A_{\bullet}^{t},\underline{X}_{\bullet}^{t})}
.
∎

Corollary 3.36
.

The rational homology of
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|
injects into the
rational homology of
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|
and its image freely generates

H_{*}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|;\mathbb{Q})
as a commutative algebra.

Proof.

For any connected space
X
the rational homology of
Q(X)
is a free
symmetric algebra on the rational homology
X
.
The zig-zag of equivalences

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|\simeq\dots\simeq Q(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|)

constructed in lemma
3.33
is compatible
with the
\Gamma
-space structure and hence induces an algebra isomorphism
on homology.
Moreover, this zig-zag is compatible with the natural map from

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|
.
∎

3.7
From cuts to factorisations

In this section we compute the realisation of the space of connected cuts in terms
of the category of factorisations.
We will denote the set of connected endomorphism of
1_{\mathcal{C}}
by:

G:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm con}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}}).

Definition 3.37
.

Define a simplicial groupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}
as follows.
For each
n
let
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}
be the full subgroupoid of
\mathrm{Cut}_{n+2}^{\rm con}
on those

(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet})\in D_{n+2}
such that
A_{1}
and
A_{n+1}
are non-empty.
(Recall that
A_{0}
and
A_{n+2}
are already required to be empty
and
\underline{X}_{0\to n+1}
is required to be connected or empty.)
The
i
th face map on
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}
is defined as the restriction of
the
(i+1)
st face map on
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
.
There is an augmentation
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\to G

defined by sending any non-empty
(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})

to the label
o_{1\to n+1}(x)\in\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})

of
\{x\}=\underline{X}_{0\to n+1}
.

Definition 3.38
.

For a map of spaces
f:X\to Y
we will use the following coordinates
on the cone of its suspension:

\Sigma\mathrm{cone}(f)=\left(*\amalg\{(x,a,b)\in X\times[0,1]^{2}\;|\;a+b\leq 1\}\amalg\{(y,a,b)\in Y\times[0,1]^{2}\;|\;a+b=1\}\right)/\sim

where
\sim
is generated by
(x,a,b)\sim*
whenever
a\cdot b=0

and by
(x,a,b)\sim(f(x),a,b)
whenever
a+b=1
.

Lemma 3.39
.

There is a homeomorphism

\alpha:|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|\xrightarrow{\cong}\Sigma\mathrm{cone}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|\to G)

defined by

\displaystyle[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{1}}A_{1}\to\dots\to A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{X_{n}}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big[[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{a}}A_{a}\to\dots\to A_{b}\xrightarrow{X_{b+1}}\emptyset),(\tfrac{t_{a}}{T_{ab}},\dots,\tfrac{t_{b}}{T_{ab}})],t_{0}+\dots+t_{a-1},t_{b+1}+\dots+t_{n}\big]

Here
0\leq a\leq b\leq n
are the smallest and largest numbers
such that
A_{a}
and
A_{b}
are non-empty,
and
T_{ab}:=t_{a}+\dots+t_{b}
is a normalisation factor.
If all
A_{i}
are empty we find the unique
c
such that
X_{c}
is non-empty
and define
\alpha
by

[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{1}}A_{1}\to\dots\to A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{X_{n}}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto\big[[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{c}}\emptyset)\in G,t_{0}+\dots+t_{c-1},t_{c}+\dots+t_{n}\big]

Proof.

The statement of the lemma we only define the map on
n
-simplices
corresponding to objects of the groupoid
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{\rm con}
,
but not on the morphisms.
To be precise, a point in
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|
is represented by a triple

((A_{\bullet,\bullet},X_{\bullet,\bullet}),(s_{0},\dots,s_{m}),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n}))

where for each
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
the tuple

((A_{i,\bullet},X_{i,\bullet}),(t_{0},\dots,t_{m}))
is as in the statement
of the lemma. We then define the map by simply applying it for each
i

and carrying along the
s_{i}
-coordinates.
Since this simplicial direction will be entirely orthogonal to the
argument in this proof, we shall continue ignoring it.

The inverse map
\beta:\Sigma\mathrm{cone}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|\to G)\to|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|

can be described as follows:

\displaystyle\big[[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{1}}A_{1}\to\dots\to A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{X_{n}}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})],x,y\big]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{1}}A_{1}\to\dots\to A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{X_{n}}\emptyset),(x,(1-x-y)\cdot t_{0},\dots,(1-x-y)\cdot t_{n},y)]

If
x+y=1
we simply send
[(X:\emptyset\to\emptyset)\in G,x,y]

to
[[\emptyset\xrightarrow{X}\emptyset,(x,y)],x,y]
,
which continuously extends the above.
By construction
\alpha\circ\beta=\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|}
,
but the other composite
\beta\circ\alpha
might not look
like
\mathrm{id}_{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|}
at first sight.
It is given by

\displaystyle[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{1}}A_{1}\to\dots\to A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{X_{n}}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{X_{a}}A_{a}\to\dots\to A_{b}\xrightarrow{X_{b+1}}\emptyset),(t_{0}+\dots+t_{a-1},t_{a},\dots,t_{b},t_{b+1}+\dots+t_{n})]

Since we assume that
A_{1},\dots,A_{a-1}
and
A_{b+1},\dots,A_{n-1}

are empty, it follows that
X_{1},\dots X_{a-1}
and
X_{b+2},\dots,X_{n}

are the identity morphisms
on
\emptyset
.
(If they were non-empty, they would contribute another connected component
to
X_{0\to n}
, which is required to be connected.)
By the definition of the geometric realisation we are allowed
to remove the identity morphism
X_{1}
and add the adjacent weights

t_{0}
and
t_{1}
. Iterating this shows that
\beta\circ\alpha

simply sends every point to a different representative of the same point,
i.e. it is the identity.
∎

Remark 3.40
.

The augmentation yields a disjoint decomposition:

\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}\cong\coprod_{g\in G}\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F,(g)}

where we let
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F,(g)}
be the fiber of
\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}\to G
at
g
.
The disjoint decomposition described above is compatible with face maps
and hence induces a decomposition:

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|\cong\coprod_{g\in G}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F,(g)}|
.
Hence lemma
3.39
equivalently gives a homeomorphism

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|\cong\Sigma\mathrm{cone}\left(\coprod_{g\in G}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F,(g)}|\to G\right)\cong\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{2}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F,(g)}|,

where
S^{2}(-)
denotes the unreduced suspension defined as

S^{2}(X):=(S^{1}\amalg D^{2}\times X)/\sim
with
(a,x)\sim a

for all
a\in S^{1}\subset D^{2}
and
x\in X
.

Now we show that
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}|
is in fact the classifying space of the category
of factorisations.

Definition 3.41
.

An object in the category
\mathcal{F}
is a triple
(M,W,W^{\prime})
where
M\in\mathcal{C}

is a non-empty object and
W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M
and
W^{\prime}:M\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}

are two morphisms such that
W\cup_{M}W^{\prime}
is a connected morphism.
A morphism
(M,W,W^{\prime})\to(N,V,V^{\prime})
is a morphism
X:M\to N

such that
W\cup_{M}X=W^{\prime}
and
V=X\cup_{M^{\prime}}V^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.42
.

In definition
2.34
we defined factorisation categories

\mathcal{F}_{g}
for specific endomorphisms
g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Composing the two morphisms of a factorisation yields a functor
\mathcal{F}\to G

where the latter is thought of as a category with only identity morphisms.
We therefore have a disjoint decomposition:

\mathcal{F}\cong\coprod_{g\in G}\mathcal{F}_{g}

compatible with the decomposition of
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}
into
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F,(g)}
.

Lemma 3.43
.

Let
N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}
be Rezk’s relative nerve as
in definition
3.21
.
Then the canonical maps:

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\longrightarrow N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}\longleftarrow N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}

induce equivalences
\|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\|\simeq\|BN_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\|\simeq B\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by considering the double-comma category

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{C}_{/1_{\mathcal{C}}}^{1_{\mathcal{C}}/}
where objects are tuples of morphisms

(W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M,W^{\prime}:M\to 1_{\mathcal{C}})
and a morphism

(M,W,W^{\prime})\to(N,V,V^{\prime})
is a morphism
X:M\to N
such that
W\cup_{M}X=W^{\prime}

and
V=X\cup_{M^{\prime}}V^{\prime}
. By definition
\mathcal{F}
is a full subcategory of

\mathcal{K}
on those objects
(M,W,W^{\prime})
where
M
is non-empty and
W\cup_{M}W^{\prime}

is connected.
The nerve of
\mathcal{K}
can be described as follows:

N_{n}\mathcal{K}\cong\{W:[n+2]\to\mathcal{C}\;|\;W(0)=1_{\mathcal{C}}=W(n+2)\}\subset N_{n+2}\mathcal{C}

where the face and degeneracy operators are given by
d_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}:=d_{i+1}^{\mathcal{C}}
and

s_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}:=s_{i+1}^{\mathcal{C}}
.
Note that because the isomorphisms in
\mathcal{K}
are isomorphisms
\mathcal{C}

that make the relevant diagrams commute, we also have an equivalence

N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{K}\simeq\{(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}(\mathcal{C}))^{2}}N_{n+2}^{\rm rel}(\mathcal{C})

where the functor
N_{n+2}^{\rm rel}(\mathcal{C})\to(N_{0}^{\rm rel}(\mathcal{C}))^{2}

is the one that picks out the first and last object.

Just like
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
induces a simplicial map

N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}
it also induces a simplicial map

D_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})\to N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
.
In defining this on morphisms we use that any bijection

\alpha:A\cong A^{\prime}
with
c_{A^{\prime}}\circ\alpha=c_{A}

induces an isomorphism
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(A,c_{A})\cong\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(A^{\prime},c_{A}^{\prime})
.
Restricting this map we obtain a map of simplicial groupoids

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\to N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{K}
,
which lands in the relative nerve of the full subcategory

\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{K}
.
We hence have the following zig-zag of simplicial groupoids:

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\longrightarrow N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}\longleftarrow N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}.

By lemma
3.22
the second map induces an equivalence
on geometric realisations. So to finish the proof we will check that
the first map satisfies the condition of the base-change
corollary
3.20
.
We have already checked in lemma
3.22
that in the
relative nerve of any category the boundary map

\partial_{n}:N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}\to(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F})^{n+1}

is an iso-fibration, which verifies the third condition.

The first condition states that
\mathrm{Cut}_{0}^{F}\to N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\rm iso}

is essentially surjective. Indeed, any factorisation

(W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M,W^{\prime}:M\to 1_{\mathcal{C}})
is isomorphic to one where
M
is the
product of connected objects. In that case we can write
M=\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(A,c_{A})

for
(A,c_{A})\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
. Since
\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}:h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

is an equivalence of categories by lemma
2.28
,
we can find
(\underline{X},o):\emptyset\to(A,c_{A})
and

(\underline{X}^{\prime},o^{\prime}):(A,c_{A})\to\emptyset
that are mapped to
W
and
W^{\prime}
, respectively.
The tuple
((\underline{X},o),(\underline{X}^{\prime},o^{\prime}))
defines a
2
-simplex in
D_{\bullet}

that is an object in
\mathrm{Cut}_{0}^{F}
and it is mapped to
(M,W,W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}
.

The second condition is the base-change condition. We would like to show that

(\partial_{n},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}):\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}\longrightarrow(\mathrm{Cut}_{0}^{F})^{n+1}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F})^{n+1}}N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}

is an equivalence of groupoids.
By lemma
2.28
the
n
the following functor
is essentially surjective and fully faithful when restricted to reduced morphisms:

(\partial_{n},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}):D_{n+2}\longrightarrow(D_{0})^{n+3}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})^{n+3}}N_{n+2}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

By fixing the first and last object to be
\emptyset\in D_{0}
and

1_{\mathcal{C}}\in N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}
,
respectively, we obtain:

\{\emptyset,\emptyset\}\times_{(D_{0})^{2}}D_{n+2}\longrightarrow(D_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})^{n+1}}N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{K}

Restricting to full subcategories we obtain the following equivalence:

\mathrm{Cut}_{n}^{F}\longrightarrow(D_{0})^{n+1}\times_{(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})^{n+1}}N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}

Using the simplicial object
T(X)_{n}:=X^{n+1}
from the proof of theorem
3.16

we can write this as a level-wise equivalence of simplicial groupoids:

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}\simeq T(D_{0})\times_{T(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})}N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}
.
We may rewrite this further as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F}

\displaystyle\simeq T(D_{0})\times_{T(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C})}N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}\cong T(D_{0}\times_{N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}}N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F})\times_{T(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F})}N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\simeq T(\mathrm{Cut}_{0}^{F})\times_{T(N_{0}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F})}N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}.

This is the desired equivalence that verifies the remaining condition
in corollary
3.20
.
∎

To summarise the previous sections we record:

Corollary 3.44
.

There are equivalences of
\Gamma
-spaces:

\displaystyle|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|

\displaystyle\simeq Q(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{\rm con}|)\simeq Q\left(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{2}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F,(g)}|)\right)\simeq Q\left(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{2}(B\mathcal{F}_{g})\right).

Proof.

Combine lemma
3.33
, lemma
3.39
,
and lemma
3.43
.
∎

3.8
The reduced category

We now prove the comparison result proposition
3.4

between
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
.

Definition 3.45
.

Let
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{\rm cl}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
denote the full subcategory
on the object
\emptyset
and let
\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}\subset\mathcal{C}
denote
the full subcategory on the object
1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 3.46
.

There are canonical equivalences of infinite loop spaces:

B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl})\simeq B\left(\bigoplus_{G}\mathbb{N}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{\rm cl})\simeq Q\left(\bigvee_{G}S^{1}\right).

Proof.

By definition
\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}
is a category with a single object
1_{\mathcal{C}}

and the endomorphisms of this object are

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}})\cong\bigoplus_{G}\mathbb{N}
by axiom (ii)
of definition
2.4
.
This implies the first equivalence.

The second equivalence can easily be proven from the perspective
of
[
Seg74
]
, but for the readers convenience
we will prove it as an application of lemma
3.35
.
(Note, however, that this lemma is also based on
[
Seg74
]
.)
Indeed, the groupoid
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{\rm cl}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)

is freely generated by its connected objects in the sense of the lemma.
Let
K_{\bullet}
be the level-wise full simplicial subgroupoid of the nerve

N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
containing all those objects
\underline{X}:[n]\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})

where
\underline{X}_{0\to n}:\emptyset\to\emptyset
is connected or empty.
Then lemma
3.35
applies to the tuple

(K_{\bullet}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))
and hence we have:

B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})^{\rm cl})\simeq Q(\|BK_{\bullet}\|).

We will show that
BK_{\bullet}
is level-wise equvialent to

Y_{\bullet}:=(\Delta^{1}\times G)/(\partial\Delta^{1}\times G)
,
the geometric realisation of which is a wedge of circles, one for each element of
G
.
The
n
-simplices of
\Delta^{1}\times G
can be thought of as tuples

(i,g)\in\{0,\dots,n+1\}\times G
where
i
represents the unique map

[n]\to[1]
with
i\mapsto 0
and
(i+1)\mapsto 1
.
The simplicial set
Y_{\bullet}
is obtained by identifying all tuples
(i,g)

with
i\in\{0,n+1\}
.
Indeed, there is a level-wise equivalence of simplicial groupoids

K_{\bullet}\simeq Y_{\bullet}
defined by sending
(\underline{X}:[n]\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\in K_{n}

to the basepoint if
\underline{X}_{0\to n}
is empty and otherwise
to
(i,[\underline{X}_{0\to n}])
where
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
is the unique

i
such that
\underline{X}_{i-1\to i}
is non-empty.
From this it follows that

\|BK_{\bullet}\|\simeq\|(\Delta^{1}\times G)/(\partial\Delta^{1}\times G)\|\simeq\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{1}.

∎

Proposition 3.47
.

There are homotopy fiber sequences of infinite loop spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\left(\bigvee_{G}S^{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\left(\bigoplus_{G}\mathbb{N}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}).}

In particular, the map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to B(\mathcal{C})
is a rational equivalence.

Proof.

For the purpose of the proof we will assume that
\mathcal{C}
has a PCM structure,
rather than a symmetric monoidal structure, and that the functor

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
is a PCM functor.
Since
h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})\to h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})

is a PCM functor, this can always be achieved by replacing
\mathcal{C}
with
h\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
.
Consider the following diagram of simplicial PCM groupoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\bullet}^{\rm cl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\bullet}^{\rm red}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}.}

Here
D_{\bullet}^{\rm cl}\subset D_{\bullet}
is the level-wise full subgroupoid
on those
(\underline{X}_{\bullet},A_{\bullet})
with
A_{i}=\emptyset
for all
i
.

D_{\bullet}^{\rm red}
is defined level-wise as the full subgroupoid
D_{n}^{\rm red}\subset D_{n}

on those
n
-simplices that represent
n
-tuples of composable reduced cospans.
Similar to the definition of
\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
we define the face maps in
D_{\bullet}^{\rm red}

by taking the face map in
D_{\bullet}
and then forgetting closed components.
In the bottom row
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}
is simply the simplicial commutative monoid
where each
N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{N}\langle G\rangle)^{n}
.

It follows from lemma
3.46
and our previous results
that the realisation of this diagram is a commutative diagram
of
\Gamma
-spaces that is equivalent to the diagram stated in
the claim of this proposition.
Moreover, each of the terms is group-like, and so we have
a diagram of infinite loop spaces.

To prove the proposition we apply the general fiber sequence criterion
theorem
3.13
to the two sequences of simplicial PCM groupoids.
For the first one, consider the PCM functor:

S_{n}:D_{n}\to D_{n}^{\rm cl},\qquad(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})\mapsto(\emptyset,\underline{Y}_{\bullet},\emptyset,(o_{\bullet})_{|\underline{Y}_{\bullet}})

where each
\underline{Y}_{i}\subset\underline{X}_{i}
