is defined as the complement
of the image of
A_{i-1}\amalg A_{i}\to\underline{X}_{i}
.
This map is a section to the inclusion
D_{n}^{\rm cl}\to D_{n}
, so to apply the fiber
sequence theorem we need to check that
(S_{n},R_{n}):D_{n}\to D_{n}^{\rm cl}\times D_{n}^{\rm red}

is an equivalence. This functor is given by

(A_{\bullet},\underline{X}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},o_{\bullet})\mapsto\left((\emptyset,\underline{Y}_{\bullet},\emptyset,(o_{\bullet})_{|\underline{Y}_{\bullet}}),(A_{\bullet},\underline{Z}_{\bullet},c_{\bullet},(o_{\bullet})_{|\underline{Z}_{\bullet}})\right)

where
\underline{Y}_{i}
is as above and
\underline{Z}_{i}\subset\underline{X}_{i}
is the image
of
A_{i-1}\amalg A_{i}
, i.e. the complement of
\underline{Y}_{i}
.
Just as in the proof of
3.31
we see that this functor is an
isomorphism onto the subcategory of disjoint tuples and that hence
it is an equivalence.

To conclude that the first row is a homotopy fiber sequence we note that

\pi_{0}|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm cl}|\cong*
,
\pi_{0}|BD_{\bullet}|\cong\pi_{0}B\mathcal{C}
,
and
\pi_{0}|BD_{\bullet}^{\rm red}|\cong\pi_{0}B\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}
are all group-like.

For the second row the proof is similar. The section is given by

S_{n}:N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}\to N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl},\qquad(W:[n]\to\mathcal{C})\mapsto(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\in(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}}))^{n}=N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}

where
V_{i}\subset W(i-1\to i)
is the union of the closed components.
This is a PCM functor where the commutative monoid
N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}

is thought of as a PCM category with only identity morphisms
and where all objects are disjoint.
Since this is indeed a section to the inclusion
N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}\to N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}

we again need to check that the functor

(S_{n},R_{n}):N_{n}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{C}\to N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm cl}\times N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm red}

is an equivalence.
This follows from axiom (iii) of definition
2.4
.
This completes the proof that both rows are homotopy fiber sequences.

Finally, we want to check that
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to B(\mathcal{C})
is a rational equivalence.
By comparing the homotopy fibers of the first two vertical maps between
the two homotopy fiber sequence we obtain the following homotopy fiber sequence:

\tau_{\geq 2}Q(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{1})\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\longrightarrow B(\mathcal{C}).

The homotopy groups of the left-hand space are

\pi_{k}\tau_{\geq 2}Q(\bigvee_{g\in G}S^{1})=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\pi_{k-1}^{\rm st}(S^{0})
for
k\geq 2
.
This is rationally trivial because the stable homotopy groups of spheres

\pi_{*}^{\rm st}(S^{0})
are finite for
*\geq 1
.
Hence the map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C}))\to B(\mathcal{C})
is an isomorphism on rational
homotopy groups, as claimed.
∎

3.9
Contracting the factorisation category

In this section we show that the factorisation categories for

\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
have contractible classifying spaces for all
d\leq 2
.
The argument is based on an argument showing
B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})\simeq*

and crucially uses the existence of the disk morphism
D^{d}:\emptyset\to S^{d-1}
.
In particular, it will not apply to
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
.

Definition 3.48
.

For a non-empty set
X
let
\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}

denote the category where objects are tuples
(A,a)

of a non-empty finite set
A
together with a map
a:A\to X
,
and morphisms
(A,a)\to(B,b)
are injections
i:A\to B

such that
a=b\circ i
.

Lemma 3.49
.

For every non-empty set
X
the classifying space
B\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}

is contractible.

Proof.

Pick any
x_{0}\in X
and consider the functor

F:\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}\to\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}\qquad(A,a)\mapsto(A\amalg\{x_{0}\},a\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\{x_{0}\}})

that adds a new point to
A
and labels it by
x_{0}
.
There is a natural transformation
\alpha:\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}}\Rightarrow F

coming from the canonical inclusion
\alpha_{A}:A\hookrightarrow A\amalg\{x_{0}\}
.
There also is a natural transformation
\beta:G\Rightarrow F
from the
constant functor
G(A,a):=(\{x_{0}\},\mathrm{id}_{\{x_{0}\}})
coming
from the other inclusion
\beta_{A}:\{x_{0}\}\hookrightarrow A\amalg\{x_{0}\}
.
Both
\alpha
and
\beta
induce homotopies after taking classifying spaces
and together they imply that the identity on
B\operatorname{Fin}_{/X}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}

is homotopic to
BG
, which is constant.
∎

Lemma 3.50
.

The factorisation category
\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
has a contractible classifying space.

Proof.

Consider the functor
\partial_{0}:\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})\to\operatorname{Fin}
that sends
the factorisation
(W:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to M,W^{\prime}:M\to 1_{\mathcal{C}})
to the underlying
set
\pi(W)
of the first morphism.
To a morphism
X:M\to N
from
(M,W,W^{\prime})
to
(N,V,V^{\prime})

this assigns the composite
\pi(W)\to\pi(W)\cup_{\pi(M)}\pi(X)\cong\pi(V)
.
A short explanation is due to argue why we can talk about
\pi(W)

as a well-defined set. A priori the morphism
W
only yields an
isomorphism class of cospans
[\emptyset\to\pi(W)\leftarrow\pi(M)]
,
but not a well-defined set
\pi(W)
. But because this is part
of a factorisation we know that
\pi(M)\to\pi(W)
is surjective.
In particular we can define
\partial_{0}(M,W,W^{\prime})
as the quotient
of
\pi(M)
by the relation induced by
\pi(M)\to\pi(W)
.

We define a new category
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
as the Grothendieck construction
of the functor
\mathcal{F}\to\mathrm{Cat}
that sends
(M,W,W^{\prime})
to

\operatorname{Fin}_{/\partial_{0}(M,W,W^{\prime})}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj,{\rm op}}
.
Spelling out the construction we see that an object in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

is a tuple
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))
where
(M,W,W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
,

A
is a non-empty finite set and
a:A\to\partial_{0}(M,W,W^{\prime})
.
A morphism
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))\to((N,V,V^{\prime}),(B,b))
is a
tuple of a morphism
X:M\to N
in
\mathcal{F}
and an injection
i:A\to B

such that
\partial_{0}(X)\circ a=b\circ i
.
Consider the forgetful functor
F:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
that sends

((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))
to
(M,W,W^{\prime})
.

It is a consequence of Quillen’s theorem A that,
given any functor
\Phi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Cat}
where each
B(\Phi(c))
is contractible,
the forgetful functor
\int_{\mathcal{C}}\Phi\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence on classifying spaces.
(One can also think of this as an instance of Thomason’s homotopy colimit theorem
[
Tho79
]
.)
In lemma
3.49
we showed that each

\operatorname{Fin}_{/\partial_{0}(M,W,W^{\prime})}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj,{\rm op}}

has a contractible classifying space,
hence
F:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
is a homotopy equivalence.
We will prove the lemma by showing that the homotopy equivalence

B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
is null-homotopic.

Define a functor:

G:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F},\quad((M,[\emptyset\to W\leftarrow M],[M\to V\leftarrow\emptyset]),(a:A\to W))\mapsto(A,[\emptyset\to A\xleftarrow{=}A],[A\to*\leftarrow\emptyset])

to a morphism
(X,i):((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))\to((N,V,V^{\prime}),(B,b))
this
assigns the morphism

[A\xrightarrow{i}B\xleftarrow{=}B].

This functor by definition factors through
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Fin}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}
.
Since the classifying space
B\operatorname{Fin}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}
is contractible
(set
X=*
in lemma
3.49
),
we can conclude that
BG:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
is null-homotopic.

We construct a natural transformation
\alpha:G\Rightarrow F

by assigning to each
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))
the morphism

\alpha_{((M,W,V),(A,a))}:=[A\xrightarrow{a}W\leftarrow M]:(A,[\emptyset\to A\xleftarrow{=}A],[A\to*\leftarrow\emptyset])\to(M,W,V).

To see that this is a well-defined morphism in the factorisation category we check

\displaystyle[A\to W\leftarrow M]\cup_{M}[M\to W^{\prime}\leftarrow\emptyset]

\displaystyle=[A\to*\leftarrow\emptyset]

\displaystyle[\emptyset\to A\leftarrow A]\cup_{A}[A\to W\leftarrow M]

\displaystyle=[\emptyset\to W\leftarrow M].

Here the first equation used that
W\cup_{M}W^{\prime}=*
holds for all objects

(M,W,W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
.
We also need to check that
\alpha
is natural.
To see this consider a morphism

(X,i):((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))\to((N,V,V^{\prime}),(B,b))

where
X=[M\to X\leftarrow N]:(M,W,W)\to(N,V,V^{\prime})

and
i:A\hookrightarrow B
.
For any such morphism we have that:

[A\xrightarrow{i}B\xleftarrow{=}B]\cup_{B}[B\xrightarrow{b}V\leftarrow N]=[A\xrightarrow{a}W\leftarrow M]\cup_{M}[M\to X\leftarrow N].

since
V=W\cup_{M}X
and
b\circ i=\partial_{0}(X)\circ a
.

In summary, we have shown that the forgetful functor induces
a homotopy equivalence
BF:B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
,
that the map
BG:B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
factors through
the contractible space
B\operatorname{Fin}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}
,
and that
\alpha
induces a homotopy between
BF
and
BG
.
This implies that
B\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp})
is contractible as claimed.
∎

The same argument applies to
B\mathcal{F}_{a}(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha))\simeq*
for all
a\in A

as long as
0\in A_{1}
, i.e. as long as
A_{1}=A
.

Figure 4:
Left: an object
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))\in\mathcal{F}_{g=3}^{\prime}(\mathrm{Cob}_{2})
.
Middle: the value of the natural transformation

\alpha:G\Rightarrow\operatorname{Id}
at this object.
Right: the value the functor
G
on this object.

Lemma 3.51
.

For all
d\geq 2
and all diffeomorphism types
[Q]
of closed
d
-manifolds
the factorisation category
\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
has a contractible
classifying space.

Proof.

An object of
\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
is a triple
(M,[W],[W^{\prime}])
where
M
is
a closed oriented
(d-1)
-manifold and
[W]
and
[W^{\prime}]
are diffeomorphism
classes of
d
-dimensional cobordisms
W:\emptyset\to M
and
W^{\prime}:M\to\emptyset

such that their composite
W\cup_{M}W^{\prime}
is diffeomorphic to
Q
.
A morphism
(M,[W],[W^{\prime}])\to(N,[V],[V^{\prime}])
is a diffeomorphism class
of cobordisms
[X]:M\to N
satisfying
W\cup_{M}X\cong V
and
W^{\prime}\cong X\cup_{N}V^{\prime}
.

We will choose explicit representatives for
[W]
and
[W^{\prime}]
throughout.
This does not change the category
\mathcal{F}
, as long as we still define
morphisms as above.

As in the proof of lemma
3.50
we construct
a category
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
as follows.
Objects are tuples
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)))
where
(M,W,W^{\prime})

is an object as above,
A
is a finite non-empty set, and
a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)
a map.
By the same argument as in lemma
3.50

the forgetful functor
F:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
is an equivalence.

The following constructions are illustrated in figure
4
.
Consider the functor
G:\operatorname{Fin}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}\to\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})

that sends a finite set
A
to the factorisation

G(A)=(A\times S^{d-1},A\times D^{d}:\emptyset\to A\times S^{d-1},Q_{A}:A\times S^{d-1}\to\emptyset)

where the cobordism
Q_{A}:A\times S^{d-1}\to\emptyset
is obtained
by picking an injection
A\to Q
, removing a neighbourhood of its image,
and identifying its boundary with
A\times S^{d-1}
in the canonical orientation
preserving way.
Since
Q
is connected and of dimension
d\geq 2

any choice of injection leads to the same diffeomorphism class of cobordism.
To injections
i:A\to B
the functor
G
assigns the cobordism:

G(i:A\to B):=[(A\times S^{d-1}\times[0,1])\amalg((B\setminus i(A))\times D^{d})]:A\times S^{d-1}\to B\times S^{d-1}.

This cobordism connects the sphere
\{a\}\times S^{d-1}
with
\{i(a)\}\times S^{d-1}

via a cylinder and caps off each of the spheres
\{b\}\times S^{d-1}

with
b\not\in i(A)
with a disk.
This defines a morphism in the factorisation category because
closing off the
(B\setminus i(A))
-spheres of
Q_{A}
yields a manifold
diffeomorphic to
Q_{B}
:

G(i:A\to B)\cup_{B\times S^{d-1}}Q_{B}\cong Q_{A}\quad\text{and}\quad(A\times D^{d})\cup_{A\times S^{d-1}}G(i:A\to B)\cong(B\times D^{d}).

Let us denote the composite of
G
with the forgetful functor

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Fin}^{\neq\emptyset,\rm inj}
also by
G
.

To conclude the proof in the same manner as for lemma
3.50

we would like to construct a natural transformation

\alpha:G\Rightarrow F
of functors
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
.
For an object
((M,W,W^{\prime}),(a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)))\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
we let

W_{A}:A\times S^{d-1}\to M
be the cobordism obtained by lifting the map

a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)
to an embedding
a:A\hookrightarrow W
and
then removing a small neighbourhood of its image.
We define the components of
\alpha
as:

\alpha_{((M,W,W^{\prime}),(a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)))}:=W_{A}:(A\times S^{d-1},A\times D^{d},Q_{A})\to(M,W,W^{\prime}).

To check that this is a well-defined morphism in the factorisation category
we note:

(A\times D^{d})\cup_{A\times S^{d-1}}W_{A}\cong W\quad\text{and}\quad W_{A}\cup_{M}V\cong Q_{A}.

To check naturality let
([X],i):((M,W,W^{\prime}),(A,a))\to((N,V,V^{\prime}),(B,b))

be a morphism in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, i.e.
X:M\to N
a cobordism and
i:A\to B

and injection, both satisfying certain conditions.
Then we have:

G(i:A\to B)\cup_{B\times S^{d-1}}V_{B}\cong V_{A}\cong W_{A}\cup_{M}X.

Here the first diffeomorphism comes from the fact that both sides
are obtained from
V:\emptyset\to N
by removing disks according to

(\partial_{0}(X)\circ a:A\to\pi_{0}(W)\to\pi_{0}(V))=(b\circ i:A\to B\to\pi_{0}(V))
,
and the second diffeomorphism comes from the fact that we can arrange
for the diffeomorphism
V\cong W\cup_{M}X
to fix the disks corresponding to
A
.

This shows that
BG,BF:B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to B\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
are homotopic.
Since
BF
is a homotopy equivalence and
BG
is null-homotopic,
this implies that
B\mathcal{F}_{[Q]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{d})
is contractible, as claimed.
∎

Corollary 3.52
.

For
d\geq 2
there are equivalences:

B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathrm{Csp}|\quad\text{and}\quad B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{d}\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathrm{Cob}_{d}|.

Proof.

Combine the decomposition theorem
3.1

with lemma
3.51
and lemma
3.50
.
∎

4
The surgery theorem

The purpose of this section is to prove the surgery theorem that relates
space of “no compact components” with the positive boundary subcategory.

Theorem 4.1

(Surgery theorem)
.

For any labelled cospan category
\mathcal{C}
that admits surgery the inclusion

N\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\to N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}
of simplicial sets induces an equivalence

B(\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}})\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|.

The proof of this theorem will be given by implementing a surgery
similar to the one described in
[
Gal11
, section 4.2, figure 3]

using language similar to that of
[
GRW14
, section 3 and 6]
.
For each
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
and point
p\in W(i\leq i+1)

there is either a path connecting it to
W(0)
or to
W(n)
.
We will use this path to do a surgery that connects
p
to
W(0)
or
W(n)
.
One of the key problems will be that there is not always a unique or canonical surgery,
but rather a contractible space of choices. We will have make sure that any
combination of surgeries commutes.

Definition 4.2
.

For the rest of this section, we fix a labelled cospan category

(\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Csp})
together with the following data:

•

a connected object
O\in\mathcal{C}
and a connected morphism
T:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to O
and

•

for any connected object
A\in\mathcal{C}
a connected morphism
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A
,

subject to the conditions in
2.42
.
(See also figure
3
.)

To construct the homotopies that are crucial to the proof of theorem
4.1

it will be extremely useful to work with a variant of
\mathcal{C}
where objects
are simply subsets of some big background set
\Omega
.

Definition 4.3
.

Fix a set
\Omega
and a map
F:\Omega\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
such that every
connected object
M\in\mathcal{C}
has infinitely many preimages in
\Omega
.
For any finite subset
A\subset\Omega
we let
6
6

6

To be precise we pick a total ordering on
\Omega
and define

F(A)=(\dots(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes a_{2})\otimes\dots)\otimes a_{n})

for
A=\{a_{1}<\dots<a_{n}\}
.
By the coherence theorem for symmetric monoidal categories
there is a preferred isomorphism
F(A)\cong F(A_{1})\otimes F(A_{2})

built from associators and braidings
whenever
A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}
with
A_{1}\cap A_{2}
.
We will be suppressing these coherence isomorphisms unless they become relevant.

F(A):=\bigotimes_{a\in A}F(a)
.

The category
\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
has as objects finite subset of
\Omega

and a morphism
W:A\to B
is defined as a morphism
W:F(A)\to F(B)
in
\mathcal{C}
.

In what follows we will often use
n
-simplices in the nerve of
\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}

so it will be useful to fix some notation.
An element of
N_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
is a functor
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}

sending any
0\leq i\leq n
to an object
W(i)\in\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
,
i.e. a finite subset
W(i)\subset\Omega
,
and any
0\leq i\leq j\leq n
to a morphism
W(i\leq j):W(i)\to W(j)
.
We will denote the underlying cospan of
W(i\leq j)
by

W(i)\to W_{\pi}(i\leq j)\leftarrow W(j)
.

Definition 4.4
.

We let
C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
be the simplicial subset containing
those
n
-simplices
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
where
W(0\leq n)

has no closed components,
i.e. those
W
for which

W(0)\amalg W(n)\to W_{\pi}(0\leq n)
is surjective.

Moreover, we let
C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
be the simplicial subset
containing those
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
where
W(k\leq k+1)

is positive boundary for all
k
, i.e. where

W(k+1)\to W_{\pi}(k\leq k+1)
is surjective for all
k
.

Lemma 4.5
.

With the usual notion of disjointnes and disjoint union

\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
is a partial commutative monoidal category
and the functor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence of categories.
Moreover,
F
induces equivalences:

|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\simeq B\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\quad\text{and}\quad|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|.

Proof.

Since the objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
are simply finite subsets of
\Omega

it is clear that the notion of disjointnes and disjoint union behave as expected.
For
\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
to satisfy definition
3.5
we need to
check that for any finite sequence of objects
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\subset\Omega

one can find isomorphic replacements
A_{1}^{\prime},\dots,A_{n}^{\prime}\subset\Omega

that are pairwise disjoint.
We required
F:\Omega\to\operatorname{Obj}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})
to have infinite preimages for this exact purpose.
Starting with
A_{2}
we can find for each
a\in A_{i}
an element
a^{\prime}\in\Omega

with
F(a)=F(a^{\prime})
and
a^{\prime}
disjoint to all previous elements.
Then
a
and
a^{\prime}
are isomorphic via the morphism
\mathrm{id}_{F(a)}:a\to a^{\prime}
.
Moreover, they are all disjoint by construction.

The functor
F
is fully faithful by construction and it is essentially surjective
because every object in the labelled cospan category
\mathcal{C}
can be written
as a product of connected objects.
Therefore
F
is an equivalence of categories and it also restricts
to an equivalence between
(\mathcal{C}^{\Omega})^{\partial_{+}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}
.
Since
C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}\cong(\mathcal{C}^{\Omega})^{\partial_{+}}
we see that
|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\simeq B\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}
.
The equivalence
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|\simeq|N_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\mathcal{C}|
follows from
lemma
2.39
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

We have so far treated
W_{\pi}(k\leq l)
as if it were a well-defined set,
but one has to be careful with this. In general, for a morphism
W(k\leq l)

we only have an isomorphism class of cospans
[W(k)\to W_{\pi}(k\leq l)\leftarrow W(l)]
.
It does therefore not really make sense to talk about elements of
W_{\pi}(k\leq l)
.
However, if the
n
-simplex
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
lies in
C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}\subset N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
,
then for each
k\leq l
the map
W(k)\amalg W(l)\to W_{\pi}(k\leq l)
is surjective
and we may define the set
W_{\pi}(k\leq l)
as the quotient of
W(k)\amalg W(l)

by the relation induced by the cospan.
As this is the only case that features in this section we will confidently
speak about elements of
W_{\pi}(k\leq l)
.

4.1
Surgery data

Given some
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
that is not yet in
C_{n}^{\partial_{+}}
there must
be parts of
W
that are not positive boundary.
Concretely, we can find an element
u\in W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
that is not in the
image of
W(i+1)\to W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
.
We would like to find a homotopy that homotops this to a simplex in
C_{n}^{\partial_{+}}
.
To do so we first choose a “path” from
u
to
W(0)
or
W(n)

along which we can introduce a morphism that connects
U
to a positive boundary.
This is the datum of a surgery path.
To make sense of the idea of a path we introduce the following notion
of representing space:

Definition 4.7
.

For an
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
we define the representing space
|W|
as

|W|=\left(\{(t,k,\omega)\in[0,1]\times\{0,\dots,n\}\times\Omega\;|\;\omega\in W(k)\}\amalg\{\bot,\top\}\right)/\sim

The equivalence relation
\sim
is defined as follows:
for
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
it identifies two elements of

\{(t,k,\omega)\in|W|\;|\;t+k=m\}\cong W(k-1)\amalg W(k)

whenever they are mapped to the same element under

W(k-1)\amalg W(k)\to W_{\pi}(k-1\leq k)
.
Moreover, we set
(0,0,\omega_{0})\sim\bot
and
(1,n,\omega_{n})\sim\top

for all
\omega_{0}\in W_{\pi}(0)
and
\omega_{n}\in W_{\pi}(n)
.

This space naturally comes with a continuous projection
\operatorname{pr}:|W|\to[0,n+1]

sending
[t,k,\omega]
to
t+k
,
\bot
to
0
, and
\top
to
n+1
.
We let
|W|_{0}\subset|W|
be the finite subset
\operatorname{pr}^{-1}(\{1,\dots,n\})
.

Remark 4.8
.

We can also think of
|W|
as a long sequence of pushouts:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(0)\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(1)\times[1,2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(n)\times[0,n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\pi}(0\leq 1)\times\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \qquad\dots\qquad\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(0)\times\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(0)\times\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(1)\times\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\dots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(n)\times\{n+1\}}

The finite subset
|W|_{0}\subset|W|
is exactly the image of the sets

W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)\times\{i\}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
We can therefore define a map
g:|W|_{0}\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C})

that sends each
(i,k,\omega)
with
k+i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
to the connected morphism
in
\mathcal{C}
that represents the relevant component of
W_{\pi}(k+i-1\leq k+i)
.
This is exactly the labeling of the cospan in the enhanced
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(\mathcal{C})
-model.
The datum of
W
together with
g
in fact uniquely encodes
the
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
.
Motivated by this we will often be drawing pictures of

(|W|,g:|W|_{0}\to\operatorname{Mor}^{\rm con}(\mathcal{C}))
as in figure
5
.

Figure 5:
On top: a
3
-simplex
W\in C_{3}^{\rm nc}
for the labelled cospan category

\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
.
Below: The representing space
|W|
with labels in
\mathbb{N}
recording the genus
and a possible surgery path.

Definition 4.9
.

For
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
a
surgery path
is a continuous path
p:[0,1]\to|W|

such that the composite
p_{\mathbb{R}}:[0,1]\to|W|\to[0,n+1]
is piece-wise linear,

p_{\mathbb{R}}(0)\in\{0,n+1\}
, and
p_{\mathbb{R}}(1)\in\{0,1,\dots,n+1\}
.

If the integer
i:=p_{\mathbb{R}}(1)
is in
\{1,\dots,n\}

then the end-point of the path corresponds
to some element
p_{\rm end}\in W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)
.
We also denote by
p_{\Omega}(1)\in\Omega
the last well-defined value of

(\operatorname{pr}_{\Omega}\circ p)(r)
as
r\to 1
,
this is an element of either
W(i-1)
or
W(i)
that
is sent to
p_{\rm end}
in the cospan
W(i-1)\to W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)\leftarrow W(i)
.

Of course one surgery path by itself is not sufficient to make some
W
be positive boundary.
We need several surgery paths at once, which we collect in a surgery datum:

Definition 4.10
.

A surgery datum for
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
is a finite subset

A\subset\Omega_{O}:=F^{-1}(O)
.
disjoint to
\cup_{i=0}^{n}W(k)\subset\Omega

together with the choice of a surgery path
p^{\alpha}:[0,1]\to|W|
for all
\alpha\in A

such that they satisfy the following connectivity condition:

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
u\in W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)

either
u
is in the image of
W(i)\to W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)
,
or there is an
\alpha\in A
such that the path
p^{\alpha}
ends at
u=p_{\rm end}^{\alpha}
.

For a surgery datum
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
we let

A^{\rm in}:=\{\alpha\in A\;|\;p_{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha}(0)=0\}\subset A

denote the subset of those
\alpha
where
the surgery path starts at the incoming boundary.

Note that the empty collection is a surgery datum for
W
if and only if
W

already lies in
C_{n}^{\partial_{+}}
. In that case we do not need to do any surgery.
We now describe how the surgery data behaves with respect to the simplicial structure:

Definition 4.11
.

For any injective morphism
\lambda:[n]\to[m]
in
\Delta
we define a map

\lambda^{*}:\{0,\dots,m+1\}\to\{0,\dots,n+1\}\quad k\mapsto\min\{j\;|\;\lambda(j)\geq k\}

where we set
\lambda^{*}(0)=0
and
\lambda^{*}(m+1)=n+1
by convention.
Extending affine linearly we obtain the piece-wise linear map

\lambda^{\bullet}:[0,m+1]\to[0,n+1]
.
This defines a functor
\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}
.

For an
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
we let
\lambda_{W}:|W|\to|\lambda^{*}W|
denote
the unique continuous map that satisfies
\lambda_{W}([t,\lambda(k),\omega])=[t,k,\omega]
for
all
[t,k,\omega]\in|\lambda^{*}W|
,
\lambda_{W}(\bot)=\bot
,
\lambda_{W}(\top)=\top
,
and makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|W|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\lambda^{*}W|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,m+1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\bullet}}

Definition 4.12
.

The semisimplicial set
C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}
has as
n
-simplices pairs of an
n
-simplex

W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
with a surgery datum
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
on
W
.
The semisimplicial structure is defined by

\lambda^{*}(W,(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}):=(\lambda^{*}W,(\lambda_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}).

Lemma 4.13
.

If
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a surgery datum for
W

then
(\lambda_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a surgery datum for
\lambda^{*}W
.
Therefore
C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}
is a well-defined semi-simplicial set.

Proof.

Since
\lambda_{W}:|W|\to|\lambda^{*}W|
is continuous the new path

\lambda_{W}\circ p:[0,1]\to|W|\to|\lambda^{*}W|
is also continuous.
Moreover,
\lambda^{\bullet}:[0,n+1]\to[0,m+1]
is a piece-wise linear map and hence

(\lambda_{W}\circ p)_{\mathbb{R}}=\lambda^{\bullet}\circ p_{\mathbb{R}}
is piece-wise linear, too.
The condition
p_{\mathbb{R}}(0)\subset\{0,n+1\}
is preserved because

\lambda^{\bullet}:[0,n+1]\to[0,m+1]
preserves the minimal and the maximal element.
The condition
p_{\mathbb{R}}(1)\in\{0,\dots,n+1\}

implies
(\lambda^{\bullet}\circ p_{\mathbb{R}})(1)\in\{0,\dots,m+1\}

since
\lambda^{\bullet}
sends integers to integers.
Therefore
\lambda_{W}\circ p
is indeed still a surgery path.

Next we need to check that
(\lambda_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a surgery datum
for
\lambda^{*}W
. It is clear that
A
is disjoint to
\lambda^{*}W
as we
can only forget elements of
\Omega
when passing from
W
to
\lambda^{*}W
.
To check the connectivity condition let us assume that
\lambda

is the unique morphism
\delta^{i}:[n]\to[n+1]
whose image does not contain
i
.
Write
d_{i}W:=(\delta^{i})^{*}W
. The general case follows as the category

\Delta_{\rm inj}
is generated by the
\delta^{i}
.
Consider some
u\in(d_{i}W)_{\pi}(j-1\leq j)
that is not in the image of
(d_{i}W)_{\pi}(j)
.
If
j\neq i
it is clear from the connectivitiy condition for
W

that there is an
\alpha\in A
such that
p^{\alpha}
ends in
p_{\rm end}^{\alpha}=u
.
We may therefore assume that
j=i
, in which case
(d_{i}W)(i-1\leq i)

is the composite of
W(i-1\leq i)
and
W(i\leq i+1)

and the set
(d_{i}W)_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)
is the pushout

W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)\amalg_{W(i)}W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
.

There are two cases: either
u\in(d_{i}W)_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)

is not hit by an element of
W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
or it is.
In the former case
u
is represented by an element
v\in W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)

that is not in the image of
W(i)
.
The connectivity condition for
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}

implies that there is
\alpha\in A
such that the surgery path
p^{\alpha}

ends at
p_{\rm end}^{\alpha}=v
.
Consequently
\delta^{i}_{W}\circ p^{\alpha}
ends at

(\delta^{i}_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\rm end}=\delta^{i}_{W}(p^{\alpha}_{\rm end})=u
and we are done.
In the other case we can represent
u
by an element
v^{\prime}\subset W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
.
This
v^{\prime}
cannot lie in the image of
W(i+1)\to W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)
,
as otherwise
u
would be in the image of
(d_{i}W)(i)\to(d_{i}W)_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)
,
which we assumed not to be the case.
This means that
v
does not have positive boundary as part of
W

and so we can find
\alpha\in A
such that
p^{\alpha}
ends in
p_{\rm end}^{\alpha}=v
,
and then
(\delta^{i}_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\rm end}=\delta^{i}_{W}(p_{\rm end}^{\alpha})=u
.

This shows that
(\lambda_{W}\circ p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a surgery datum for
\lambda^{*}W

and hence the face operators
\lambda^{*}
yield well-defined elements of
C_{m}^{\sigma}
.
Since the
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{\bullet}
construction is functorial it follows that
this assembles into a well-defined functor
C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}:\Delta_{\rm inj}^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Set}
.
∎

4.2
The basic surgery

We now want to describe how to do the surgery given a surgery datum.
The problem of choosing this surgery datum will be dealt with
in the next subsection.
In formulas this means that we want to construct a homotopy from
the forgetful map
|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
to another map that lands
in the subspace
|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\subset|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
.
Moreover, we would like this homotopy to be constant in the case of an empty surgery datum.
This is summarized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{1}}

Here
\mathcal{S}:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
is a homotopy,
which we think of as a continuous family of maps
\mathcal{S}_{r}
indexed
by
r\in[0,1]
such that
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is the forgetful map and
\mathcal{S}_{1}

is a retraction onto the subspace
|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|
.

The homotopy
\mathcal{S}
will be obtained by concatenating two homotopies
\rho
and
K
.
With
\rho
we introduce a disjoint copy of
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in A^{\rm in}

as pictured in figure
6
.
This is based on the idea of how the morphism
T:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to O

yields a natural transformation
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow O\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
which in turn induces a homotopy of maps
B\mathcal{C}\to B\mathcal{C}
.
The second homotopy
K
will be more complicated as we have to
follow a surgery path to move a copy of the morphism
P_{M}:M\to O\otimes M

from
W(0)
or
W(n)
to morphism at the end-point of the surgery path,
in order to make that morphism positive boundary; see figure
8
.

Definition 4.14
.

We let
\Omega_{O}:=F^{-1}(O)\subset\Omega
.
For any
\alpha\in\Omega_{O}
and
\omega\in\Omega
with
\omega\neq\alpha
we define
morphisms
T_{\alpha}:\emptyset\to\{\alpha\}

and
P_{\omega,\alpha}:\{\omega\}\to\{\omega,\alpha\}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
by:

T_{\alpha}:=(F(\emptyset)=1_{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{T}O=F(\alpha))\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\omega,\alpha}:=(F(\omega)\xrightarrow{P_{F(\omega)}}O\otimes F(\omega)\cong F(\{\alpha,\omega\}))

The basic surgery will not be a semi-simplicial map, rather it is only defined on geometric
realisations. Recall that the space
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
is a quotient of the disjoint union

\coprod_{n\geq 0}C_{n}^{\rm nc}\times|\Delta^{n}|\longrightarrow|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|.

We will parametrise the space
|\Delta^{n}|
as

\{\mathbf{t}=(t_{0},\dots,t_{n+1})\in[0,1]^{n+2}\;|\;0=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\dots t_{n}\leq t_{n+1}=1\}
.
A point in
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
can be represented by
(W,\mathbf{t})

where
W:[n]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Omega}
and
\mathbf{t}
is as above.

Figure 6:
A slide show of the homotopy
\rho_{\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t})
for a
2
-simplex
W

as
s
moves from
1
to
0
.

We begin by defining a homotopy from the forgetful map

|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
to a map that sends
(W,\mathbf{t})

to
(W\amalg\mathrm{id}_{A},\mathbf{t})
for
A
some finite subset of
\Omega_{O}
disjoint to
W
.

Definition 4.15
.

Given a point
(W,\mathbf{t})\in|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
,
\alpha\in\Omega_{O}
disjoint to
W

and
s\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
we define

\rho_{\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t}):=(W^{\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})

where
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
is
(t_{0},\dots,t_{i},s,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})

and the
(n+1)
-simplex
W^{\prime}
is defined as

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(j)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j)&\text{ for }j\leq i\\
W(j-1)\amalg\{\alpha\}&\text{ for }j\geq i+1\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(j\leq j+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j\leq j+1)&\text{ for }j\leq i\\
\mathrm{id}_{W(j+1)}\amalg\ T_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j=i+1\\
W(j-1\leq j)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j\geq i+2.\end{cases}

Given a finite set
A=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}\}\subset\Omega

that is disjoint to
W
we define:

\rho_{A}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t})=(\rho_{\alpha_{1}}^{r}\circ\dots\circ\rho_{\alpha_{N}}^{r})(W,\mathbf{t}).

Lemma 4.16
.

The construction in definition
4.15
yields a continuous map

\rho:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\longrightarrow|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad((W,(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},\mathbf{t}),r)\mapsto\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t})

and restricted to
r=1
this map is the standard projection

|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
.
Here as before
A^{\rm in}\subset A
denotes the subset corresponding to those
paths that start at
0
.

Proof.

The map clearly only depends on the finite subset
A^{\rm in}\subset\Omega
and not
on the rest of the surgery path
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
,
which we will only use later for the second part of the homotopy.
For simplicity we will consider the case of a single element
A^{\rm in}=\{\alpha\}
,
the general case follows as the operations
\rho_{\alpha}^{r}
and
\rho_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{r}

commute when
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
.

Definition
4.15
is ambiguous when
r=t_{i}
as we can think
of it as both
r\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
and
r\in[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.
In either case the tuple
(W^{\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})
that is specified in the definition
represents the same point as
(W^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{t})
in the geometric realisation
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|

where:

\displaystyle W^{\prime\prime}(j)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j)&\text{ for }j\leq i\\
W(j)\amalg\{\alpha\}&\text{ for }j>i\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle W^{\prime\prime}(j\leq j+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j\leq j+1)&\text{ for }j\leq i-1\\
W(i\leq i+1)\amalg\ T_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j=i\\
W(j\leq j+1)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j\geq i+1.\end{cases}

This shows that the definition of
\rho
on the closed subsets

U_{i}=\{(\mathbf{t},r)\in|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]\;|\;r\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\}

fits together on the overlaps.
Since
\rho
is continuous on each of the
U_{i}
we get for every
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\sigma}

a well-defined continuous map:

\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{\bullet}(W,\underline{\ \ }):|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad(\mathbf{t},r)\mapsto\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t}).

To obtain a well-defined map
|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|

it remains to check that these maps are compatible with taking face maps
in the sense that
\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(d_{i}W,\mathbf{t})=\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(W,\delta^{i}\mathbf{t})
.
Here
\delta^{i}:|\Delta^{n-1}|\to|\Delta^{n}|
is defined by repeating the
i
th entry.
The equality
\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(d_{i}W,\mathbf{t})=\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{r}(W,\delta^{i}\mathbf{t})
is clear when

r\neq t_{i}
and in the case that
r=t_{i}
it holds because of

W(i\leq i+2)\amalg T_{\alpha}=(W(i\leq i+1)\amalg T_{\alpha})\cup(W(i+1\leq i+2)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}).

Therefore
\rho
descends to a well-defined and continuous map

|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
as claimed.
∎

Now that we have the homotopy that introduces the cylinder
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}
we proceed
to describe a similar, but more complicated homotopy that homotops this cylinder
along a surgery path to make a certain part of
W
positive boundary.

Definition 4.17
.

Given a point
(W,\mathbf{t})\in|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
,
\omega\in W_{i}
for some
i
,

\alpha\in\Omega_{O}
disjoint to
W
and
s\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
we define

\sigma_{\omega,\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t}):=(W^{\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})

where
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
is
(t_{0},\dots,t_{i},s,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})

and the
(n+1)
-simplex
W^{\prime}
is defined as

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(j)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j)&\text{ for }j\leq i\\
W(j-1)\amalg\{\alpha\}&\text{ for }j\geq i+1\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(j\leq j+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}W(j\leq j+1)&\text{ for }j\leq i+1\\
\mathrm{id}_{W(i+1)\setminus\{\omega\}}\amalg P_{\omega,\alpha}&\text{ for }j=i+1\\
W(j-1\leq j)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j\geq i+2.\end{cases}

Lemma 4.18
.

If
\sigma_{\omega,\alpha}^{s}
and
\sigma_{\omega^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{r}
are both defined
on
(W,t)
and
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
, then the operations commute:

\sigma_{\omega,\alpha}^{s}(\sigma_{\omega^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{r}(W,t))=\sigma_{\omega^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}^{r}(\sigma_{\omega,\alpha}^{s}(W,t)).

Proof.

After applying both operations we obtain a point
(W^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime})

where
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}=(t_{0},\dots,t_{i},s,r,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})
, if
s\leq r
.
If
s\neq r
, then it is a straight-forward check that the order does not matter.
In the case
s=r
there is a repetition in
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}
and we may use the
simplicial relation to delete one of them by composing the relevant morphisms.
To see that the operations commute we hence need to check that:

(\mathrm{id}_{W(i)\cup\{\alpha\}\setminus\{\omega^{\prime}\}}\amalg P_{\omega^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{W(i)\setminus\{\omega\}}\amalg P_{\omega,\alpha})=(\mathrm{id}_{W(i)\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}\setminus\{\omega\}}\amalg P_{\omega,\alpha})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{W(i)\setminus\{\omega^{\prime}\}}\amalg P_{\omega^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}).

For
\omega\neq\omega^{\prime}
this follows from the general fact that
morphisms with disjoint support commute.
In the more interesting case
\omega=\omega^{\prime}
we need to show that

(\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\amalg P_{\omega,\alpha^{\prime}})\circ P_{\omega,\alpha}=(\mathrm{id}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\amalg P_{\omega,\alpha})\circ P_{\omega,\alpha^{\prime}}:\{\omega\}\to\{\omega,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\}.

Letting
A:=F(\omega)
both of these morphisms correspond to

(\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes P_{A})\circ P_{A}:A\to O\otimes O\otimes A

with the only difference being in how we identify
F(\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\})
with
O\otimes O
.
To prove the lemma we need to show that the morphism is symmetric in the two
O
s
in the sense that:

(\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes P_{A})\circ P_{A}=(\beta_{O,O}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes P_{A})\circ P_{A}.

As illustrated in figure
7
this follows from the first and third
naturality condition we imposed on the morphisms
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A
in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Figure 7:
When applying
P_{A}
twice the order does not matter.

Definition 4.19
.

Consider a point
(W,\mathbf{t})\in|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
, a surgery path
p
for
W
,
an element
\alpha\in\Omega_{O}
disjoint to
W
, and a parameter
r\in[0,1]
.
Then we define the basic surgery of
(W,\mathbf{t})
along the path
p
to be

K_{(p,\alpha)}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t})=\sigma_{p_{\Omega}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)}(W,\mathbf{t}).

Whenever
p_{\Omega}(r)
is not defined because
p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)\in\{0,\dots,n+1\}

we choose either the most recent well-defined value
p_{\Omega}^{-}(r)

or the next possible well-defined value
p_{\Omega}^{+}(r)
.
In lemma
4.21
we show
that this choice does not matter.
For a surgery datum
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
on
W
we define

K_{A}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t})=(K_{(p^{\alpha_{1}},\alpha_{1})}^{r}\circ\dots\circ K_{(p^{\alpha_{a}},\alpha_{a})}^{r})(W,\mathbf{t})

where we choose any enumeration of
A=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{a}\}
.

Figure 8:
A slide-show depiction of the homotopy
K_{p,\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t})

along the surgery path
p
indicated in the first picture,
as
s
goes from
0
to
1
. See remark
4.20
.

Figure 9:
A slide-show depiction of the homotopy
K_{p,\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t})

along a surgery path
p
that starts at
1
.

Remark 4.20
.

Figure
8
illustrates the homotopy
K_{l,p,\alpha}^{s}(W,\mathbf{t})
in the case
\alpha\in A^{\rm in}
.
The morphisms in the simplex
W
are
f:\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{3}\to\omega_{3}
,

g:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to\omega_{1}
, and
h:\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{3}\to\omega_{2}
.
Throughout the homotopy we see other morphisms

f^{\prime}=P_{\omega_{3},\alpha}\circ f=(\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\otimes f)\circ(P_{\alpha,\omega_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{3}})
,

g^{\prime}=P_{\omega_{1},\alpha}\circ g
, and

h^{\prime}=(\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\otimes h)\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes P_{\alpha,\omega_{3}})=(\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\otimes h)\circ(P_{\alpha,\omega_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{3}})
.
The key step of lemma
4.21
is to show that the two different
expressions we have for
f^{\prime}
and for
h^{\prime}
yield the same morphisms.

See also figure
9
, for an illustration of the case
\alpha\not\in A^{\rm in}
,
where the surgery path starts at
p_{\mathbb{R}}(0)=1
.
Note that in this case we do not need to start out with a disjoint copy of
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}
on top of the diagram.
This difference will play a role when gluing the homotopies in proposition
4.23
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Definition
4.19
defines a continuous map

K:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad((W,(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},\mathbf{t}),r)\mapsto K_{A}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t}).

Proof.

We will proceed similarly to lemma
4.16
.
If we fix an
n
-simplex
W\in C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
and a single surgery path
(p,\alpha)

for
W
, then definition
4.17
gives us continuous maps:

U_{i}:=\{(\mathbf{t},r)\in|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]\;|\;p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\}\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad(\mathbf{t},r)\mapsto\sigma_{p_{\Omega}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)}(W,t).

The sets
U_{0},\dots,U_{n}
are finite polyhedra that cover
|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]

and the key step of the proof will be to show that the map is consistent on overlaps.
Let us consider those points where
p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)=t_{i}
, i.e.
V_{i}=U_{i-1}\cap U_{i}
.
For such an
(\mathbf{t},r)\in V_{i}
the value of
K_{(l,p,\alpha)}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t})
is ambiguous
as we can either use
\sigma_{p_{\Omega}^{+}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)}

or
\sigma_{p_{\Omega}^{-}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)}
.

For either choice the resulting tuple
(W^{\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})
has a repeated value in
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

and we may use the relation
(W^{\prime},\delta^{i}\mathbf{t})\sim(d_{i}W,\mathbf{t})
to rewrite this
point of the geometric realisation
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
. The result is:

W^{\prime\prime}(j)=\begin{cases}W(j)&\text{ for }j\leq i\\
W(j)\amalg\{\alpha\}&\text{ for }j\geq i\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{\prime\prime}(j\leq j+1)=\begin{cases}W(j\leq j+1)&\text{ for }j\leq i-1\\
X_{\pm}&\text{ for }j=i\\
W(j\leq j+1)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}&\text{ for }j\geq i+1.\end{cases}

Here the cobordism
X_{\pm}
depends on whether we use
p_{\Omega}^{+}(r)
or
p_{\Omega}^{-}(r)

and we need to show that
X_{+}=X_{-}
.
There are four possible cases one needs to check, depending on whether
the values
p_{\Omega}^{\pm}(r)
correspond to incoming or outgoing boundary components
of the cospan
[W(i-1)\to W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)\leftarrow W(i)]
.

Each of the cases follows from the conditions on the morphisms
P_{A}:A\to O\otimes A

as stated in definition
2.42
.
Let us for example consider the case where
p_{\Omega}^{+}(r)\in W(i+1)

and
p_{\Omega}^{-}(r)\in W(i)
.
Then

X_{+}=W(i\leq i+1)\cup_{W(i+1)}(\mathrm{id}_{W(i+1)}\amalg P_{p_{\Omega}^{+}(r),\alpha})\qquad X_{-}=(\mathrm{id}_{W(i)}\amalg P_{p_{\Omega}^{-}(r),\alpha})\cup_{W(i)}(W(i\leq i+1)\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}).

To check that
X_{-}=X_{+}
we only need to consider the connected component
of the morphism
W(i\leq i+1):W(i)\to W(i+1)
corresponding to
p(1)
.
Without loss of generality we may therefore assume that
W(i\leq i+1)

is connected.
Write
M:=F(W(i)\setminus\{p_{\Omega}^{-}(r)\})
,
N:=F(W(i+1)\setminus\{p_{\Omega}^{+}(r)\})
,

A^{\pm}:=F(p_{\Omega}^{\pm}(r))
, and
U:=W(i\leq i+1):A^{-}\otimes M\to A^{+}\otimes N
.
By definition
2.42
we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{-}\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A^{-}\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{+}\otimes N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\otimes A^{+}\otimes N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A^{-}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A^{+}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}}

In this diagram the two ways of going from the top left to the bottom right
are exactly the morphisms

F(W(i))=A^{-}\otimes M\to O\otimes A^{+}\otimes N=F(W(i+1)\amalg\{\alpha\})

that represent
X_{-}
and
X_{+}
. Hence it indeed follows from
definition
2.42
that
X_{-}=X_{+}
.
Similar arguments apply in the other three cases.
(Two of the cases agree after swapping
p_{\Omega}^{+}(r)
and
p_{\Omega}^{-}(r)
.)

We have now shown that the continuous maps on the
U_{i}
fit together
on the overlaps and yield a continuous map:

|\Delta^{n}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad(\mathbf{t},r)\mapsto\sigma_{p_{\Omega}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(r)}(W,t).

Similar to lemma
4.16
we see that this is compatible
with face maps. Hence it yields a well-defined map

|C_{\bullet}^{s}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
for
C_{\bullet}^{s}
the semisimplicial set
where each
W
comes equipped with a single surgery path.

Given a collection of surgery paths
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}

it follows from lemma
4.18
that the operations

\sigma_{p_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(r),\alpha}^{p_{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha}(r)}(\underline{\ \ })
commute for varying
\alpha
.
Therefore we can pick any order on
A
and
compute
K:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
by applying the map

|C_{\bullet}^{s}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
multiple times.
It follows that
K
is well-defined and continuous.
∎

We can now concatenate the two homotopies to obtain the basic surgery.

Definition 4.22
.

The homotopy
\mathcal{S}
is defined as follows:

\mathcal{S}:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\times[0,1]\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|,\quad((W,(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A},\mathbf{t}),r)\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{A}^{r}(W,\mathbf{t}):=\begin{cases}\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{1-2r}(W,\mathbf{t})&\text{ for }0\leq r\leq\tfrac{1}{2},\\
K_{A}^{2r-1}(W,\mathbf{t})&\text{ for }\tfrac{1}{2}\leq r\leq 1.\end{cases}

To conclude this section we record the fact that the homotopy
\mathcal{S}
we constructed
has all the desired properties.

Proposition 4.23
.

The above defines a continuous family of maps
\mathcal{S}^{r}:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|

satisfying:

(i)

\mathcal{S}^{0}:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
is the realisation of
the map
C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}\to C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
that forgets the surgery data.

(ii)

\mathcal{S}^{1}:|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
factors through the subspace

|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\subset|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
.

(iii)

Precomposed with the inclusion
|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}|
that equips
the empty surgery data, the family
\mathcal{S}^{r}
is the standard inclusion

|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
for all
r
.

Proof.

We have seen in lemma
4.16
and
4.21
that
the two homotopies
\rho_{A}^{s}
and
K_{A}^{s^{\prime}}
are well-defined and continuous.
So to check that
\mathcal{S}
is well-defined and continuous we only need to verify
that
\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{0}(W,\mathbf{t})=K_{A}^{0}(W,\mathbf{t})
holds for all
A
and
(W,\mathbf{t})
.
Indeed both sides can be described as
(W\amalg\mathrm{id}_{A^{\rm in}},\mathbf{t})
.
To see this note that property (ii) for surgery paths says that
p_{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha}(0)=0
or
n+1
.
For those
\alpha
where
p_{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha}(1)=n+1
the operation
\sigma^{p_{\mathbb{R}}(1)}_{p_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(1),\alpha}

does not do anything and in the case
p_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(1)=0
we have

\sigma^{p_{\Omega}(1)}_{p_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(1),\alpha}(W,\mathbf{t})=(W\amalg\mathrm{id}_{\alpha},\mathbf{t})
.

In claim (i) the map
\mathcal{S}^{0}
is by definition
\rho_{A^{\rm in}}^{1}
, which can easily
be seen to map
(W,\mathbf{t})
to
(W,\mathbf{t})
. So all it does is forgetting
the surgery data. Claim (iii) is similarly straightforward:
if the surgery data is empty then neither
\rho_{\emptyset}
nor
K_{\emptyset}

change anything about
(W,\mathbf{t})
.

Claim (ii) is the most interesting one.
Before reading the proof it is useful to look at figure
8

and think about why the final picture is a simplex in the nerve of the positive
boundary category. The following proof is just a formalisation of that
visual verification.
We need to show that
(W^{\prime},\mathbf{t}^{\prime})=K_{A}^{1}(W,\mathbf{t})
is positive boundary for any
n
simplex
W

and surgery datum
A
. Since
p_{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha}(1)
is some integer
i_{\alpha}
for any surgery
path
p^{\alpha}
we have that
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}=\mathbf{t}
and the simplex
W^{\prime}

after the surgery can be described as:

W^{\prime}(j)=W(j)\amalg\{\alpha\in A\;|\;i_{\alpha}\leq j\}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{\prime}(j-1\leq j)=P_{A}^{j}\cup W(j-1\leq j)\amalg\{\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}\;|\;i_{\alpha}<j\}

Here
P_{A}^{j}
is the morphism

W(j-1)\amalg\{\alpha\;|\;i_{\alpha}\leq j-1\}\to W(j-1)\amalg\{\alpha\;|\;i_{\alpha}\leq j\}

obtained by suitably gluing all those

P_{p_{\rm end}^{\alpha},\alpha}:\{p_{\rm end}^{\alpha}\}\to\{p_{\rm end}^{\alpha},\alpha\}

where
i_{\alpha}=j-1
.
(Note that the order of glueing does not matter by lemma
4.18
.)

For
W^{\prime}
to be positive boundary we need to check that for any
j
and

u\in W_{\pi}^{\prime}(j-1\to j)
there is some
x\in W^{\prime}(j)
that maps to
u
.
Any such component
u
is either an identity
\mathrm{id}_{\alpha}
that we added,
or a component of
P_{A}^{j}\cup W(j-1\to j)
. In the first case it is clearly
positive boundary.
In the second case consider the corresponding
u^{\prime}\in W_{\pi}(j-1\leq j)
.
If this was already in the image of
W(j)
, then so is
u
and we are done.
If
u^{\prime}
is not positive boundary, then the connectivity condition that we imposed
on surgery data implies that there is an
\beta\in A
for which

p_{\rm end}^{\beta-}=u^{\prime}
ends at
u^{\prime}
.
In the glued morphism
P_{A}^{j}\cup W(j-1\leq j)
the element
u^{\prime}
is
equivalent to
\beta
coming from
P_{p_{\rm end}^{\beta},\beta}
,
since the morphism
P_{p_{\rm end}^{\beta},\beta}
is connected.
So
u^{\prime}\in W_{\pi}^{\prime}(j-1\leq j)
is the image of

\beta\in W^{\prime}(j)=W(j)\amalg\{\alpha\in A\;|\;i_{\alpha}\leq j\}
.
So
W^{\prime}(j-1\leq j)
is indeed positive boundary.
This proves claim (ii).
∎

4.3
Contractible surgery data

One problem we have not addressed yet is how to choose the surgery data

(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}

for a given point
(W,t)
in
|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
.
Concretely, the problem is that the forgetful map
C_{\bullet}^{\sigma}\to C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}

will not induce an equivalence on realisations.
To resolve this we need to interpolate between different choices of surgery data.
This is not really a problem as the operators
K_{A}^{\lambda}
and
K_{A^{\prime}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
commute.

The following definition allows for multiple pieces of surgery data.

Definition 4.24
.

The bi-semi-simplicial set
C_{\bullet,\bullet}^{\sigma}
has as
(n,m)
-simplices
tuples
(W,((p_{0}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{0}},\dots,(p_{m}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{m}}))

where
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}
and each
(p_{i}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{i}}

is a surgery datum for
W
, such that
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are disjoint for
i\neq j
.
The
i
-th face operators in the second direction is defined by forgetting

(p_{i}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{i}}
.

We can always find surgery data that is disjoint to all previous surgery,
which makes it very easy to show contractibility.

Lemma 4.25
.

For any
n
the augmentation of
C_{n,\bullet}^{\sigma}
induces an equivalence

|C_{n,\bullet}^{\sigma}|\simeq C_{n}^{\rm nc}
.

Proof.

We will derive this from
[
GRW14
, Theorem 6.2]
though it should be remarked
that the statement we are proving here is much simpler than the full generality
to which their theorem applies.
The augmented semi-simplicial set
C_{n,\bullet}^{\sigma}\to C_{n}^{\rm nc}
is essentially
by definition an augmented topological flag complex in the sense of

[
GRW14
, Definition 6.1]
. Since it is discrete, the first condition
of the theorem is trivially satisfied.
The the third condition is also easy to see:
if
(p_{0}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{0}},\dots,(p_{m}^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A_{m}}
is a sequence of surgery data we can define a new surgery datum
that is disjoint to all these by simply copying the first one.
Concretely, we choose a subset
A^{\prime}\subset\Omega
that is disjoint to
W
and
all of the
A_{i}
and pick a bijection
\varphi:A^{\prime}\cong A_{0}
.
Then
p_{n+1}^{\alpha}:=p_{0}^{\varphi(\alpha)}
defines a surgery datum indexed by
\alpha\in A^{\prime}

and is disjoint to all the others by construction.

Finally, the second condition simply states that every
n
-simplex
W\in C_{n}^{\rm nc}

admits at least one surgery datum. We will show this by constructing one.
Let
A\subset\Omega_{O}
be a subset disjoint to
W
and big enough
so that we may choose a surjection

f:A\twoheadrightarrow\coprod_{i=1}^{n}W_{\pi}(i-1\leq i)\subset|W|
.
Since
W\in C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}
every connected component of
|W|
is connected
to either
\bot\sim[W(0)\times\{0\}]
or
\top\sim[W(n)\times\{n+1\}]
.
We can therefore find piece-wise linear paths
p^{\alpha}:[0,1]\to|W|

such that
p^{\alpha}(0)\in\{\bot,\top\}
and
p^{\alpha}(1)=f(\alpha)
.
This trivially satisfies the connectivity condition because
it hits all components of morphisms, in particular those that are not
positive boundary. Therefore
(p^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is the desired
surgery datum for
W
.
∎

We now need to construct the surgery homotopy
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
dependent
on a weighted collection of surgery data.
To do so let
\|C_{\bullet,\bullet}^{\sigma}\|
denote the geometric realisation
of the semisimplicial space
[n]\mapsto|C_{n,\bullet}^{\sigma}|
.

Definition 4.26
.

We define a continuous map

\mathcal{S}:\|C_{\bullet,\bullet}^{\sigma}\|\times[0,1]\longrightarrow|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|.

A point on the left is represented by a tuple

((W,\mathbf{t}),((A_{0},\dots,A_{m}),(s_{0},\dots,s_{m})),r)

where the
s_{i}\in[0,1]
are such that
\sum_{i}s_{i}=1
.
We normalise them as
\overline{s}_{i}:=s_{i}/\max(s_{0},\dots,s_{m})
and define:

\mathcal{S}^{r}((W,\mathbf{t}),(A_{0},\dots,A_{m}),(s_{0},\dots,s_{m})):=(\mathcal{S}_{A_{0}}^{r\cdot\overline{s}_{0}}\circ\dots\circ\mathcal{S}_{A_{m}}^{r\cdot\overline{s}_{m}})(W,\mathbf{t}).

Proof of theorem
4.1
.

We begin by noting that the map
\mathcal{S}
defined above is indeed well-defined.
This was mostly covered in the previous section
(see proposition
4.23
, lemma
4.16
, and lemma
4.21
),
but we also need to check that it is well-defined with respect to face maps
in the new semi-simplicial direction. Concretely this means that for
s_{i}=0

we need to be able to forget
A_{i}
without changing the value of
\mathcal{S}
.
Indeed this is the case as
\mathcal{S}^{0}
acts as the identity by part (i)
of proposition
4.23
.

Now that we know that
\mathcal{S}^{r}
is a continuous family of continuous maps

\|C_{\bullet,\bullet}^{\sigma}\|\to|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
we can fit it into the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|C_{\bullet,\bullet}^{\sigma}\|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{1}}

The map
I
is defined by equipping
(W,t)
with the empty surgery
A_{0}=\{\emptyset\}
.
This is possible because
(W,t)
is already positive boundary in this case.
By part (ii) of
4.23

\mathcal{S}^{1}
indeed lands in the subspace

|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|\subset|C_{\bullet}^{\rm nc}|
, so the dashed map exists.
For the empty surgery the map
\mathcal{S}^{1}
does not do anything
by part (iii) of
4.23
and so the top-left triangle commutes.
By construction
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
is a homotopy for the bottom-right triangle in the diagram.
Moreover, part (i) of
4.23
tells us that the map
\mathcal{S}^{0}

simply forgets the surgery data and we saw in lemma
4.25

that this map is an equivalence.

In summary the above diagram is homotopy commutative and the right-hand
vertical map is a homotopy equivalence. From this it follows formally from
the
2
-out-of-
6
property that all other maps in the diagram are weak equivalences.
We can check this by hand:
Pick any base-points and apply
\pi_{k}
to the diagram.
Considering the top-left triangle we see that the diagonal map
\pi_{k}(\mathcal{S}^{1})

has to be surjective because the identity on
\pi_{k}|C_{\bullet}^{\partial_{+}}|
factors through it.
Similarly, the homotopy commutativity of the bottom-right triangle
and the fact that
\pi_{k}(\mathcal{S}^{0})
is an isomorphism implies that
\pi_{k}(\mathcal{S}^{1})

is injective. Hence we have shown that
\pi_{k}(\mathcal{S}^{1})
is an isomorphism
and it follows quickly that all the other maps are, too.
∎

4.4
The positive boundary subcategory

In this section we compute the classifying space of the positive boundary subcategory

\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\subset\mathcal{C}
for all weighted cospan categories.
This includes the three cases
\mathrm{Csp}
,
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
that appear in our main theorems.

Proposition 4.27
.

For any weighting monoid
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
the classifying space of the positive boundary
subcategory
\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)^{\partial_{+}}\subset\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
is

B(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)^{\partial_{+}})\simeq BA

and the inclusion
BA\simeq B(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)^{\partial_{+}})\to B(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha))

admits a splitting as an infinite loop space map.

Proof.

In the case of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}
the equivalence
B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}\simeq S^{1}

was shown in
[
Til96
, Proposition 6]
and the splitting constructed
in
[
Til96
, Theorem 10]
.
We begin by recalling the constructions made there so we can modify
them for our purposes.

There is a functor
\Phi:\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}\to\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}
that sends any object to
S^{1}

and that sends a cobordism
W:M\to N
to the unique cobordism
\Phi(W):S^{1}\to S^{1}

that is connected and has genus:

g(\Phi(W))=\tfrac{1}{2}(|\pi_{0}(N)|-|\pi_{0}(M)|+\chi(W))=g(W)+|\pi_{0}(N)|-|\pi_{0}(W)|.

This is always a non-negative integer as
\pi_{0}(N)\to\pi_{0}(W)

is always a surjection for
W
in the positive boundary subcategory.
Using that the Euler characteristic is additive under gluing of even-dimensional
manifolds one checks that this is functorial.
There is a natural transformation
\rho:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}}\Rightarrow\Phi

defined by letting
\rho_{M}:M\to S^{1}
be the unique connected genus
0
cobordism.

Now we can consider the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}

on the object
S^{1}
. By recording the genus as a natural number
\mathcal{C}_{1}

is isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}
as a category with one object and hence
B\mathcal{C}_{1}\simeq S^{1}
.
The functor
\Phi
lands in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and defines a retraction

B\Phi:B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}\to B\mathcal{C}_{1}
to the inclusion.
The natural transformation
\rho
gives a homotopy between the identity and

B\Phi:B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}\to B\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{\partial_{+}}
and hence the inclusion and

B\Phi
form a homotopy equivalence.

We will now generalise this argument to the weighted cospan category

\mathcal{C}:=\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}
denote
the full subcategory on the object
*
. This category has a single object
and morphisms
A
, so
B\mathcal{C}_{1}=BA
.
The functor
\Phi:\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\to\mathcal{C}_{1}
again sends all objects to the single object
*
,
and on morphisms it is defined by sending
(M\to W\leftarrow N,a:W\to A)

to the cospan
(*\to*\leftarrow*)
weighted by:

\Phi(W)=\sum_{x\in W}a(W)+\alpha\cdot(|N|-|W|).

The number
|N|-|W|
is again non-negative as
N\to W
is surjective
in the positive boundary category. One checks that this is functorial
by using the definition of the composite weighting in definition
2.13
.

To define the natural transformation
\rho:\operatorname{Id}\Rightarrow\Phi

we let
\rho_{M}:(M\to*\leftarrow*,a:*\to A)
where the labeling is
a(*)=\alpha
.
(We cannot set the labelling to be
0
like we did for the surface category case,
as this might not be an allowed cospan for
M=\emptyset
.)
Naturality is checked by inserting the definitions.
By the same argument as above it now follows that
BA=B\mathcal{C}_{1}\to B\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}

is a homotopy equivalence.

To construct the splitting of
B\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\to B\mathcal{C}

let
A^{\rm gp}
be the group completion of
A
.
Define a functor
\Phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to A^{\rm gp}
by sending a morphism
W:M\to N
to

\Phi^{\prime}(W)=\sum_{x\in W}a(W)+\alpha\cdot(|N|-|W|).

The number
|N|-|W|
can now be negative, but this is fine because we have
a formal inverse
-\alpha
for
\alpha
in
A^{\rm gp}
.

\Phi^{\prime}
clearly extends
\Phi
and it is functorial for the same reason that
\Phi
was.
This yields the desired splitting:
the composite
BA=B\mathcal{C}_{1}\to B\mathcal{C}^{\partial_{+}}\to B\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\Phi^{\prime}}B(A^{\rm gp})

is the standard map
BA\to B(A^{\rm gp})
, which is an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 4.28
.

The classifying space of a weighted cospan category is:

B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{sp}(A,A_{1},\alpha))\simeq BA\times Q\left(\bigvee\nolimits_{a\in A}S^{2}B(\mathcal{F}_{a}(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)))\right).

Proof.

The surgery theorem applies and by proposition
4.27
the left-hand map
in theorem
F
(corollary
2.45
)
admits a splitting as infinite loop space map.
Therefore the middle term decomposes as a product of
B(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)^{\partial_{+}})

and the free infinite loop spaces on the
\Sigma S(B\mathcal{F}_{a})
.
∎

5
Factorisation categories and filtered graphs

Our work in the previous sections shows that computing the classifying space

B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
of the
(\chi\leq 0)
-surface category essentially reduces to understanding
its factorisation categories, which we will denote by

\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}:=\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{g}}(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
.
In this section we show that on classifying spaces
\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}

is equivalent to a certain finite category
J_{g}
of weighted graphs of genus
g
,
which was introduced in
[
CGP16
]
7
7

7

Note that as discussed in remark
5.11
our version
J_{g}

differs from their version in that we remove the terminal object
\bullet_{g}
.

and plays an important role
in studying the moduli spaces
\Delta_{g}
of tropical curves of genus
g
and volume
1
.
Concretely we show:

Theorem 5.1
.

For
g\geq 2
there is an equivalence
B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}\simeq BJ_{g}

and for
g=1
we have
B\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\chi\leq 0}\simeq BO(2)
.

In section
6
we will also see that for
g\geq 2
,

BJ_{g}
is rationally equivalent to
\Delta_{g}
.
In the case
g=1
we expect that
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\chi\leq 0}
is equivalent
to
\mathcal{F}_{[S^{1}]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1}^{\rm unor})
.
Just like we showed in
[
Ste21
, Section 8]
that
\mathcal{F}_{[S^{1}]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1})

is equivalent to Connes’ cyclic category
\Lambda
,
it should be possible to show that
\mathcal{F}_{[S^{1}]}(\mathrm{Cob}_{1}^{\rm unor})

is the dihedral version of Connes’ cyclic category.
This dihedral category is known to have classifying space
BO(2)
,
see
[
Lod87
, Proposition 3.11]
.

However, the approach we take to prove the theorem will be
to treat the
g=1
and
g\geq 2
computation of
B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}

in the same framework by considering a category of topological graphs
\mathcal{J}_{g}

such that
\mathcal{J}_{g}\simeq J_{g}
for
g\geq 2
and
\mathcal{J}_{1}\simeq O(2)
.
In fact we prove the following more general theorem,
which describes the factorisation category of the weighted cospan category

\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
in the sense of
2.13
.
This recovers theorem
5.1
because
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\simeq\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},1)
.

Theorem 5.2
.

For any weighting monoid
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
and
a\in A
there is an equivalence:

B\mathcal{F}_{a}(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha))\simeq B\mathcal{J}_{a}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}.

Note that this will only be interesting for
0\not\in A_{1}
as otherwise
an argument similar to lemma
3.50

shows that
B\mathcal{F}_{a}(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A,\alpha))\simeq*
.

5.1
Categories of graphs

5.1.1
Topological graphs

We begin by defining a category of weighted topological graphs.
Throughout this section we fix a weighting monoid
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
.
Recall that this means that
A
is an abelian monoid,
A_{1}\subset A

is a subset closed under
A_{1}+A\subset A_{1}
, and
\alpha\in A
is an element.
The most relevant case is that of
(A,A_{1},\alpha)=(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},1)

and it will suffice to think of this case.

Definition 5.3
.

An
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted
topological graph
is a tuple
(X,w)

of a topological space
X
and a (non-continuous) map
w:X\to A
such that

•

X
is homeomorphic to a connected
1
-dimensional CW complex
with finitely many cells and is not a point.

•

The set
X_{w}:=\{x\in X\;|\;w(x)\neq 0\}
is finite.

•

For any
x\in X
with
\nu(x)=1
we have
w(x)\in A_{1}
.

Here we define the
valence

\nu(x)\in\mathbb{N}
to be

\nu(x):=|\pi_{0}(U\setminus x)|

where
U\subset X
is a small contractible neighbourhood of
x
.

The
singular set

X_{\rm sing}\subset X
is defined as the set of
points of valence
\nu(x)\neq 2
.

The
genus
of
(X,w)
is defined as:

g(X,w):=\sum_{x\in X_{w}}w(x)+b_{1}(X)\cdot\alpha\in A.

A
subgraph
of
(X,w)
is a tuple
(Y,w^{\prime})
such that
Y\subset X

is a closed subset with
\partial Y\subset X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing}
and
w^{\prime}=w_{|Y}
.

Definition 5.4
.

The topologically enriched category
\mathcal{J}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

has as objects
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted topological graphs
and as morphisms
(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
continuous maps

f:X\to Y
such that for all
y\in Y
the preimage
f^{-1}(y)

is either a single point of the same weight as
y
or
a connected subgraph of
(X,w)
with genus

g(f^{-1}(y),w_{|f^{-1}(y)})=w^{\prime}(y)
.
We topologise the space of morphisms with the compact-open topology.

Example 5.5
.

The only
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted topological graph
(X,w)

with
X_{w}=\emptyset=X_{\rm sing}
is the circle
(S^{1},0)
.
Since this cannot have any subgraphs the fibers of morphisms
f:(S^{1},0)\to(S^{1},0)

have to be single points. It follows that
f
is in fact a homeomorphism.
So
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}((S^{1},0),(S^{1},0))=\mathit{Homeo}(S^{1})\simeq O(2)
.
We will see that this case is the exception and that in general
the spaces
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}((X,w),(Y,w^{\prime}))
have contractible components.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any two topological graphs
(X,w)
and
(Y,w^{\prime})
with
(Y,w^{\prime})\not\cong(S^{1},0)

the space of morphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}((X,w),(Y,w^{\prime}))
has contractible components.
If
(Y,w^{\prime})\cong(S^{1},0)
, then the components are equivalent to
S^{1}
.

Proof.

Since each map
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w)
is a surjective map between compact Hausdorff
spaces, it is also a quotient map. We can therefore factor it as

X\to(X/\!\sim_{f})\cong Y
where
\sim_{f}
is the equivalence relation
defined by
(x\sim_{f}x^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow(f(x)=f(x^{\prime}))

and the second map is a homeomorphism.
Let us call an equivalence relation
\sim\subset X^{2}
admissible
if each of its equivalence classes is either a single point
or a subgraph of
(X,w)
, and let
\mathrm{Eq}(X,w)
denote the set
of admissible equivalence relations.
For such an equivalence relation, let
w_{\sim}:X/\!\sim\to A
be the function
that sends and equivalence class
[x]
to the genus
g([x])
of the subgraph.

We will show that sending a map
f
to the equivalence relation
\sim_{f}

and the isomorphism
[f]:(X/\!\sim,w_{\sim})\cong(Y,w^{\prime})
induces
a homeomorphism:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}((X,w),(Y,w^{\prime}))\cong\coprod_{\sim\in\mathrm{Eq}(X,w)}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\cong}((X/\sim,w_{\sim}),(Y,w^{\prime})).

We begin by noting that the set of admissible equivalence relations
is finite because the set of subgraphs of
(X,w)
is finite.
One can check, using the definition of the compact-open topology,
that the assignment
f\mapsto\sim_{f}
is continuous
with respect to the discrete topology on
\mathrm{Eq}(X,w)
.
Similarly, one checks that for each
\sim\in\mathrm{Eq}(X,w)
the
assignment
f\mapsto[f]
is continuous.
Since there is a continuous inverse given by

(\sim,[f])\mapsto(X\to X/\sim\to Y)
it follows that the map
is indeed a homeomorphism.

To determine the homotopy type of the connected components it therefore
suffices to study the group of automorphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\cong}((Y,w^{\prime}),(Y,w^{\prime}))
.
This the subgroup of the homeomorphism group
\mathit{Homeo}(Y)
containing
precisely those homeomorphism
f:Y\cong Y
with
w^{\prime}\circ f=w^{\prime}
.
If
(Y,w^{\prime})=(S^{1},0)
, then this is exactly the group of self-homeomorphism
of the circle, which is homotopy equivalent to
O(2)
.
In the case where
(Y,w^{\prime})\not\cong(S^{1},0)
,
we can find some
y\in Y
with either
w(y)\neq 0
or
\nu(y)\neq 2
.
Consider the group
\mathit{Homeo}(Y,Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing})
of homeomorphisms of
Y

that send the subset
Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}
to itself, possibly permuting it.
This contains the automorphism group we are interested in as a union
of connected components.
Since
Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}
is non-empty, the components of

\mathit{Homeo}(Y,Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing})
are all homeomorphic to

\prod_{i}\mathit{Homeo}([0,1]\text{ rel }\{0,1\})
, which is contractible.
∎

5.1.2
Combinatorial graphs

We now give a combinatorial version of the notion of an
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted graph.
This is based on the notation of
[
CGP16
, section 2.2]
and recovers their definitions
in the case of
(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{1},1)
.

Definition 5.7
.

A
graph

G
is a finite set
X
together with maps
s:X\to X
and
r:X\to X

such that
s\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{X}
,
r\circ r=r
, and satisfying that
for all
x\in X
we have
s(x)=x
if and only if
r(x)=x
.
The set
X
decomposes as the disjoint union
of the set of vertices
V_{G}:=\{x\in X\;|\;r(x)=x\}

and the set of half-edges
H_{G}=\{x\in X\;|\;r(x)\neq x\}
.
The map
r
sends a half-edge to its root and the map
s
sends a half-edge
to its partner half-edge; both send vertices to themselves.
We define the set of edges as
E_{G}=\{\{h,s(h)\}\;|\;h\in H_{G}\}
.

A graph
G
is
connected
if the equivalence relation on
X

generated by
x\sim s(x)
and
x\sim r(x)
has a single equivalence class.
We define the
valence
of a vertex
v
in a graph to be the number of
incident half-edges:
\operatorname{val}(v):=|r^{-1}(v)\setminus\{v\}|
.

Example 5.8
.

The graph with one vertex and one loop

can be defined in the above context
as
X=\{v,h,h^{\prime}\}
with
s(h)=h^{\prime}
,
s(h^{\prime})=h
,
r(h)=r(h^{\prime})=v
,
s(v)=r(v)=v
.
This has one vertex
v
, two half-edges
h
and
h^{\prime}
, and a single edge
\{h,h^{\prime}\}
.

We can also consider combinatorial graphs weighted by some
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
.
The most important weighting monoid is
(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},1)
,
and we will usually think about this case.

Definition 5.9
.

An
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-
weighted graph
is a graph
G
together with a function

w:V_{G}\to A
such that for any vertex
v\in V_{G}
of valence
1
we have
w(v)\in A_{1}

and for any vertex
v^{\prime}\in V_{G}
of valence
2
we have
w(v^{\prime})\neq 0
.
We define the genus of a connected weighted graph
(G,w)
to be

g(G,w)=(|E_{G}|-|V_{G}|+1)\cdot\alpha+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}w(v)\in A.

We define the relevant notion of morphisms between weighted graphs:

Definition 5.10
.

Let
(G,w)=((X,s,r),w)
and
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime})=((X^{\prime},s^{\prime},r^{\prime}),w)
be two
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted graphs.
A
morphism
of
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted graphs
q:(G,w)\to(G^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a map

q:X\to X^{\prime}
satisfying
q\circ s=s^{\prime}\circ q
and
q\circ r=r^{\prime}\circ q
,
such that for each half-edge
h\in H_{G^{\prime}}
the preimage
q^{-1}(h)

consists of a single half-edge of
G

and for each vertex
v\in V_{G^{\prime}}
the preimage
q^{-1}(v)
is a connected subgraph
of
G
with genus
g(q^{-1}(v),w_{|q^{-1}})=w^{\prime}(v)
.

We let
J^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
denote the category of those
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted graphs
that are connected and have at least one edge, with morphisms defined as above.
For any
g\in A
and we let
J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}\subset J^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

denote the full subcategory on the graphs of genus
g
.
We will often suppress the weighting monoid from the notation.

Remark 5.11
.

In
[
CGP16
, section 2.2]
the authors define a category called
J_{g}
,
on which our definition of the category
J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
is based.
Let us denote their category by
J_{g}^{\rm CGP}
for the purpose of comparing it to ours.
One key difference is that
J_{g}^{\rm CGP}
contains the graph
\bullet_{g}
with
one vertex and no edges. This is a terminal object, which makes the classifying
space
BJ_{g}^{\rm CGP}\simeq*
contractible.
If we remove this terminal object then
J_{g}^{\rm CGP}\setminus\{\bullet_{g}\}

is equivalent to our
J_{g}^{(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)}
.
We will argue in remark
6.5
that our version of
J_{g}

still leads to the same tropical moduli space
\Delta_{g}
as theirs.

Remark 5.12
.

The isomorphisms in this category
\mathcal{J}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
are exactly those

q:(G,w)\to(G^{\prime},w^{\prime})
where the map
q:X\to X^{\prime}
is a bijection.
Moreover, if two graphs have different genus, then there are no morphisms between them
and so we can split
J^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
as a disjoint union of categories

J^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}=\coprod_{g\in A}J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}.

Remark 5.13
.

Note that for general weighting monoids
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
the category

J^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
is not as well-behaved as we would like.
For example in the case of
(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z},1)
-weighted graphs
we cannot collapse an edge between two valence two vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{G}

if they are labelled by
w(v)=k
and
w(v^{\prime})=-k
as the resulting
vertex would be a valence
2
vertex with label
0
.
Similarly, we cannot collapse an edge between two vertices
u,u^{\prime}\in V_{G}

where
u
has valence
1
,
u^{\prime}
has valence
3
and
w(u)=0=w(u^{\prime})
.
The latter example also works in the case of
(0,0,0)
-weighted graphs,
that is relevant for
\mathrm{Csp}=\mathrm{Csp}(0,0,0)
.
This is why we have to impose the conditions in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
be a weighting monoid where the only invertible
element of
A
is
0
and
0\not\in A_{1}
.
Let
g\in A
be some element that is neither
0
nor
\alpha
.
Then there is a functor of topologically enriched categories:

\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}\to J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

which is essentially surjective and an equivalence on Hom-spaces.

Proof.

For a topological graph
(X,w)
we define its set of vertices as

V_{(X,w)}:=X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing}
and, by abuse of notation,
let
w:V_{(X,w)}\to A
denote the restriction of
w
.
Unless the topological graph is
(X,w)\cong(S^{1},0)
this set of vertices
is non-empty. Note that the case
(S^{1},0)
will not come up in what follows
as it has total genus
g(S^{1},0)=\alpha
, which we excluded.
The set of half-edges
H_{(X,w)}
is defined as the set of maps

h:[0,1]\to X
such that
h_{|(0,1)}
is an embedding and

h^{-1}(V_{(X,w)})=\{0,1\}
, up to isotopies respecting these conditions.
The involution
s:H_{(X,w)}\to H_{(X,w)}
is given by
[h]\mapsto[h^{\prime}]

where
h^{\prime}(t)=h(1-t)
and the root map
r:H_{(X,w)}\to V_{(X,w)}

is
[h]\mapsto h(0)
.

We define the functor

F:\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}\to J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

on objects by sending
(X,w)
to the combinatorial graph

(H_{(X,w)}\amalg V_{(X,w)},s,r,w)
.
To a morphism
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
we assign the map

F(f):H_{(X,w)}\amalg V_{(X,w)}\to H_{(Y,w^{\prime})}\amalg V_{(Y,w^{\prime})}

\displaystyle F(f)(v\in V_{(X,w)})

\displaystyle=f(v)\in V_{(Y,w^{\prime})},\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle F(f)([h:[0,1]\to X])

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[f\circ h]\in H_{(Y,w^{\prime})}&\text{ if }(f\circ h)_{|(0,1)}\text{ injective }\\
f(h(0))\in V_{(Y,w^{\prime})}&\text{ if }f\circ h\text{ constant.}\end{cases}

For this to be well-defined we need to check for each

x\in V_{(X,w)}=X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing}
the image
f(x)
lies in

V_{(Y,w)}=Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}
.

Write
y:=f(x)
and consider the preimage
f^{-1}(y)\subset X
.
If it consists of single point then
f
is a homeomorphism near
x

and so
x
and
y
agree in valence and weight, which already implies

y\in Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}
.
We may therefore assume that
f^{-1}
is a subgraph of
(X,w)

that contains at least one edge. If there is any
x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(y)

with
w(x^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
w^{\prime}(y)=w(x^{\prime})+\dots
is non-zero because we
assumed that
A
does not have any invertible elements but
0
.
Similarly, if
f^{-1}(y)
is not contractible, then
b_{1}(f^{-1}(y))>0
and

w^{\prime}(y)=b_{1}(f^{-1}(y))\cdot\alpha+\dots
is non-zero because
\alpha
is not invertible.
So it suffices to consider the case where the subgraph
f^{-1}(y)\subset(X,w)

has trivial weight and is contractible. This means that the subgraph
is a finite tree with at least one edge, so it contains at least two
leaves
l,l^{\prime}\in\partial f^{-1}(y)
whose valence within
f^{-1}(y)
is
1
.
But we have already established that
w(l)=0=w(l^{\prime})
, so
l
and
l^{\prime}

cannot be valence
1
or
2
in
(X,w)
as we assumed
0\not\in A_{1}
.
Hence there are at least four edges of
(X,w)
that do not lie in
f^{-1}(y)
,
but end in
l
and
l^{\prime}
. This implies that
y\in(Y,w^{\prime})
has valence at least
4
