and hence lies in
Y_{\rm sing}
.

To see that the functor
F
is essentially surjective we build,
given a combinatorial graph
(G=(X,s,r),w)
, its geometric realisation as:

|G|:=\left(X\times[0,1]\right)/\sim,\quad\text{ where }\quad(x,1-t)\sim(s(x),t)\quad\text{and}\quad(x,0)\sim(r(x),t).

The set of vertices includes into this as
v\mapsto[v,0]=[v,t]
for any
t
,
and we can define
w:|G|\to A
by extending it to
0
on edges.
The space
|G|
is a one-dimensional CW complex by construction
and it is finite and connected because
G
is.
Moreover, since vertices retain their valence and weight, all valence
1

vertices are still labelled in
A_{1}
and hence
(|G|,w)
is
an
(A,A_{1},\alpha)
-weighted topological graph.
In fact, this construction defines a functor:

|\underline{\ \ }|:J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

and it follows by inspection that
F\circ|\underline{\ \ }|
is naturally isomorphic
to the identity functor.

While the composite functor
|\underline{\ \ }|\circ F
is not on-the-nose isomorphic
to the identity functor, it is still isotopic to it in some sense.
Concretely, we can pick isomorphisms
\alpha_{(X,w)}:(X,w)\cong(|F(X,w)|,w)
.
If we require them to be the identity on vertices and to preserve edges,
then the only choice we have is how to identify the edges relative to their boundary.
There is no canonical choice because the edges of
(X,w)
are not identified
with
[0,1]
, whereas the edges of
|F(X,w)|
are (modulo reflection).
However, any two choices are isotopic because
\mathit{Homeo}([0,1]\text{ rel }\{0,1\})

is contractible. From this one can show that, no matter which choice we make,

\alpha
defines a natural transformation from the identity to
|\underline{\ \ }|\circ F

as functors to the homotopy category
h\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
.
Therefore,
J_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
is equivalent to the homotopy category

h\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
.

To conclude the proof we show that
\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}\to h\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}

is an equivalence on Hom-spaces.
As noted before, our assumption that
g\neq\alpha
implies that none of
the graphs involved are isomorphic to
(S^{1},0)
.
Hence it follows from lemma
5.6
that the components of
the Hom-spaces in
\mathcal{J}_{g}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
are contractible,
which is what we claimed.
∎

5.2
From factorisations to graphs

The purpose of this section is to prove theorem
5.2
,
i.e. to show that there is an equivalence on classifying spaces between
the category of factorisation
\mathcal{F}(\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha))
and the category of
graphs
\mathcal{J}^{(A,A_{1},\alpha)}
. We fix the weighting monoid
(A,A_{1},\alpha)

throughout and suppress it in notation.
The equivalence we will construct does not come from a functor in either direction.
Rather, we define a third category
\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
where objects are topological graphs

(X,w)
together with an “admissible” subset
U\subset X
and
an ordering on
\partial U
.
There are two functors:

\mathcal{J}\longleftarrow\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\ S\ }\mathcal{F}

The one to the left simply forgets the subset
U
,
and the one to the right takes
(X,w,U)
and thinks of it as a factorisation

X=U\cup_{\partial U}(X\setminus U^{\circ})
.
We will show that both functors induce equivalences on classifying spaces.

Definition 5.15
.

A subset
U\subset X
of a weighted topological graph
(X,w)
is called

admissible
if

•

U
is closed and has finitely many components,

•

U
is neither empty nor all of
X
,

•

if
x\in U\cap(X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing})
then
U
is a neighbourhood of
x
.

A boundary ordering for
U
is an injection
\alpha:\{1,\dots,u\}\to X

for some
u\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha(\{1,\dots,u\})=\partial U
.

We let
\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
be the poset where objects are admissible
subset
U\subset X
and morphisms are
U\leq V
if
U\subset V
.
Similarly, we let
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
be the poset where objects
are tuples
(U,\alpha)
of an admissible subset with boundary ordering
\alpha

and morphisms are
(U,\alpha)\leq(V,\beta)
if
U\subset V
.

We topologise
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
with the subspace topology under the inclusion

\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\hookrightarrow\coprod_{u\geq 2}\mathrm{Map}^{\rm inj}(\{1,\dots,u\},X\setminus(X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing}))\times\mathrm{Map}(X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing},\{\bot,\top\})

that sends
(U,\alpha)
to
(\alpha,\chi)
where
(\chi(x)=\top)\Leftrightarrow(x\in U)
.
This inclusion in fact identifies
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
with a union of certain
connected components of the space on the right.
We topologise
\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
with the quotient topology under the map

\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\to\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
that forgets the boundary ordering.

Both
\mathrm{Sub}
and
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
define functors
\mathrm{Sub},\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}:\mathcal{J}^{\rm op}\to\mathit{TopCat}

\mathrm{Sub}(f)(U):=f^{-1}(U)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(f)(U,\alpha):=(f^{-1}(U),f^{-1}\circ\alpha)

to the category of topological categories.

Remark 5.16
.

Let us briefly recall the Grothendieck construction, in the example of
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
.
An object of
\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
is a tuple of an object
(X,w)\in\mathcal{J}

and an object
(U,\alpha)\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
.
It is important to keep in mind that the space of objects of
\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}

is no longer discrete, even though it was for
\mathcal{J}
.
A morphism
((X,w),(U,\alpha))\to((Y,w^{\prime}),(V,\beta))
is a morphism

f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
together with a morphism
(U,\alpha)\to\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(f)(V,\beta)
.
Since
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
is a poset we can can simply say that a morphism

((X,w),(U,\alpha))\to((Y,w^{\prime}),(V,\beta))
is a morphism

f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
such that
U\subset f^{-1}(V)
,
or equivalently such that
f(U)\subset V
.
See the left-hand side of figure
10

for an example of such a morphism.

Figure 10:
The functor
S
evaluated on a morphism
f:(X,U)\to(Y,V)
.
The identifications
\partial U\cong\{1,2,3,4\}
and

\partial V\cong\{1,2,3,4\}
are left implicit.

Definition 5.17
.

We define a functor
S:\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
by sending

(X,w,U,\alpha)
to the factorisation

S((X,w),(U,\alpha)):=\left(\emptyset\to[\pi_{0}(U),w_{U}]\xleftarrow{\alpha}\{1,\dots,u\}\xrightarrow{\alpha}[\pi_{0}(X\setminus U^{\circ}),w_{X\setminus U}]\leftarrow\emptyset\right)

Here the weights
w_{U}:\pi_{0}(U)\to A
and

w_{X\setminus U}:\pi_{0}(X\setminus U^{\circ})\to A

are defined by sending a component to its total genus.

To a morphism
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
with
f(U)\subset V
we assign the cospan

S(f)=\left(\{1,\dots,u\}\xrightarrow{\alpha}[\pi_{0}(f^{-1}(V)\setminus U^{\circ}),w_{V,U}]\xleftarrow{f^{-1}\circ\beta}\{1,\dots,v\}\right)

where
w_{V,U}
sends each component of
V\setminus f(U)^{\circ}

to its total genus.
See figure
10
for an illustration of how this functor
is applied to two objects and a morphism.

Lemma 5.18
.

The construction described above indeed defines a continuous functor

S:\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that
S
is continuous on objects and morphisms.
Indeed, the object
S((X,w),(U,\alpha))\in\mathcal{F}
does not change
as we continuously vary the admissible subset
U\subset X
.
(This is the reason why we need the boundary ordering
\alpha

and cannot take
\partial U
instead of
\{1,\dots,u\}
:
it would not be continuous.)
Similarly, if we continuously vary a morphisms
f:(X,w)\to(Y,u)

and an admissible subset
(U,\alpha)\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(Y,u)
,
the space
V\setminus f^{-1}(U^{\circ})
does not change up to label-preserving
homotopy equivalence and hence the resulting cospan
S(f)
remains the same.
(If we moreover required that
V
is a neighbourhood of
f(U)
,
then
V\setminus f(U)^{\circ}
would even remain the same up to homeomorphism.
We do not do this as it would remove the identity morphisms from
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
.)

It remains to check that
S
is functorial.
Consider two morphisms
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
,
h:(Y,w^{\prime})\to(Z,w^{\prime\prime})

and admissible opens
(U,\alpha)\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
,
(V,\beta)\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(Y,w^{\prime})
,
and
(W,\gamma)\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(Z,w^{\prime\prime})
such that
U\subset f^{-1}(V)
and
V\subset h^{-1}(W)
.
We would like to show that the weighted cospan
S(h\circ f)

agrees with the composite
S(f)\cup_{\{1,\dots,v\}}S(h)
.
On underlying sets one can describe this pushout as:

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(f^{-1}(V)\setminus U^{\circ})\cup_{\{1,\dots,v\}}\pi_{0}(h^{-1}(W)\setminus V^{\circ})

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{0}(f^{-1}(V)\setminus U^{\circ})\cup_{\partial V}\pi_{0}((h\circ f)^{-1}(W)\setminus h^{-1}(V^{\circ}))

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{0}((h\circ f)^{-1}(W)\setminus U^{\circ})

To conclude
S(f)\cup_{\{1,\dots,v\}}S(h)=S(h\circ f)
we need to
check that both sides are weighted in
A
in the same way.
To show this let
C\subset(h\circ f)^{-1}(W)\setminus U^{\circ}
some component.
As a point in
S(h\circ f)
this is labelled by its total genus.
We have a decomposition of
C
into
C_{0}=C\cap(f^{-1}(V)\setminus U^{\circ})

and
C_{1}=C\cap((h\circ f)^{-1}(W)\setminus f^{-1}(V)^{\circ})
.
The total genus of each component of
C_{1}
agrees with that of the relevant
component in
f(C_{1})
, which is a union of components of
h^{-1}(W)\setminus V^{\circ}
.
One can express the total genus of
C
in terms of the genus of
the components of
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
as follows:

g(C)=\sum_{x\in C}w(x)+\alpha\cdot b_{1}(C)=\sum_{x\in C_{0}}w(x)+\sum_{y\in C_{1}}w(y)+\alpha\cdot b_{1}(C)

The first Betti number of
C
can be written as:

b_{1}(C)=\sum_{[x]\in\pi_{0}(C_{0})}b_{1}([x])+\sum_{[y]\in\pi_{0}(C_{1})}b_{1}([y])+(|C_{0}\cap C_{1}|+1-|\pi_{0}(C_{0})\amalg\pi_{0}(C_{1})|).

(This follows from
\chi(C)=\chi(C_{0})+\chi(C_{1})-\chi(C_{0}\cap C_{1})
.)
From this it follows that:

g(C)=\sum_{[x]\in\pi_{0}(C_{0})}g([x])+\sum_{[y]\in\pi_{0}(C_{1})}g([y])+(|\partial V\cap C|+1-|\pi_{0}(C_{0})\amalg\pi_{0}(C_{1})|.

This is the same formula as in definition
2.13

where we defined the weighting for composite cospans.
Hence
S(g\circ f)
indeed agrees with
S(f)\cup_{\{1,\dots,v\}}S(g)

as a weighted cospan.
∎

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{F}
be the full subcategory on all those

(M,[\emptyset\to W\leftarrow M],[M\to W^{\prime}\leftarrow\emptyset])

where
M=\{1,\dots,u\}
for some
u\in\mathbb{N}
.
For all
n
the map

B\int_{\mathcal{J}}N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\ S_{n}\ }N_{n}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subset N_{n}\mathcal{F}

is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{n}:=\int_{\mathcal{J}}N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
be the topological category defined
as the Grothendieck construction. An object in this category is
a topological graph
(X,w)
together with a filtration

(U_{0}\subset\dots\subset U_{n}\subset X)
by admissible subsets
and bijections
\alpha_{i}:\partial U_{i}\cong\{1,\dots,u_{i}\}
.
A morphism
(X,w,U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})\to(Y,w^{\prime},V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet})
is a morphism

f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{J}
with
f^{-1}(V_{i})=U_{i}
for all
i

and such that
\alpha_{i}\circ f_{|\partial U_{i}}=\beta_{i}
.

Let us call a filtration
(U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})
on
(X,w)

maximal

if every edge of
(X,w)

intersects
\coprod_{i=0}^{n}\partial U_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathcal{C}_{n}
be the full subcategory on these objects.
Note the objects of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
are a union of connected components
in the space of objects of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
.

Claim 1:
The inclusion
B\mathcal{M}_{n}\to B\mathcal{C}_{n}
is a homotopy equivalence.

Consider the topological functor
Q:\mathcal{C}_{n}\to\mathcal{C}_{n}
that sends

(X,w,U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})
to the quotient
(X/_{\!\sim},w^{\prime},(U_{\bullet})_{/\sim},\alpha_{\bullet})

where we collapse all edges that do not intersect
\coprod_{i=0}^{n}\partial U_{i}
.
Since each of the collapsed edges was either contained entirely in
U_{i}

or its complement, the quotient
(U_{i})_{/\sim}\subset X_{/\!\sim}
is still
an admissible subset.
By construction the functor
Q
lands in the subcategory
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathcal{C}_{n}
.
Since we constructed
Q
by collapsing edges there is a natural transformation

\eta:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\Rightarrow Q
defined by the projections
(X,w)\to(X_{/\sim},w^{\prime})
.
This natural transformation continuous, in fact the morphism does not change
as we continuously vary the filtration.
On classifying spaces
Q
is a map
BQ:B\mathcal{C}_{n}\to B\mathcal{M}_{n}
and

\eta
is a homotopy between
\mathrm{id}_{B\mathcal{C}_{n}}
and
BQ
postcomposed with the inclusion
BI
.
Moreover,
\eta
restricts to an isomorphism between
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}
and
Q_{|\mathcal{M}_{n}}
,
which yields a homotopy between
BI\circ BQ
and
\mathrm{id}_{B\mathcal{M}_{n}}
.
This shows that
B\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
B\mathcal{M}_{n}
are homotopy equivalent.

Claim 2:

\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a topological groupoid.

By lemma
5.6
any morphism
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{J}

can be factored as
(X,w)\to(X/\!\sim_{f},w_{f})\to(Y,w^{\prime})
where
the first map collapses some edges and the second map is an isomorphism.
If
f
collapses some edge
e:[0,1]\to X
and
(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet})

some filtration on
(Y,w^{\prime})
, then
e([0,1])
never intersects
\partial f^{-1}(V_{i})
,
and hence
f^{-1}(V_{\bullet})
is not maximal. Therefore any morphism
between objects in
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is necessarily invertible.

Claim 3:
The components of
B\mathcal{M}_{n}
are contractible.

We begin by showing that
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a fibrant topological category
in the sense that the source and target map
(s,t):\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\to\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{M}_{n})^{2}

is a Serre fibration. The space of morphisms is by definition the space
of morphisms
f:(X,w)\to(Y,w^{\prime})
together with a filtration
(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet})
on
Y
.
The target map projects this to
(Y,w^{\prime},V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet})
and the source map
to
(X,w,f^{-1}(U_{\bullet}),(f^{-1}\circ\beta_{\bullet}))
.
We can fix
(X,w)
and
(Y,w^{\prime})
as
\mathcal{J}
has a discrete space of objects.
So we need to show that sending
(f,(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet}))
to

((f^{-1}(V_{\bullet}),f^{-1}\circ\beta_{\bullet}),(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet}))
is a Serre fibration.
Moreover, since
V_{i}
is, up to passing to complements, uniquely determined
by
\beta_{i}:\{1,\dots,v_{i}\}\hookrightarrow Y\setminus(Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing})
,
it will suffice to show that
(f,\beta_{\bullet})\mapsto(f^{-1}\circ\beta_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet})

is a Serre fibration.
This follows from the fact that inverting and evaluating a homeomorphism
f
on a finite
subset
A\subset Y\setminus(Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing})
induces a Serre fibration

\mathit{Homeo}(Y\,\mathrm{rel}\,(Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}))\to\mathit{Emb}(A,Y\setminus(Y_{w}\cup Y_{\rm sing}))
.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is fibrant the base-change theorem

[
ERW19
, Theorem 5.1]

(or equivalently theorem
3.16
)
implies that the inclusion of a skeleton
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\rm sk}\subset\mathcal{M}_{n}

induces an equivalence on classifying spaces.
Therefore, the components of
B\mathcal{M}_{n}
are equivalent to

B\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}(X,w,U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet})
.
Such an automorphism is a homeomorphism
f:X\cong X
that respects the weight,
the
U_{i}
and satisfies
f\circ\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}
.
The latter means that it has to fix all elements in the image of the
\alpha_{i}
.
Since the filtration is maximal each edge contains such an element and
hence
f
has to preserve each edge as a set. In fact, it also has
to preserve the orientation of the edge as either it contains two
points of some
\partial U_{i}
or one end lies in
U_{i}
and the other does not.
This implies that
f
has to fix all points in
X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing}
.
In summary, this group is exactly the group of homeomorphism that fix
all vertices and all elements of
\partial U_{i}
.
Therefore, the group is equivalent to a product
\prod_{j}\mathit{Homeo}^{\partial}([0,1])
,
which is contractible.

Claim 4:

S_{n}
induces a bijection between the isomorphism classes
of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and the
n
-simplices of
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

Given an
n
-simplex in
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
we will construct a preimage under
S_{n}
.
Such an
n
-simplex can be thought of as an
(n+2)
-simplex in

N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
. Using the notation from section
4

we write this as
W:[n+2]\to\mathrm{Csp}(A,A_{1},\alpha)
where:

(\emptyset=W(0)\to W_{\pi}(0\leq 1)\leftarrow W(1)\to\dots\leftarrow W(n+1)\to W_{\pi}(n+1\leq n+2)\leftarrow W(n+2)=\emptyset).

Moreover, we have that
W(i)=\{1,\dots,u_{i-1}\}
for all
i=1,\dots,n+1
.
Each of the
W(i\leq i+1):W(i)\to W(i+1)
is a cospan of finite sets

W(i)\to W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)\leftarrow W(i+1)
together with a labeling

o_{i}:W_{\pi}(i\leq i+1)\to A
.

As discussed in definition
4.7
we can construct a representing space

|W|
with a map
\operatorname{pr}:|W|\to[0,n+3]
and a labeling

o:|W|_{0}=\operatorname{pr}^{-1}(\{1,\dots,n+1\})\to A
coming from the
o_{i}
.
Since for the simplex at hand
W(0)=\emptyset=W(n+2)
are both empty
the space
|W|
will come with two discrete points
\bot,\top\in|W|
.
Let us remove these and denote the result by
|W|^{\prime}:=|W|\setminus\{\bot,\top\}
.
By construction
|W|^{\prime}
is a
1
-dimensional CW complex and since

W(0\leq n+2)
is a connected morphism
|W|^{\prime}
is a connected space.
Hence
(|W|^{\prime},o)
is a well-defined object of
\mathcal{J}
.
Moreover, we have a filtration on
|W|^{\prime}
defined by
U_{i}:=\operatorname{pr}^{-1}((0,i+1))

and a bijection
\alpha_{i}:\{1,\dots,u_{i}\}\cong W(i+1)\cong\partial U_{i}
.
The tuple
((|W|^{\prime},o),(U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet}))
hence defines an object in
\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

One can now verify that for
W=S_{n}((X,w),(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet}))

the resulting
((|W|^{\prime},o),(U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet}))
is isomorphic to

((X,w),(V_{\bullet},\beta_{\bullet}))
, and conversely,
S_{n}((|W|^{\prime},o),(U_{\bullet},\alpha_{\bullet}))

always recovers
W
. This shows that
S_{n}
indeed induces a bijection
between the isomorphism classes of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and the elements of
N_{n}\mathcal{F}
.

Conclusion:

By claim 2
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a groupoid and since isotopic objects are isomorphic
in this topological groupoid,
\pi_{0}B\mathcal{M}_{n}
is the set
of isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. By claim 4 the functor

S_{n}
hence induces a bijection
\pi_{0}B\mathcal{M}_{n}\cong N_{n}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
By claim 1 and 3 the maps
\pi_{0}B\mathcal{M}_{n}\leftarrow B\mathcal{M}_{n}\to B\mathcal{C}_{n}

are homotopy equivalences, and hence
BS_{n}:B\mathcal{C}_{n}\to N_{n}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

is indeed an equivalence.
∎

Assuming the contractibility of
B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)
, which we prove in the next section,
we can now show that both functors
\mathcal{J}\leftarrow\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}

are equivalence on classifying spaces.

Proof of theorem
5.2
.

Thomason showed that the classifying space of a Grothedieck construction

\int_{\mathcal{I}}F
is equivalent to the homotopy colimit of the classifying space
BF(i)
,
see
[
Tho79
]
.
While Thomason only considered discrete categories, the analogous statement
is true for topologically enriched categories,
see
[
Rap14
, Proposition 2.1.1]
.
By corollary
5.23
the classifying space
B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(G,w)

is contractible for all
(G,w)\in\mathcal{J}
. Therefore if we replace

\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(G,w)
by the trivial category
*
, then this induces an equivalence
on the homotopy colimits:

B\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\simeq\hocolim_{(G,w)\in\mathcal{J}^{\rm op}}B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(G,w)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\hocolim_{(G,w)\in\mathcal{J}^{\rm op}}*\simeq B\int_{\mathcal{J}}*\cong B\mathcal{J}.

This shows that the left-wards functor
\mathcal{J}\leftarrow\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}

is an equivalence on classifying spaces.

For the other functor we note that, because geometric realisation commutes
with homotopy colimits, we may also compute the classifying space of
the Grothendieck construction as:

\left|B\int_{\mathcal{J}}N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\right|\simeq\left|\hocolim_{(X,w)\in\mathcal{J}^{\rm op}}N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\right|\simeq\hocolim_{(X,w)\in\mathcal{J}^{\rm op}}B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\simeq B\left(\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\right)

We have shown in proposition
5.19
that for all
n
the map

S_{n}:B\left(\int_{\mathcal{J}}N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\right)\longrightarrow N_{n}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

is an equivalence. Hence, after passing to geometric realisations,
we see that
BS:B\int_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\to B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is an equivalence.
Moreover
B\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\simeq B\mathcal{F}
since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a full subcategory
that contains at least one object per isomorphism class.
∎

5.3
Height functions for graphs

In this section show that, in some sense, the choice of a filtration on
a graph is a contractible choice. Concretely, we will show
in proposition
5.22
that
B(\mathrm{Sub}(X,w))
is contractible
for any topological graph
(X,w)
.
To prove this we first introduce height functions
h:X\to\mathbb{R}

and then we obtain filtrations as preimages of
(\infty,t]
for regular values
t
.

For the purpose of this section we will assume that all topological graphs

(X,w)
come with a metric
d
.
In particular this induces the structure of a Riemannian
1
-manifold
on
X\setminus X_{\rm sing}
.

Definition 5.20
.

A
height function
for a graph
(X,w,d)
is continuous map
h:X\to\mathbb{R}

such that
h_{|X\setminus X_{\rm sing}}
is smooth.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)
denote the vector space of height functions on a graph
G
.
We topologise this as a subvector space of

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X\setminus X_{\rm sing},\mathbb{R})

equipped with the Whitney
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-topology.

Given a graph
(X,w,d)
with height-function
h:X\to\mathbb{R}
we say that

t\in\mathbb{R}
is a
regular value
for
h

if for any vertex
v\in(X_{w}\cup X_{\rm sing})
we have that
h(v)\neq t

and if
t
is a regular value of
h_{|X\setminus X_{\rm sing}}
.

In order to use the height-function to cut
G
non-trivially we need
to require the height-function to not be constant.
Homotopically, this is not a problem because of the following fact:

Fact 5.21

(
[
BP75
, Theorem VI.5.2, Corollary V.6.2, and Theorem V.6.3]
)
.

If
V
is an infinite-dimensional Frechét space and
U\subset V

is a finite-dimensional subspace, then the complement
V\setminus U

is contractible.

Figure 11:
A filtration induced by a height function
h:G\to\mathbb{R}

and regular values
t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.

Proposition 5.22
.

For any
(X,w)\in\mathcal{J}
the classifying space of the topological poset

\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
is contractible.

Proof.

Fix
(X,w)
and pick some metric
d
.
Consider the topological poset
\mathcal{P}

where an object is a tuple
(h,t)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)\times\mathbb{R}

such that
t
is a regular value for
h
and
t\in h(X)
.
We define the relation of
\mathcal{P}
to be

(h,t)\leq(h^{\prime},t^{\prime})
whenever
h=h^{\prime}
and
t\leq t^{\prime}
.
The nerve
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}
admits an augmentation where we let

N_{-1}\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)

be the subspace of functions
h
such that there exists a regular value

t\in\mathbb{R}
with
t\in h(X)
.

The augmented (semi-)simplicial space
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}
is a topological flag complex
in the sense of
[
GRW14
, Definition 6.1]
. Moreover,

\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)\times\mathbb{R}
is open,
and therefore
[
GRW14
, Theorem 6.2]
tells us that the augmentation map

B\mathcal{P}\to N_{-1}\mathcal{P}
is a weak equivalence.
(See lemma
[
Ste21
, Lemma 7.9]
for a formulation of the special case we are using.)

By definition
N_{-1}\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)
is the space of those
height functions
h
that admit a regular value
t
that lies in the image of
h
.
By Sard’s theorem the set of regular values of a given
h:X\to\mathbb{R}

is open and dense in
\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore we can always find such a regular value, unless the function
h
is constant.
This means that
N_{-1}\mathcal{P}
is the complement
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)\setminus\mathbb{R}

of the one-dimensional subspace of constant functions.
By fact
5.21
this space is contractible and so we conclude
that
B\mathcal{P}\simeq N_{-1}\mathcal{P}\simeq*
is contractible.

To complete the proof we will construct a level-wise equivalence
between
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}
and
N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
.
Consider the map
P:\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
defined by sending
(h,t)
to
the preimage
U:=h^{-1}(-\infty,t]
as illustrated in figure
11
.
This subset is always admissible because
t
is regular.
It follows from inspection that this map is continuous and it preserves
the relation as
h^{-1}(-\infty,t]\subset h^{-1}(-\infty,t^{\prime}]
when
t\leq t^{\prime}
.
We therefore get a well-defined map of simplicial spaces

P_{\bullet}:N_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}\to N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
.

Fix some
n
. We would like to show that
P_{n}
is an equivalence.
Consider the subspace
N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)^{<}\subset N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
that only
contains those filtrations
U_{0}\subset\dots\subset U_{n}

where
\partial U_{i}
is disjoint from
U_{i-1}
for all
i
.
Similarly let
N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}\subset N_{n}\mathcal{P}
be the subspace
of those tuples
(h,t_{\bullet})
where
t_{i-1}<t_{i}
for all
i
.
In both cases the inclusion of this subspace is a homotopy equivalence.
We will construct a map
q:N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)^{<}\to N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}

that is homotopy inverse to the restriction of
P_{n}
to
N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}
.

Fix some smooth function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\sigma(x)=0
for
x\leq 0
,

\sigma(x)=1
for
x\geq 1
, and
\sigma^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x\in(0,1)
.
Given some length
(n+1)
-filtration

U_{0}\subset\dots\subset U_{n}\subset X

we define a height function
h_{U_{\bullet}}:X\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:

h_{U_{\bullet}}(x)=\begin{cases}-\sigma(d(x,\partial U_{0}))&\text{ if }x\in U_{0}\\
i+\sigma(\frac{d(x,\partial U_{i})}{d(x,\partial U_{i+1})+d(x,\partial U_{i})})&\text{ if }x\in U_{i+1}\setminus U_{i}^{\circ}\\
n+\sigma(d(x,\partial U_{n}))&\text{ if }x\in|G|\setminus U_{n}^{\circ}\end{cases}

One checks that
h
is indeed a smooth function with image in
[-1,n+1]

and that
h
depends smoothly on the filtration
U_{\bullet}\in N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)^{<}
.
Any
i=0,\dots,n
is a regular value of
h
and we have that

h_{U_{\bullet}}^{-1}(-\infty,i]=U_{i}
by construction.
We define
q:N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)^{<}\to N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}
by sending
U_{\bullet}

to
(h_{U_{\bullet}},t_{\bullet})
with
t_{i}:=i
.
By construction
P_{n}\circ q
is the identity on
N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)^{<}
,
so
q
is a section of
(P_{n})_{|N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}}
.
To construct a homotopy between
q\circ(P_{n})_{|N_{n}\mathcal{P}^{<}}
and the identity
note that given
(h,t_{\bullet})
and
(h^{\prime},t_{\bullet}^{\prime})\in N_{n}\mathcal{P}
that induce
the same filtration the function
(1-\lambda)h+\lambda h^{\prime}
is a height function
with regular values
(1-\lambda)t_{\bullet}+\lambda t^{\prime}_{\bullet}
a for any
\lambda\in[0,1]
.
We can therefore simply write down an affine linear homotopy between
q\circ P_{n}

and
\mathrm{id}_{N_{n}\mathcal{P}}
.

This completes the proof that
P:N_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}\to N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)

is a level-wise weak equivalence.
After taking geometric realisations have weak equivalences

B\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)\simeq B\mathcal{P}\simeq N_{-1}\mathcal{P}\simeq*.

∎

Corollary 5.23
.

For any topological graph
(X,w)
the classifying space
B\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)

is contractible.

Proof.

This will from the above together with the base-change theorem
3.16

if we can show that
N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\to N_{\bullet}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
is a base-change.
This map is clearly surjective on
0
-simplices so we need to check that
the following diagram is a homotopy pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w))^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w))^{n+1}.}

The map
\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\to\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
is a covering and the fiber at some

U\in\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
is the discrete set of bijections
\{1,\dots,u\}\cong\partial U
.
(This covering is actually trivial unless
(X,w)=(S^{1},0)
.)
Hence
(\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w))^{n+1}\to(\mathrm{Sub}(X,w))^{n+1}
is a covering
and the fiber is the set of
(n+1)
-tuples of bijections

\alpha_{i}:\{1,\dots,u_{i}\}\cong\partial U_{i}
.
Similar to the above
N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(X,w)\to N_{n}\mathrm{Sub}(X,w)
is a covering
with fiber the set of
(n+1)
-tuples of bijection
\alpha_{i}
.
Since both vertical maps are coverings and their fibers agree it follows
that the square is indeed a homotopy pullback square.
∎

6
Comparison to
\Delta_{g}

In the previous sections we computed the classifying space
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})

up to weak equivalence of infinite loop spaces and showed that it
splits off free infinite loop spaces on the double-suspension of
BJ_{g}
.
Below we recall the moduli space
\Delta_{g}
from tropical geometry and show that
there is a rational homology equivalence
BJ_{g}\to\Delta_{g}
.
In fact, one can argue that
BJ_{g}
is the homotopy type of a moduli
stack

who’s coarse space is
\Delta_{g}
, though we defer this to future work.

One shortcoming of our results so far is that
the proof does not yield an explicit splitting map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\to Q(\Sigma^{2}BJ_{g})
.
This makes it difficult to understand how the much-studied rational cohomology
of
\Delta_{g}
embeds into the cohomology of
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
.
The purpose of this section is to show that this can be fixed by
“ignoring finite automorphism groups”.
Concretely, we will work up to rational homotopy equivalence,
which leads to several simplifications:
we can work with the
1
-category
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
instead of the
(2,1)
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
,
we can map to the coarse tropical moduli space
\Delta_{g}
instead of
BJ_{g}
,
and we can work with the free commutative topological monoid

\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(X)=(\coprod_{n\geq 0}X^{n}/\Sigma_{n})/\sim
on a based space
X

instead of the free infinite loop space
Q(X)
.

Note that the space
B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
has a partially defined commutative multiplication
defined by

\displaystyle[(M_{0}\xrightarrow{[W_{1}]}\dots\xrightarrow{[W_{n}]}M_{n}],(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

\displaystyle+[(N_{0}\xrightarrow{[V_{1}]}\dots\xrightarrow{[V_{n}]}N_{n}],(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

\displaystyle=[(M_{0}\cup N_{0}\xrightarrow{[W_{1}\amalg V_{1}]}\dots\xrightarrow{[W_{n}\amalg V_{n}]}M_{n}\cup N_{n}],(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

whenever
\forall i:(t_{i}>0\Rightarrow M_{i}\cap N_{i}=\emptyset)
,
and undefined otherwise.
(We can always arrange for the
(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})
component to be identical
by inserting identity morphisms.)
We will construct a continuous map of partially defined
commutative topological monoids as described in figure
1
in the introduction:

\mu:B\left(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\right)\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}\left(S^{2}(\Delta_{2})\vee S^{2}(\Delta_{3})\vee\dots)\right).

Here
S^{2}(-)
denotes the unreduced double-suspension.
The goal of this section is to show that
\mu
corresponds,
up to rational equivalence, to the projection onto the third factor
in theorem
C
; in the following sense:

Theorem 6.1
.

The map
\mu
fits into a commutative diagram
of partially defined commutative topological monoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\left(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathbb{Z}\times|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g=1)}|\times|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\left(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}\left(S^{2}(\Delta_{2})\vee S^{2}(\Delta_{3})\vee\dots)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
Theorem
C
project
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where the maps labelled by
\simeq
and
\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}

are (rational) homotopy equivalences.

Remark 6.2
.

To compare this to the formulation of theorem
C

given in the introduction,
note that by combining corollary
3.44
with theorem
5.1
we get:

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g=1)}|\simeq Q(\Sigma^{2}BSO_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|\simeq Q\big({\bigvee}_{g\geq 2}\Sigma^{2}BJ_{g}\big).

To obtain the specific map
B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\to B\mathbb{Z}\times|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|

in theorem
6.1
we use the canonical quotient map on the second and third factor,
and on the first factor we use the functor
F:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\to\mathbb{Z}

that sends a cobordism
W:M\to N
to the integer

\tfrac{1}{2}(|\pi_{0}(N)|-|\pi_{0}(M)|+\chi(W))
.
This is the homotopy splitting of
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0},{\partial_{+}}}\subset\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}
constructed in
proposition
4.27
.
Note that
B\mathbb{Z}
is a topological abelian group and
BF:B(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\to B\mathbb{Z}

sends disjoint unions to sums.

The Dold-Thom theorem
[
DT58
]
states that there is a natural isomorphism

\pi_{*}\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(X)\cong\tilde{H}_{*}(X)
between the homotopy groups
of the infinite symmetric power of
X
and the by the reduced homology of
X
.
Hence
\mu
induces a map from
\pi_{n}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})
to

\bigoplus_{g\geq 2}H_{n-2}(\Delta_{g};\mathbb{Z})
.

Corollary 6.3
.

On rational homotopy groups
\mu
induces a surjection:

\pi_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\mu):\pi_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}B(\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}})\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{g\geq 2}H_{*-2}(\Delta_{g};\mathbb{Q})

the kernel of which is spanned by a single class
\alpha
in degree one
and a class
\rho_{i}
in each degree
4i+2
.

6.1
Constructing the maps

We now recall the definitions used above, starting with
the moduli space
\Delta_{g}
, which we define
as a colimit indexed by
J_{g}
following
[
CGP16
]

Definition 6.4
.

Consider the functor
\Delta^{E}:J^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}
that sends
(G,w)
to the space

\Delta^{E_{G}}=\{d:E_{G}\to[0,1]\;|\;\sum_{e\in E_{G}}d(e)=1\}.

For
g\geq 2
we define the

moduli space of tropical curves of genus
g
and volume
1

\Delta_{g}
as the colimit
\colim_{(G,w)\in J_{g}^{\rm op}}\Delta^{E}(G,w)
.

A point in
\Delta_{g}
is represented by a stable graph
G\in J
of genus
g

together with a function
d:E_{G}\to[0,1]
, which we think of as recording
the lengths of the edges. Every point in
\Delta_{g}
has a representative where
the edge lengths are strictly positive, and in this case there is a unique metric
on the topological space representing
G
such that any edge
e\subset G

is isometric to
[0,d(e)]\subset\mathbb{R}
.
The underlying set of
\Delta_{g}
can hence be identified with the set of
of stable metric graphs up to weight-preserving isometry.

Remark 6.5
.

In
[
CGP16
]
the authors first define a moduli space
M_{g}^{\rm trop}

as the colimit over the functor
\sigma:J_{g}^{\rm CGP}\to\mathrm{Top}

that sends
G
to
\sigma(G)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E_{G}}=\{l:E_{G}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\}
.
This colimit has a natural map to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by taking the
sum of all edge-lengths: this is the volume of the tropical curve.
The moduli space
\Delta_{g}
is then defined as the subspace of
M_{g}^{\rm trop}

containing the tropical curves of volume
1
.
This is canonically homeomorphic to our definition via:

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGP16}{}{}]}}}}{{=}}\{1\}\times_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\left(\colim_{G\in J_{g}^{\rm CGP}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E_{G}}\right)\cong\colim_{G\in J_{g}^{\rm CGP}}\left(\{1\}\times_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E_{G}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{G\in J_{g}^{\rm CGP}}\Delta^{E_{G}}\cong\colim_{G\in J_{g}}\Delta^{E_{G}}.

Here the last homeomorphism uses that
J_{g}\simeq J_{g}^{\rm CGP}\setminus\{\bullet_{g}\}

(see remark
5.11
)
and
\Delta^{E}(\bullet_{g})=\emptyset
.

Definition 6.6
.

The map
\Phi:B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}\to\Delta_{g}
is defined as follows;
compare figure
12
.
A point in
B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}
is represented by a tuple

[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{[W_{0}]}M_{0}\xrightarrow{[W_{1}]}\dots M_{n}\xrightarrow{[W_{n+1}]}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots t_{n})]

whose first entry is an element of
N_{n}\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}\subset N_{n+2}\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}

and whose second entry is a point in the topological
n
-simplex
\Delta^{n}
.
Define a graph
G
with vertices
\coprod_{i=0}^{n+1}\pi_{0}(W_{i})

and edges
\coprod_{i=0}^{n}\pi_{0}(M_{i})
.
Each edge
a\in\pi_{0}(M_{i})
is connected to the two vertices corresponding
to the images of
a
under
\pi_{0}(W_{i})\leftarrow\pi_{0}(M_{i})\to\pi_{0}(W_{i+1})
.
This graph is weighted by
w:V_{G}\to\mathbb{N}
, which sends each

[U]\in\pi_{0}(W_{i})
to the genus of the subsurface
U\subset W_{i}
.
Moreover, we define an edge length function
d:E(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

by
d(a)=t_{i}\cdot|\pi_{0}(M_{i})|^{-1}
for
a\in\pi_{0}(M_{i})
.
Now we set
\Phi((M_{\bullet},[W_{\bullet}]),t_{\bullet}):=[G,d]
in
\Delta_{g}
.

Figure 12:
An example of how the map
\Phi:B(\mathcal{F}^{\chi\leq 0}_{5})\to\Delta_{5}

from definition
6.6
can be evaluated on a
2
-simplex.
The
2
-simplex is parametrised by
(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2})\in[0,1]^{3}
with
t_{0}+t_{1}+t_{2}=1
.

\Phi
sends this to the weighted metric graph with one vertex per component
in each morphism
W_{i}
, weighted by the genus of this component,
and one edge of length
\tfrac{1}{|\pi_{0}M_{i}|}t_{i}
per circle in each object
M_{i}
.
Afterwards, all valence
2
and genus
0
vertices are deleted
and the length of their adjacent edges is added.

Definition 6.7
.

For a space
X
let
\Sigma_{+}^{2}(X)
denote the double-suspension of
X_{+}
:

\Sigma_{+}^{2}(X)=\{(x,a,b)\in X\times[0,1]^{2}\;|\;a+b\leq 1\}/\{(x,a,b)\;|\;a=0\text{ or }b=0\text{ or }a+b=1\}.

Definition 6.8
.

For a based space
(Y,y_{0})
let
\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(Y,y_{0})
denote
the infinite symmetric power of
(Y,y_{0})
.
The underlying set of
\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(Y,y_{0})
is the free abelian monoid on the set
Y

modulo the relation
[y_{0}]=0
.
This is topologised with the quotient topology with respect to the map

\coprod_{n\geq 0}Y^{n}\to\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(Y,y_{0}),\quad(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}[y_{i}].

Definition 6.9
.

We construct a continuous map:

\displaystyle\mu:B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}\left(\Sigma_{+}^{2}(\Delta_{2}\amalg\Delta_{3}\amalg\dots)\right)

\displaystyle[(M_{0}\xrightarrow{[W_{1}]}\dots\xrightarrow{[W_{n}]}M_{n}),(t_{0},\dots t_{n})]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{U\subset W_{1}\cup\dots\cup W_{n}\atop\text{ closed of genus }\geq 2}[[G_{U},w_{U},d_{U}],t_{0}+\dots+t_{a_{U}-1},t_{b_{U}+1}+\dots+t_{n}]

The sum runs over all connected components
U\subset W_{1}\cup\dots\cup W_{n}

that are closed surfaces of genus at least
2
.
Here
1\leq a_{U}\leq b_{U}\leq n
are the smallest and largest number, respectively,
such that
U\cap M_{a_{U}}\neq\emptyset\neq U\cap M_{b_{U}}

and
[G_{U},w_{U},d_{U}]\in\Delta_{g(U)}
is defined using the map
\Phi

from definition
6.6
:

[G_{U},w_{U},d_{U}]:=\Phi\left(\big(\emptyset\xrightarrow{[W_{a_{U}}\cap U]}M_{a_{U}}\cap U\xrightarrow{[W_{a_{U}+1}\cap U]}\dots\to M_{b_{U}}\cap U\xrightarrow{[W_{b_{U}+1}\cap U]}\emptyset\big),(t_{a_{U}},\dots,t_{b_{U}})\right)\in\Delta_{g(U)}

6.2
The map
B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}\to\Delta_{g}
is an isomorphism on rational homology

In this subsection we will define maps
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
and
\Phi_{3}

making the following diagram commute up to homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BJ_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathcal{F}_{g}\left(\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{1}}

(1)

The maps labelled by
\simeq
are equivalences.
For the bottom left map this follows from the equivalence
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\simeq\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>1},1)

constructed in lemma
2.16
.
For the two maps in the middle this was shown in our proof of theorem
5.2
,
which stated that
B\mathcal{F}_{g}\left(\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)\right)\simeq BJ_{g}
via this zig-zag.
For the horizontal map on the right it follows from the observation that each

\Delta^{E_{G}}
is contractible and that hence
\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\to J_{g}

is a level-wise equivalence on nerves.
Once the diagram is established we show that
\Phi_{3}
is an isomorphism on rational homology
by applying a version of the Vietoris-Begle theorem
and then it follows that the same is true for
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
.

Definition 6.10
.

The maps
\Phi_{i}
in diagram
1
are defined as follows.

•

The map
\Phi_{3}:B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\right)\to\Delta_{g}
is defined by

[(G_{0}\to G_{1}\to\dots\to G_{n}),d\in\Delta^{E_{G_{n}}},(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto[G_{n},d].

This is the canonical comparison map from the homotopy colimit
to the strict colimit.

•

The map
\Phi_{2}:BJ_{g}\to\Delta_{g}
is defined by

[(G_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}G_{1}\to\dots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}G_{n}),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto[G_{0},d^{f}]

where the edge length function
d^{f}\in\Delta^{E_{G_{0}}}
is given as

d^{f}(e)=\sum_{i=0}^{m_{e}}\frac{t_{i}}{|E_{G_{i}}|}\quad\text{ where }m_{e}=\max\{i\leq n\;|\;G_{0}\to G_{i}\text{ does not collapse }e\}.

•

The map
\Phi_{1}:B\mathcal{F}_{g}\left(\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)\right)\to\Delta_{g}
is defined
in analogy with definition
6.6
as

[([X_{0}]\leftarrow A_{0}\to[X_{1}]\leftarrow\dots\leftarrow A_{n}\to X_{n+1}),(t_{0},\dots t_{n})]\longmapsto[G,d]

Where the graph
G
has vertices
\coprod_{i=0}^{n+1}X_{i}

and edges
\coprod_{i=0}^{n}A_{i}
.
Each edge
a\in A_{i}
is connected to the two vertices corresponding
to the images of
a
under
X_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}\to X_{i+1}
.
The edge length function
d:E(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is

d(a)=t_{i}\cdot|A_{i}|^{-1}
for
a\in A_{i}
.
(To be precise, we should also remove all bivalent vertices and
add the lengths of their incident edges.)

Remark 6.11
.

To see that the above maps are indeed continuous one check
that they are continuous on each simplex and well-defined on faces.
We will not spell out the details here.

Lemma 6.12
.

The maps
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
, and
\Phi_{3}
make the diagram
1
commute
up to homotopy.

Proof.

First, consider the bigon involving
\Phi
and
\Phi_{1}
.
This commutes on-the-nose, basically because
\Phi
was defined by combining
\Phi_{1}

with the functor
\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\to\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)
from lemma
2.16
.

Next, consider the triangle on the right. The two maps

B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\right)\to\Delta_{g}
are given by

[(G_{0}\to G_{1}\to\dots\to G_{n}),d\in\Delta^{E_{G_{n}}},(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto[G_{n},d]\text{ or }[G_{0},d^{f}]\text{, respectively.}

We can rewrite
[G_{n},d]=[G_{0},f^{*}d]
where
f^{*}d\in\Delta^{E_{G_{0}}}

assigns
0
to each edge that is collapsed by
f:G_{0}\to G_{n}

and
d(f(e))
to any other edge.
The homotopy can then be defined as

H_{\lambda}:[(G_{0}\to G_{1}\to\dots\to G_{n}),d\in\Delta^{E_{G_{n}}},(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto[G_{0},\lambda f^{*}d+(1-\lambda)d^{f}],

which yields a family of maps

H_{\lambda}:B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\right)\to\Delta_{g}

depending continuously on
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

Finally, we consider the square on the left.
Inspecting the definitions, one sees the the composite

B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\Delta^{E}\right)\to B\mathcal{F}_{g}\left(\mathrm{Csp}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{>0},1)\right)\to\Delta_{g}

is given by

[(G_{0}\to G_{1}\to\dots\to G_{n}),(U_{i}\in\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}(G_{i})),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]\longmapsto[G_{0},d^{U}]

where the edge length function
d^{U}
is defined as

d^{U}(e)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}t_{i}\frac{|e\cap(f_{i}\circ\dots\circ f_{1})^{-1}(\partial U_{i})|}{|\partial U_{i}|}.

The other composite in the square simply sends
[(G_{\bullet}),(U_{\bullet}),(t_{\bullet})]

to
[G_{0},d^{f}]
with
d^{f}
as in the definition of
\Phi_{2}
.
Now that we have written the value of both maps as
G_{0}
with a certain metric,
we can again use affine linear interpolation of metrics to construct the desired homotopy:

[G_{0},\lambda d^{U}+(1-\lambda)d^{f}]
.
∎

We will show that
\Phi_{3}
is an isomorphism on rational homology,
by checking that its fibers are
K(\pi,1)
s for
\pi

the automorphism group of a finite metric graph, which is finite.
Then we use the following variant of the Vietoris-Begle theorem:

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
(r:X_{\bullet}\to X_{-1})
be an augmented simplicial space such that each

X_{n}
is a finite CW complex. Assume further that for each
x\in X_{-1}

the fiber
Y_{\bullet}:=r^{-1}(x)
has trivial rational homology
H_{*}(|Y_{\bullet}|;\mathbb{Q})=0
.
Then the augmentation
|X_{\bullet}|\to X_{-1}
is an isomorphism in rational homology.

Proof.

For some
N\gg 0
consider the
N
-skeleton
X_{\bullet}^{(N)}\subset X_{\bullet}
.
Then
|X^{(N)}|
is a compact Hausdorff space and
|X^{(N)}|\to|X|
is
N
-connected.
The fiber of the augmentation
|X^{(N)}|\to X_{-1}
at some
x
is the
N
-skeleton

|Y^{(N)}|
, which has trivial rational homology
H_{*}(|Y^{(N)}|;\mathbb{Q})

for
*<N
. We may therefore apply the Vietoris-Begle theorem to the restricted map

r:|X^{(N)}|\to X_{-1}
and conclude that it is an isomorphism on rational
homology in degrees
*<N
. This implies
r:|X_{\bullet}|\to X_{-1}
is
a rational homology isomorphism in this range.
Since
N
was arbitrary the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

The map
\Phi_{3}:B\left(\int_{J_{g}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}\right)\to\Delta_{g}
is an isomorphism on rational homology.

Proof.

Write
X_{\bullet}
for the augmented simplicial space given by
X_{n}:=N_{n}\int_{J_{g}}\mathrm{Sub}^{\prime}
,

X_{-1}:=\Delta_{g}
and augmentation map
r:(G_{0}\to\dots\to G_{n},d)\mapsto[G_{n},d]
.
We will complete the proof by showing that this satisfies
the conditions of lemma
6.13
.)
For the purpose of this proof let us assume that we have replaced the category
J_{g}

with a skeleton. Then each
N_{n}J_{g}
is a finite set and each
X_{n}
is a finite CW-complex.

It remains to show that every fiber of the augmentation map
\Phi_{3}:\|X_{\bullet}\|\to X_{-1}

has trivial rational homology.
An element in the colimit
\Delta_{g}
is represented by a triple
(G,w,d)

where
(G,w)\in J_{g}
and
d\in\Delta^{E_{G}}
is a metric on
(G,w)
.
By collapsing those edges with
d_{e}=0
we can always find a representative
where every edge has non-zero length.
The fiber
\Phi_{3}^{-1}([G,w,d])
can be written as the classifying space of the full subcategory

\mathcal{C}\subset\int_{J}\Delta^{E}
on those objects
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})

such that
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
(G,w,d)
as a metric weighted graph.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathcal{C}
denote the full subcategory on those
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})

where we also require that all edges
e\in E_{G^{\prime}}
has non-zero length
d_{e}^{\prime}>0
.
The inclusion of this subcategory has a left-adjoint
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}_{0}

given by sending a graph to the quotient where we collapse all the edges of length
0
.
On classifying spaces this yields a homotopy equivalence
B\mathcal{C}_{0}\simeq B\mathcal{C}
.
Now note that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is in fact a connected groupoid,
and so
B\mathcal{C}_{0}
is equivalent to
B\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})
for any object
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})
.
The automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is the isometry group
of the finite metric graph
(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime})
, which is finite.
Since finite groups have trivial rational homology, we can conclude:

H_{*}(r^{-1}([G,w,d]);\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{*}(B\mathcal{C};\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{*}(B\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime},w^{\prime},d^{\prime});\mathbb{Q})\cong 0.

The claim now follows from lemma
6.13
.
∎

6.3
The map into the symmetric power

In this section we prove theorem
6.1
by constructing
the following commutative diagram of partially defined commutative
topological monoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}(\Delta_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}

(2)

To clarify the notation used in the diagram, recall that

\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}
is a simplicial object in partial commutative monoidal groupoids.
This means that each
B\mathrm{Cut}_{n}
is a partially defined commutative topological
monoid and the set of isomorphism classes
\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{n}:=\pi_{0}(B\mathrm{Cut}_{n})

is a commutative monoid.
We therefore have a canonical map
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|\to|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}|
,
which is compatible with the partially defined multiplication.
This describes the three vertical maps in the diagram.

In the left-hand square the top vertical map is the composite

B\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}\hookrightarrow|BD_{\bullet}|\xrightarrow{\text{project}}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|

and the bottom map is defined by passing to the quotient where isomorphic

n
-simplices are identified.
One checks that this is well-defined, and the square commutes automatically.

For the square in the middle the top vertical map is defined by summing over connected
components, as described in the following lemma, where we also show that it
is a rational equivalence. The bottom vertical map is again defined by
passing to the quotient.

Lemma 6.15
.

The map

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)

\displaystyle[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{[W_{0}]}M_{0}\to\dots\to M_{n}\xrightarrow{[W_{n+1}]}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{U\subset W_{0}\cup\dots\cup W_{n+1}}[(\emptyset\xrightarrow{[W_{0}\cap U]}M_{0}\cap U\to\dots\to M_{n}\cap U\xrightarrow{[W_{n+1}\cap U]}\emptyset),(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})]

is a rational homotopy equivalence.
Here the sum runs over all connected components
U

of the closed surface
W_{0}\cup\dots\cup W_{n+1}
.

Proof.

The map described is compatible with the partially defined commutative monoid
structure and hence induces a map of
\Gamma
-spaces.
It suffices to check that the map is an isomorphism on rational homology
(because both sides are equivalent to infinite loop spaces,
or alternatively because both sides are simply connected).
By corollary
3.36
the rational homology of

|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|
is freely generated by the rational
homology of
|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|
.
The same is true for
\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)
,
so all we need to check is that the following composite

{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|}\longrightarrow{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)

is the canonical inclusion.
This follows from inspection: the sum
\sum_{U\subset W_{0}\cup\dots}
only has a single summand
as
W_{0}\cup\dots\cup W_{n+1}
is connected.
∎

To complete diagram
2
we only have to construct
the commutative triangle on the right in such a way
that the diagonal map is a rational equivalence.
This will be obtained by applying
\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}
to the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},g\geq 2}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}|B\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{F,(g)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}|BN_{\bullet}^{\rm rel}\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}B\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}(\Delta_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}

The horizontal maps in the top row are the equivalences constructed
in lemma
3.39
and
3.43
:
The vertical map
\Phi
is taken from definition
6.6
.
The diagonal dashed map exists because
\Phi:|N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}|\to\Delta_{g}

already maps any two isomorphic
n
-simplices in
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}_{g}^{\chi\leq 0}

to
\Delta_{g}
in the same way.
The horizontal dashed map exists for the same reason.

To conclude the proof of theorem
6.1
we combine the formula
of lemma
6.15
and lemma
3.39
observe that the composite

{B\mathrm{Cob}_{2}^{{\chi\leq 0}}}\longrightarrow{|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{(g\geq 2)}|}\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(|\pi_{0}\mathrm{Cut}_{\bullet}^{{\rm con},(g\geq 2)}|)\longrightarrow\mathrm{SP}^{\infty}(\bigvee_{g\geq 2}S^{2}(\Delta_{g}))

is exactly the
\mu
described in definition
6.9
.
